Interrupted, and he regretten that he had aistureect her, for her expression now was comparatively lame, as raising her face she returned his kiss.

al ye 3, ° she murmured, placidly, "it. is very good. lam so clad. 3lou Mint be pleased, after all your hard siork." Again, as when he had returned from the theatre/ overnight and found her sleeping, Gqd- ward was consciors of a slight sense of disappointment, and again he pulled hitaseli together. He recognised the danger of growing exacting. Surely enough and to spare had been said in praise of his play. His wife was not in the habit of expressing all she felt, he had known that from the beginning; her eyes at all times spoke the thorghts of her heart more plainly than her tongue. Smoothing the soft cheek of the sleeping child with a tender touch, he said, cheerily: , "I wish the boy were old enough to pat his father on the back with all the rest. Who knows, Celia? That; child may be an infant Shakespeare. In that case I had better set to work again at once. I must make hay for us all while the sun shin.% for if he turns out a very big genius, lie wont be self-supirrting incst likely. Good-bye, my treasures."

"Are you going out, Harold "Yes, I must Just run down to the theatre. I expect they will most of them look in between twelve and one, though there is nothing to alter, so far as I can tell."

He kissed her again, and went out into the bright spring morning. Then, jumping into a hansom, and leaning back luxuriously, once more he reviewed his delightful circumstances.

The play being sti-ch-an undoubted success would bring him in a large sum of money, there could be little qnestion of that, though this being his first dramatic venture, he had received in the original instance only a small sum from the manager. In the future, therefore, Godward felt that he would be justified in giving .up the magazine work at which he had laboured incessantly during the first year of his married life, and in devoting himself exclusively to the writing of plays, in which direc- tion he had always believed his literary strength lay. He felt himself entitled to a short holiday, how- ever; he had worked almost night and day for the last six months in the composition and production of the play ; indeed, it seemed to him he had scarcely had leisure even to notice tile growth and development of that wonderful little son. of his. He would take a month, at any rate, to revel in home pleasures—ho had promised his wife a few weeks at the sea—and then he would buckle on his armour again, and the second play should be better than the first, for he was not so conceited that he did not recognise, after all, there were weak poims in the first. The second act, for instance, - was not up to the others. The next play Ahould he more evenly.balanced, and Celia should be there on the first night, in the author's own box, and she could take her little cousin Marion with her. He smiled as he recol- lected Marion's disappointment that she had not been able to go the night before! The stage seemed full of people when Godward entered the dimly-lighted theatre, and a moment after he was the centre Of a group of excited men and women, who chipped. him on the back and shook hint by the hand in the fervour of their congratulations.; the leading lady, an irreproach- able young matron, being indliciently carried away to take the younz author by the coat lapels, and kiss him on the cheek. Then the members of. the orchestra, who were present in case there should be any alterations in the music cues, struck up an improvised version of "See the Conquering Hero Comes." And at that everybody laughed, and the tension of time prevail- ing excitement was to a certain extent relaxed.

It was nearly four before Harold could get away from the Cecil, where he had lunched with Walter Chester, the manager of a rival theatre. Godward's face was radiant with gratification. Everybody had been so good to him. Not a soul had seemed to grudge his easily-earned triumph ; and, after all, it had been easily earned, for he had revelled in the writing of the play. Chester followed him to his hansom.

",You'll give me :the refusal of the next, then. Yon promise ? " he said. "Yes, I promise." „ . . .

"And you think you can turn it out in six months? "

"There or thereabouts. I am going to take a month off at Eastbourne with my wife, 1 tell you, to study my kiddy. 1 scarcely know the little chap. Give a fellow time to breathe." "A little time only," Chester replied, with a smile ; "the public will be waiting impatiently for your next. Don't let them cool,Godward." Softly whistling a graceful melody that occurred more than onc.e in the new piece, Harold let him- self into his house with his latch-key, and, throw- ing off his light overcdat, looked into the drawing- room. Celia sat at her inlaid escritoire, writing busily in a bulky volume.

The smile deepened on the husband's face. Of course, she was occupied with her journal. She always devoted an hour in the afternoon to it. He could very well imagine she had plenty to say that day, and he could also understand that to women to whom speech is difficult, when their hearts are full a journal must be a great comfort.

He advanced a step, and she looked up quickly, and then, as usual, drew a sheet of blotting-paper over the half-covered page. "Harold! I didn't hear you come in!" "So absorbed in your literary occupations, eh?" he responded, bending his head to kiss her, She smiled, but made no answer, and then he walked away and seated himself near to the cosy hearth, on which there burnt a bright fire. "Everything all right at the; theatre, Harold?" "Splendid h" "That's a good thing," Celia replied, dipping her pen again into the ink. But before she could re-commence her writing, ,there came n knock at the door, followed immediately by the entrance of a white gowoed nurse. With a start the young woman rose to her feet.

"What is it, nurse ?" "Oh, nothing particular, ma'am, only I wanted just to shew you those new socks of baby's." "I'll come at once." "-The young mother tossed her journal into the writing-table, and turned the key hastily in the lock. In another moment she had quitted the room, and then Harold walked to the window and looked out into the street.

He wished the nurse hadn't come at that precise moment, he wanted to tell Celia about the offer for the new play, and the leading lady; he knew how her soft eyes would kindle when she heard of that enthusiastic kiss. lie laid his hand ,•n the escritoire, and the lid moved. His wife had not lock, d it, then. _ .

_ , Idly he lined the lid, and then a sudden gleam of half mischievous excitement leaped into his eyes. Ms wife's journal! He must look at it just for once. He must see what she had said about his triumph. He remembered her dreamy gaze of rapture over that glorious 'Press notice. He knew her heart tied been too full for speech, but he had so loliged to add hers to the outspoken chorus of praise that had sounded so sweetly in his ears. His mother! What would not she have taid had she lived to see this tiny Surely there could be notl,4ing really dishonourable for a husband to peer into the recesses of a heart that he could swear was altogether pure and faithful. Nevertheless, ,Godward glanced nervously over his shoulder, as he seated himself and opened the volume. He saw where the new writing commenced, and ,aegan to read there. The home tale was taken up on the evening of April 17th. "lied dinner early on Harold's account. Sat in the bedroom in the evening. Baby slept like a cherub. He takes wonderful notice now, I was quit.a struck with his ,intelligence in his bath this afternoon. I dropped the soap into the water, and he stretched his hand out after it. Nurse says she never saw a child like it at his age. Put him into his cot. and sat building castles in the air about him until ten, then nursed him and went to bed. A happy, peaceful time. Harold's play produced at the King's. I asleep when he cause home."

Godward's bands were as cold as ice, and in that warm soft atmosphere he eh vem d slightly as he turned the leaf, and nabbed his eyes. Was it really dusk? Was there a palpable gloom in the air, or was it only a trick of his fancy? Setting his teeth, he rend on: "Friday, April 18th.--Feel very tir:id this after- noon. Trust I am not going to have anything the matter with ire ; what should I do about baby in that case? He appelis k) extraordinarily impres- sionable. When I smile at him, he answers directly 7 but if I look at all grave, the corners of his dear little month go down at once. A fine day. Nurse able to take hint out in the gardens for three hours. Fv.tys she's- certain he noticed the crocuses. I shall go out with theva to- morrow ,to see-- I can hardly believe it at his age but, then, baby is not ordinary. Harold's

p:ay seems to have been a success last night, He read me no end of newspaper article,: on it. I very soon got hopelessly muddled with them. However, now I suppmte the thing is oil his brain. Ile has been awfully abrorbed in if tatity. While he was reading I made up my mind about baby's future. Last night I fancied I should like him to be a doctor, but now I want him to be a clergyman; it is surely the noblest of all professions. I quite lost in self in a draam about my darling. A good thing Harold didn't know. He claims so much attention, poor fellow, 'his mother spoilt him! Harold thinks baby may grow up to be a writer. I trust not. One author in a family is quite enough. Harold not at home to , lunch, so baby sat on my knee while I ate mine. He looked so astonished at all my proceedings. Nurse very much anmsed. He's really the sweetest companion. His feet are growing so—" There was no more, but Godward did not move; still he sat there staring at the pretty writing, his , face grey with pallor. Could it be true ? Was this his wife? This babbling, unsympathetic creature?

He raised his hand to his brow, whereon drops of sweat stool thickly; and then there came a loud knock at the outer door.

Dropping the lid of the escritoire, he stumbled to the chair by the fireside, and wiped his glistening forehead. In a moment the drawing - room door opened noisily, and there entered a girl a about eighteen, who, running to him, laid her eager hands on his arm.

"Harold!" she cried. "Oh, lam 30 proud and happy How splendid, how splendid it all is! I waited in the library news-room until the evening papers came out. I have read them, every one, morning and evening. 1 have been there nearly all day. I think the people must have thought me half crazed. I had to bite my tongue to prevent telling everyone (lint it was my cousin's husband who had written the new play. Oh, isn't Celia off her head with pride? He could not speak, but he looked no into the young face. it -was not a pretty face. Marion was delicate and thin, and as a rule somewhat, wan ; but to-day her eyes sparkled with enthusiasm, and her cheeks were flushed.

She IV: too excited, however, to notice the emotion in his fare, and the door opening at that moment, she sprang to Cuba's side, and threw her arms round her cousin's neck, kissing her heartily. "Congratulations, congratulations !" she sang out. "Oh, Celia, when will you take me to see ? When ? When?".

The young wife shook her head smilingly. . "You must be patient a little longer, Marion," she answered; "I will take you presently, but I shall not be able to leave baby for any length of. time for eight tweeks yet." "Eight weeks ! Oh, Celia! Then Harold will take me, won't you, Harold ? I can't wait, and I shall not bo here in eight weeks." "Not be litre?"

"No, my- neptnother says I must, go tas a nursery governess now at once ; and, oh, Celia., I'm not so very fond of children; I'm afraid. If I could only get some slicrthand secretarial work." - "Well, dear, I'm sorry," Celia said, softly .‘but we can't all choose our ways of living."

Rising, Harold Godward stood frr a minute looking down into the fire ; and then he spoke, and neither of the women noticed how carefully and slowly he articulated.

"If you want a secrebrry's post, Marion," lie said, "I can offer you one." "Harold !" •

"I should get on quicker if I dictated my plays," he explained ; "and Chesttr wants a new piece from we at once."

"Not at once, surely, Harold ?" .Celia cried, ghat ply. "Yon are going to take us to Eastbourne for a ui,ntt ? "

"I cannot do so now," he responded, calmly; "Chester presses for the play. I will, send you down with baby and noise." "Oh, very well, dear; you must do as you thini best," Celia answered, contentedly. His rage rose at her ready acceptance of liii absence, and he turned ; but his wife reinarke nothing unusual in her husband's saspect, though Marion wondered at the curious hard lines round his mouth.

"Marion, can you be hero at ten o'clock eret, morning?" he asked. "Oh, earlipr than that, Harold!"

"From ten to four I should like you to stay," he continued. "You can lunch with us, and I will pa) you thirty shillings a week to begin with. Will that do ? ./

With a sob the girl went to him, and claspee his cold, clammy fingers in both her bands. "I can't thank you," she gasped. "I am toc twiny. Heaven bless you!" :And Celia, examining the heel .of baby's new socks, emilt,d as she said:

"Then that's all right. You won't be lonely now, Harold, while baby and I. are away." On January 18th, just eight months later, Harold Godward sat in his study, with a heap a news- papers littering the floor. One lay upon him knee, crumpled fiercely in Ms clutching right hand. The clock struck, and he raised his haggard- eyei to it.

"Eleven," he muttered. "Then she doesn't mean to come. Poor child, 1 expect she's upset. I wish she hadn't been there last night, and 1 hope she hasn't read :his. But if she has, she won't understand all it means to me: Than Heaven, she at least is innocent."

Then once again be smoothed the paper out,• and re-read the words which appeared to scorch his eyes. It was the same writer whose praiser had raised hint to the summit of his joyful pride eight months ago: "Chilled to the heart, cruelly disappointed, we sratched the curtain descend on the most cynically depressing play we ever remember to have witnessed and we came away in a state of actual bewilder-, went, for only half-a-mile away we knew that a theatre was ringing with plaudits for the work oh, the same author, a pure human work, which ir contrast to this is as light to darkness. If such things were possible, it might be supposed that the writer of the present pessimistically repulsive drama had come under the spell of some malignant personal influence during the last few months, which has twisted and warped his sense of right and wrong ; but there are no witches nowadays, and the enigma why the same man should have e'en us within a year a play that excites our noblest aspirations, and another that scoffs at all we hold sacred, is one that we cannot hope to solve. The piece is undoubtedly brilliantly clever, and will in all probability draw the town, which, considering the morbidly unwholesome tone of the plot, is a fact that may well be regretted by all true lovers of art. We can only offer our sympathy to the artists who had to represent some of these most repellant characters."

_ "It is true," he mottered. "I have gone to the bad, to the bad utterly. Marion doesn't know it yet; but how long shall I be able to keep silence ? It tears my heart out that she stays away to-day." He sat another minute, with grimly compressed lips, and then, with a stifled groan, he rose. lie must go down to the theatre, a rehearsal had been called for twelve. lie went out of the room, and met Celia in the

"Gin v, to the theatre?" she asked, brightly. -

"Yes, w he responded, curtly. "Then baby and I will not expect you to lunch. Come, Harold, cheer up! You can't hope that all your pieces will be equally successful. And it doesn't mattei" half so much in this case, since the play was bought outright. That. was a real bit of luck for us." Wit Is a soft smile, the young woman turned away, and Godward opened the house door. There was a tine sleet falling, and the outlook was as utterly cheerless as the author's heart ; .before he had taken a step, however, a messenger- boy ran up. "Harold Gpdward, Esq.?" Godward nodded, and the lad put into, his hand' an envelope with typed address. It appeared an entirely uninteresting communication, yet Godivard was conscious of a curious shrinking sense of chill as the note AV:I3 given to him. Instinctively he turned to the study and shut and locked the door ; and then, removing his, hat, he seated himself and opened the note.

-As his eyes fell upon the writing of the enclosure, he gave a sigh of relief. "Marion!" he cried, under his breath. "I am glad she has written." "Harold," the letter eommenced, "I have typed the envelope because I de not want anyone to know I have written to you to-day. lam more unhappy than I can. say. The notices are so cruel—so terribly cruel. Harold, I cannot come to you any more. Do not try to Persuade me. I will not come! Never again! Oh, try, try to do differently In the time to come. You are clever; there is no one who can approach you. I would say, try for my sake; but it would not be right. lam going straight away to-day, a long way off. If we ever meet in the future, please Heaven you will be your old self again. Burn this letter at once_ I bee it of von as a favour.

CELIA GODWARD'S DIARY.

BY MARY H. TENI•TYSON.,

Inthor of "Mrs. Montcrieff Settles Accounts,* "The Old Church block " "Violet Blerion's

On the morning of April 18th, 1900, the happi:l Seas of Harold Godward's life appeared to have reached its culminating point. Indeed, the universe / might have been searched in vain for another man in quite such an ecstatic state of contentment; for Godward was entirely satistied with his fellow man and woman, and also with himself, that day. He was of vigorous physique, and healthy minded en the whole, and hitherto his life had flowed so smoothly that no disturbing doubts of the perman ence of his good fortune obtruded themselves to cast an ominous shadow over the greatness; of his It is said, as a truism, that to know perfect joy 4t is necessary to have suffered ; but surely it is .hose who have suffered on whom those ghastly 'doubts obtrude with the strongest force. • Emil his boyhood Godward's lines had been cast in pleasant places. He was a posthumous child, but his mother had worshipped her only son. Ind had fostered in every any the boy's obvious tal,,,at.s. His school life had been a series of triumphs, triumphs which were not grudged to the brilliant lad, who combined athleticism with Latin verse; and who, considering what a clever chap he was, was singularly free from "side." At Ox7ford, whither he went in due course, his career was an honourable one; and, returning to his mother's home at the age of twenty-seven, he stated his intention of taking up literature as a means of increasing their somewhat limited income. And the doting, grey-haired mother rejoiced at her son's determination, being fully assured in her own mind her darling would be an honour to whatever profession he might select. For a few months Harold worked with a will, writing short stories and magazine articles to try his strength, and then a new interest came into his already full life. At the house of a college chum, he met the most beautiful and amiable girl in the world; and forthwith the young man fell deeply in love, so deeply that the silent applause he perceived in Celia Dent's lustrous soft blue eyes, when he had been persuaded into reading to a few privileged friends one of his stirringly dramatic romances, was more precious to him than his Another's out- spoken praise, or the demonstrative approval of hi- college cronies. Ire was already engaged to Celia Dent, and the marriage day edis fixed, when his mother fell and in three days died. Her son mourned her loss truly, but it was not unnatural that he was able to find consolation in the soft pressure of Celia's hand and the liquid tenderness of her eyes. The devoted mother, with her dying breath, bad begged her son not to postpone his Wedding; and, therefore, six weeks only after she had been reverently laid to rest., Harold married the beautiful girl, and a month later they conunenced their lives - together in the pretty house in Kensington in which he had been born. On April 18th, 1900, Harold. and Celia had been married for a year and n; half; their little eon Wis four mouths old. stron'; knd beautiful as •Cherub, and on -the previens ev'ening Godward's 'first mOdern comedy lilt? been produced, with tritolphant success, at a leading London theatre. But, although the previous night the almost franzit delight of the audience in the discovery of a new plimywright, who, even in his 'prentics days, could move them to tears or laughter at his ' will, was a joy to the young author, the noise and excitement of the situation had been almost over- whehaing ; and, moreover, his pleasure had been to some extent damped by the fact that Celia had not witnessed his glorious success. He could not find it in his heart to argue when -she had said that her duty to their child forbade her presence at the theatre. How proudf he had - been of her when she had declared her determina- tion to nurse her darling herself; how beautiful to him had been the spectacle of the tender mother and her baby. He dared not attempt to persuade her to neglect for once her sacred duties; but in the midst of his triumph his eyes had grown humid with sympathy, sympathy for the joy she had missed, and .with himself that he could not raise his eyes to hers, and see her soft lips part in the sweet smile of silent approbation which to him was so fraught with meaning. • At night when he had come home she had already gone to bed. He knew that this would be so, for she had told him that for the little son's sake she dared not risk late hours and over- fatigue ; and when, quivering with excitement and love, he had looked into her room, he had resolutely stilled the slightly chilling sense of dis- appointment that had crept over him, as in the dim light he perceived her lying with her child upon her arm, fast asleep. He had murmured a prayer for the two, but his voice was unsteady, and he shook his head im- patiently when he gained his own apartment. "What a fool I am!" he muttered. "1 am alto- gether overwrought. ram not super-sensitive as a rule." But here Harold Godward made a mistake. Throughout his life he had never lacked his full meed of appreciation; hitherto his self-love had received not even a prick, and therefore he was not aware that self-love existed in his composition. But a night's sound sleep had entirely restored his equanimity, and now, as he sat after breakfast with his pipe in the corner of his mouth, in the midst of a veritable sea of newspapers, reading aloud the notices of his play to his young wife, his happiness was complete. For an hour and a half lie continued to rend, while Celia nodded and smiled at him, and then he perceived that she grew restless. "I can't bear to interrupt you," she said, softly "but 1 ought to go to baby now." "Oh, let nurse bring him down,* he answered, quickly; "nobody will come in here. I have left the two most important papers to the last on purpose." The baby was brought, and then ifarold settled himself squarely in his chair, and took up the journal generally achnitted to be the most critical • in matters dramatic. A column 'and a half oh unstinted praise was devoted to the new play, and Godward's voice actually shook with emotion, as very huskily he read the peroration : "Indeed, it is many years since we have wit- nessed a play so entirely satisfying. Brilliant from • a literary point of view, intensely interesting, and breathing a spirit of benevolence that warms the heart and cheers the spirit. Here vice is punished and virtue rewarded, as they should be always, the true mission of art being to soothe and strengthen. We feel the better for a play like this which enlarges our sympathies, %bile it upholds our ideals, and with open arms We welcome this strong and pure-minded dramatist, who makes us weep for the sins of the world, but teaches us to judge 4ith charity." • Unable for 'the moment to express the.proud delight that almost unmanned him, Godward looked up at his wife, his strong, well-cut mouth tremulous with happiness, and then the tears rose in his eyes, and one or two tiny drops gathered on the lashes. Celia sat with her baby at her breast, pressing her treasure tenderly to her with outspread,delieate hands; her beautiful face was flushed, her lips were parted in a smile of rapture, and her slightly 'distended violet eyes shone with a lustrous light. ' He gazed 'at her for ,a moment, with almost reverent admiration. He could read her so truly. At last she was satisfied for him. He had remarked that during the time he had ';.wen reading to her, although she had smiled and nodded encouragingly, her satisfaction had been somewhat superficial, and he had wondered at it; ,but now he understood, and his heart yearned ' with love. ; This was the first notice in which the praise tontainzd no drop of leaven. All the critics had been imenimous in their commendation • but this man, the head of them all,. alone had had the courage t say the work as it stood was absolutely . beyond t improvement, and his wife was satisfied 'for him. t: Her wi-de countenance glowed, and as plainly as though she had spoken he could read her thmghts. "At last, it has come This is what I have waited for. This man rates my husband at his true worth." Celia was not looking at him, -her rapturous eyes seemed to be gazing into a distant paradise, nor did she move when the author's voice stopped with an abrupt catch of emotion; and in a minute he rose softly, and folding the mother and child in his aims, pressed his lips to hers. "That is good enough, isn't it, little woman ?" be said, trying to speak lightly. He felt her start strongly, as though it were • Ithuost ne.infid to her to have had her Proud dreams

War, be said, had been abolished in the civil realm, Hut there was a Iday in the English affairs when a grievance was tr>ttled upon some green sward. The Government Bench, the opposition Bench, all prayed for peace. Why then no reduction of armaments ? The financiers lived on war, then financiers must be annihilated. We must have peace,ir for no other reason than national safety, but peace also because the Lord our Master lathe Prince of Peace. On Christmas day a short service was held in the

Bold-street Wesleyan Chord.

The service on Sunday evening was mainly choral in character, selections from the " Messiah " being rendered by the choir. The choruses were especially well sung, the choir displaying a Nbry fine appreciation of the expression in "For unto us "end • Surely he bath boine our griefs." The soloists acquitted them- selves admirably. Mrs A Booth sang '• Rejoice "in bar usual style • Miss Carrie Hastings gave a very sweet rendering " I know that my Redeemer liieth," arid alias Whitmore was very successful in the singing of" He was despised." Mr Goo stry sang " Comfort ye "and 'Every valley," r.nd Mr W Warburton sang • For behold ! darknerri" and "The people that walked in dar kress." The general execution and musical rendition of the parts reflected great credit tivn choir and choirmaster (Mr Frank Hickman). During the evening the Rev H Williamson, 8.A.. delivered a short address on Retaliation " _ Christmas is the time when all thoughts should turn to Yeace,aud this thought suggests its opposite, " Retaliation." Although person knows why be retaliates, It is hardly likely that a valid reason can be found for his retaliation. Not being bared on well-grounded moral and spiritual principles, it is impossible really to justify retaliation. There is no question of material gain involved ; the sole benefit derived being the brutal and fiendish pleasure which the heart finds in inflicting injury. It is this aspect of retaliation which makes modern civilization a mere sham. The argument is often advanced, that not to retaliate signifies weakness ; but reflection will soon dispel this idea. The easier course is to let passion rule, the harder to restrain the animal nature, and thus prove the stronger and better man. The noble type of manhood is seen when, instead of the blind fury of anger the aggrieved person displays an infinite pity towards-the small mind which attempts the wrong. This is the Christ-like attitude which, instead of crying " Venge;• ance," weeps for Jerusalem, and in place ot hatred, is filled with love and compassion for those who revile and crucify. •

===== 0002601_19121228_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
EGGENTRIGHIES OF GENIUS.

[COPYRIGHT.]

To attempt to define genius would be a.rasb .indertaking. It would appear that almost ever:t writer and t4i.nker has a-different idea of what constituts nails. The well-worn adage which insists that it is " an infinite capacity for taking pains " is scouted by scientists, who considet genius as a morbid condition of the mind and prefer -to sneak 'of '" great Wits" a teitiadnese near allied." To others, genius simply means high intellectual ability. Elit we are not sc much, concerned with geniuses in themselves UF With the Inannerisms and abnormalities of these ;trangi?ly-constituted mortals. That -eccentricity 3ften accompanies genius has been remarked from tho time of Aristotle onwards. lie observed "Men illustrious in poetry, politics, and arts have. often been melancholic and mad,. like Ajax, or misanthropic like Bellerophon."

===== 0002601_19121228_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HABITS AND CONSERVATISM.

One of the best-known examples of eccentricity in personal habits. on the part of a great inai Vas Dr. Johnson's Curious impulse to touck every post he passed. He also, as is well known invariably went in Or out of a door or passages that the same foot (Boswell was not sure whethe it was the right or the left) should be first. Whenever he made a mistake in this respect he would return and carefully perform the feat to his own evident satisfaction. The great Napo- leon could not pass through a street, even at the bead of his army, without counting and adding' up the rows of house windows. A more common personal trait is left-handedness. Many notable lien, such as Tiberius, Michelangelo, and Ber- ri:ion, have been left-handed, or else, like • Leonardi da Vinci, ambidextrous. Many great men have manifested a marked distaste for change or novelty. It was as if they were too deeply absorbed in their own ideas. Frederick 11., who wished to foster a German national 'art and literature, ignored the merits of such men as Lessing, Herder and Goethe. rhis conservative prince disliked changing his coke so much that he had only two or three during his lifetime. The same may be said of Napoleon and his hats. Rossini could never tolerate travelling by rail. Laplaceldenied the fall of meteorites, for he said, and his objection .7arried much weight with his contemporaries, how can 'stones drop from the sky when there "Ire none there? And again we have it on good authority that Darwin believed neither in the ttone age nor in hypnotism.

CELIBACY AND INSPIRATION. While the number and brilliance of the names of men of genius who have lived and died unmarried, removes any possibility of celibacy being considered an eccentricity, it is equally 7,ertain that the misogynist is abnormal. Many great men have avoided marriage in order to have More time to devote ,to study. Michael Angelo sale "I have more than enough of wife in my art." Cavendish had a morbid. antipathy to women. Flaubert wrote to George Sand, the gifted French authoress: " The muse, however intractable, givis fewer sorrows than woman. I cannot- reconcile one with the other. One must choose." Adam Smith said he re- served his galltintry. for his books. What was put down as an eccentricity in Dr. Johnson, namely, the twitching of the muscles of the face, was, of course, quite involuntary. Buffon, Brougham, Hook, J. S. 'SIDI, Paley, and others were afflicted in a similar way. Many great authors have formulated 'ideas from the .subjects of . their dreams. Voltaire is said to have conceived during sleep one of the books of the " Henriade." Newton, it is stated, resolved mathematical problems in his sleep. But in their wakeful hours men of genius often seem to be inspired, and when the mood seizes them, work with feverish energy and intense absorption. Marini, when writing his "Adone," did not feel a sexions' burn of the foot. Tasso when composing, was like a man possessed. Instinctively or otherwise the in- spired genius takes 4iteps to augment the cere- bral circulation. Thus, ,Schiller plunged his feet into ice. Descartes buried his head in a sofa:. Leibnitz is said to have "meditated horizontally." • Rossini composed in his bed. Shelley often lay on the hearthrug with his head close to the fire.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
MELANCHOLY AND PEACTION.

It has been said—and there is some truth in .the contention—that when the period of. •in- spiration is over, the man of genius becomes an ordinary man, if not something less. In other words, there is a period of reaction. Isaac Disraeli,-in his "Curiosities of Literature," re- marks that some of the greatest poets have written the worst lines, and he instances Shakespeare and Dryden. A somewhat similar process ]olds zoo with the emotions. Cowper

wrote -JOllll unpin,- one Or tale mosz numorous of poems, between two attacks of melancholia. His habitual gravity found its reaction in a temporary gaiety. The story told of Aber- nethy, the great physician, will be familiar to many. He said to a patient: "You need amusement; go and hear Grimaldi—he will make you laugh, and that will be better for you than any drugs." "My God!" exclaimed the "but I am Grimaldi!" This recalle the reply given by Elopstock, the German poet, when asked to explain certain lines in one of his poems. "God and I both knew what it meant once," he said; "now God alone knows." The, tendency to melancholy seems to be the common heritage of thinkers. Nearly every language has its proverb to the effect that to feel sorrow more than other men is the crown of thorns of genius. Burns wrote in one of his letters: " My constitution and frame were ab origine blasted with a deep incurable taint of melancholia which poisons my existence." Schiller passed through a period of m.?lancholy which caused him to be suspected of insanity. The great painter, Van Leyden, believed him.. self.poisoned, and for years never rose from hia bed. Rossini about 1848 took it into his head that he was reduced to extreme poverty, so that he must beg. .This, of course, was an entire delusion, but his mind was so unbalanced that he was unable to compose, and indeed would not hear music spoken of. Zimmerman actually died of voluntary starvation, the result of fixed idea that he had no money to pay for food. This state of mind has frequently led to in- sanity and death, but then it ceased to be an eccentricity and became a monomania. STUPIDITY AND SENSITIVENESS.

Certainly it would ,seem that men of genius are either endowed with a dual personality or at times make almost incredible blunders. What could be more fatuous, for example, than this extract from " Marmonie de la Nature," by Bernardin de Saint Pierre, the talented autnor of "Paul and Virginia": "The melon has been divided into slices by nature in order that it may be eaten en famine; the pumpkin, being larger, may be eaten with neighbours." On the other hand, most highly endowed in- tellects are blessed or plagued with acute Sen- sitiveness. Diderot said: "If nature has ever made a sensitive soul it is mine. Multiply sen- sitive souls, and you will augment good and evil actions." Music has an extraordinary effect on some highly-susceptible natures. Alfieri NYne-I,Adies with Sterne. BousSeau, and Berlioz: "There is nothing which agitates the soul with such unconquerable force est musical sounds." Malibran, on first hearing one of Beethoven's Symphonies, had a convulsive attack, and had to be carried away. Carlyle, Musset, and Gon- court lived in a state of continual torment on account of the noises of the streets and the bells of the neighbourhood. With Jules de Goncourt, indeed, the horror of noise became an obsession. "During the last years of his life," wrote his brother, "lie suffered from noise as from a brutal physical touch."

• ABSENT-MINDEDNESS. ForgetfulneFs is a familiar characteristic which genius Shares with less-favoured mortals ft is related of Newton that lie once rammed the lontnts of his pipe with his y_iece's finger in- tStend of his own; and Brzoffsier said of him that • when left II:, room to seek for anything he asunlly returned without bringing it. Anollet zrsat seientiest, Amnere, once wrnte out urmuln iiiwhllll he was very much absorbed. n cab. IVIIPTI the cab dew-0 oway ui CEndok,nly rem enthe-ed Im is formula and set, off in pursuit. But perhaps the supreme exampl, of a bs:eill:lntndeirmas.i oarrratrel—of ' Bishop -:!!onster, On one °erasion a notice IVnti affixed -to th door of his chambers wl•i-h read: " The Master of the •house is out." Will it be believal that he stood thcrci await:ng his own return?

The wore this subject ef genius or filmoirmal intellectuality is studied the elehrer it becomes that in alinormality, as ekimlicre, 'extremes meet. EGOTISM.

"Man is the vainest of animals, and the poet is the vainest' of men," wrote Heine, in his; ironic style (he may be suppossd to have known),. and again, "Do not forget that ) um a poet,. and, as such convinced that men must forsake all and read my. verses." Many examphs of- this,inflated egoism might be cited, d:cl space. permit—from Budlitts, the poet, who refused. to rise when Julius Cmsar entered the coll:ge of poets, because he believed himself his super:cr- irt-the art of verse; to Balzac, who, when he had bn a new dressing-gown, wished to go out,. thus clothed, with a lamp in his hand, to excite the admiration of the public. It would alme;t seem as if genius implied eccentricity, or that: great excellencies were invariably accompanied by deficiencies. , Put in the opinion if many the subject defies exact treatment either by alienist or psychologist; iu other words, originality knows DO law.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
MODERN BETHLEHEM.

A turn in the road and Bethlehem comes into view.. As we draw nearer we are charmed with its aspect. It is built in a semi-circle on the side of a bill, with ft verdant plain stretching beyond it. In its setting of olive groves and fig trees it looks so old and hoary that one might well believe it has remained unchanged since Christ first opened His eyes upon it. And it is certainly the least changed of any of the places in the. Holy Lind made sacred by our Saviours association with them. You can visit Bethlehem with less shocks to reverential feelings, and certainly with a purer and simpler enjoyment, than you can in any other place in, Palestine. Despite the crowds, there is an air of repose and even a quiet dignity about the ancient city that is very gratifying after the noise and bustle of Jerusalem.. Modern Bethlehem may welt be described as a prosperous village of charming white houses, situated in the midst of beautiful country. As we stroll through, its narrow streets, filled with men and women in picturesque holiday attire, we recall that .Bethlehem was the scene of Ruth's primitive story, and that it, was here that thimuel anointed David as king.—Harold J. libepstone, in the " Millgate Monthly."

THE LEIGH CAPTAIN.

By means of brilliant passing and strong forward play Leigh were able, before 3,000 spectators, to gain a very creditable victory at Mather-lane, in 53132 Johnson's benefit match. Although playing against the wind in the first half, Leigh were able to maintain an almost incessant attack through their immense advantage for- ward, but the only try scored in this half was one by Roberts after a loon run, no goal resulting. Warrington v. Leigh.

RAIN SPOILS THE GAME.

It was fortunate for the finances of the club that the rain held off until the game was in progress. There was a fair crowd of about 5,000 spectators when Lee started for the visitors. The first item of note was an excellent round of passing by the Leigh backs which resulted in T Johasiin passing inside to Keegan, but the latter failed to pass Jolley. A heavy deluge of rain then came on and soaked the ground, makidg correct play impossible. ‘Varringtou had the advantage of playing with the wind ano rain at their backs, but they did not make the most of their opportunities. Leigh passed out the ball with surprising freedom, but the Warrington defence held out. The home forwards headed by G Thomas, R Thomas and Skelhorn rained the Leigh line and a score lookeil imminent, but Prescott saved in is daring manner. The pressure was maintained and the home backs handling in unison, Williams was put in possession by Renwick but he was well held a yard from the line. He scrambled over but the reteree negatived the point. Several hot scrimmages were fought out near the Leigh line but whatever chances Warrington had of opening out the game to the three quartere v,ere spoiled by the offside tactics of the Leign halves. On tour occasions they were penalised within easy distance of goal but Renwick and Thomas could not steer the greasy hall between the posts. The first score was a penalty goal to Warrington for offsicie by McGeiver near the posts. On this occasion Renwick landed a goal. On resuming Leigh made a raid on the Warrington line and Jolley was not over safe in clearing. Keen Game at Oldham.

Oldham were decidedly fortunate to win their match with Warrington at Watersheddings, Oldham, on Boxing Day. There were several changes on both sides, Oldham being without Wood,at full back,who fractured his arm the previous day, his place being taken by Cook Oliver was crafted into the back division. On the Warrington side Nicholas was brought in for Brown at half back.

The game was very fast from the outset. After Daiuteth bad failed at goal from an easy position, Oldham took up the running, and neat passing between Wharton and Farnsworth ended in the latter scoring, Fergusson failing at goal. Warrington took up a strong position afterwards, but a pass from Dainteth to Davies was intern lited by Lomas, and the play was transferred Several attempts were made to cross the Oldham line, but the defence proved sound, though Cook could not be considered safe. For offside Warrington were penalised, and Ferguson landed a good goal from the quarter line.' The game after this was confined chiefly to forward work, and play became scrambling. On the resumption the visiting forwards rustled tee ball almost to the Oldham posts, where Cook was called OD to save. Several onslaughts were made, and once or twice men got over, but were brought back for offside. Oliver made a splendid run for Oldham half the length of the tielu, but was eventually pushed into touch. Thig was practically the only piece of work tothe orea it of Oldham in this half, the final arriving with the home side hold- ing their five points' lead. Flue! : Latchford, three ; %Torrington C.L.B , nil. FAIRFIELD UNITED AGAIN VICTORIOUS. - - •

Fmitfield United were sg4in victorious on Saturday when they defeated Sankey Juniors by 2 goals to nil. Both goals were scored in the first half, Fairfield taking a,lvantage of thl strong wind blowing at the kick off. In the fist teu minutes Fairfield had rushed the ball into the opposing goal and, through one of Sankey's hacks banding the ball a penalty was given, from which Rowlands, Fairfild's skipper scored. Shortly after wards a neat shot Was put in by the versatile Simkin, making the score two up for Fairfield. WIDNES AND DISTRICT ALLIANCE. GREAT SANKEY V. WIDNES RECS.

A record gate witnessed this match on the Recreation Ground, Penketh. Considering the condition of the ground, a fairly good game was witnessed. Sankey opened strongly, Forster, Cook nd Whitfield having hard lines. Kelly was a stumbling block to the visit- ing forwards. Forster received a well-timed pass from Cook and opened the home team's account with a lovely left foot drive. A moment later Beech equalised for the Recs, after a tricky run. Just, before tne interval Cook broke through and left Doyle helpless with a fast grounder. In the second half end-to-end play followed The defence of both bides was magnificent. Result : Great Sankey, two goals ; Widnes Recs., one goal. FRIENDLY MATCHES Sr. HELENS CABLE JUNIORS V. WILDERSPOOL.

The Juniors showed capital form in the early stages of this match and Green opened the scoring, closely , followed by Mather. It was near half-time before the Cable added to their lead, they being three goals to the good when ends were changed. In the snood stage Wilderspool rallied and a good goal was scored by Knight. This proved to he their last chance, for the St Helens Cable Juniots had the bnlk of the play, and after H Morgan had scored twice in quick succession, the sixth and seventh goals were notched by Mather auri Morgan respectlvely. Result :—St. Helens Cables Juniors, seven goals ; ruilderspool, one goal.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LAND INQUIRY. RURAL LABOURER KEPT AS A SERF."

Minimum Wage Favoured.

Some of the results of Mr. Lloyd George's inquiry into the land system of the country are published In the current issue of the" National Weekly." Upon the reports which have new been sent in from many districts which have been covered by this inquiry (says the journal) the future policy of the Liberal Government is being framed, and, in view of the knowledge in our possession, we are able to affirm that this policy sill be of enormous importance to the social structure of the country, and put on foot a revolution in the conditions of life in rural England. . . All the private reports that are coming in to the Committee form a bony of facts and opinions which constitute a great indictment of the present land system, and a nirect challenge to the landowners upon their dealings with the labourers. It is pointed out in these reports that the rural labourer is kept in his position of a serf owing to the practical impossibility of becoming a peasant proprietor in the conditions now prevailing,and owing to the housing system in rural England. According also to the reports, the journal says, there is a woeful scarcity of cottages for labourers on the land, and the majority of landowners refuse to build others because they realise that so long as this scarcity continues they have the labourers under their control.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
TUDOR AND STUART COTTAGES.

The reports of Mr. Lloyd George's Land Committee state again and again, according as they deal with various districts, that the housing condition of the rural Population in England and Wales are appalling and intolerable. In thousands of cases the cottages are too small for the families living in them, so that over- crowding is seen in its most dreadful form, leading to many vices, and the inevitable &gradation of human beings. Large numbers of cottages are so old—dating from Tudor and Stuart times—that there are great cracks in the walls. The beams have warped and the wood and plaster work has become rotten; while no kind of sanitary comforts exist for people of our modern civilisation. Hut, again, according to the reports, there is no attempt on the part of many land- owners to remedy these great evils. It is stated that the worst conditions of rural life exist in the Midlands and South of England, which are still in a practically feudal state, and hardly touched by the spirit of modern progress.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
RADICAL CHANGE NEEDED.

By many details and statistics and stories of actual life in rural England (adds the "National Weekly") the Government, making use of the information which has been provided in this way, will attempt to prove that a Radical change in the relations between the landlords and the labourers must be ondertaken in I order to avert the continual and steady weakening of our national stock. The general trend of opinion in these reports is in favour of the establishment of a minimum wage for the agricultural labourers, and this, it is thought, will be one of the chief points in the Government policy. I The question is formulated, " How can the rural ' labourers he provided with sufficient cottages in which human beings may live decently and comtortably, and I how can they be made peasant proprietors acoordiug to some s 3 stem of occupying ownership ?" The suggestion put forward is that there should be a big national grant for this plirpose, and it la probable that the British ' public will be confronted with that proposition. Members of Mr Lloyd George's Committee are at present studying the Irish Land Purchase Acts,in order to draft out a scheme based on the pioposals of the Irish system which may be applied in a modified way to this country. It is recognised that the English land system is more complicated, and that purchase would he more costly. The proposal of the "single tax," based entirely on land, would not be brought forward,even in a tentative way, in the Government's programme tof land reform, adds the journal, which concludes:—" The object lesson of the Irish Land Acts will be used as a powerful argument in favour of a similar system in England."

THE OLD YEAR-AND THE NEW.

I give you the health of the oldest friend That, short of eternity, earth can lend— A friend so faithful and tri,d and true That nothing can wean him from me and y,m. He never leaves us—he never will Till our hands are cold and our hearts are still; On birthdays, and Christmas, and New years, too, He always remembers both me and you. How he loves us ! He pats our heads; And, lo ! they are gleaming with silver threads ; And he's always begging one lock of hair, Till our shining CI owns have nothing to wear. So here's a health in homely rhyme To our oldest companion, Father Time! May our last survivor live to be As bold and as wise and as tough as he. 0. W. Hmites. A NEW YEAR'S WISE.

Do you wish the world were better? Let me tell you what to do. Set a watch upon your actions, keep them always straight and true. Rid your mind of selfish motives, let your thoughts be true aud high, You can make a little Eden of the sphere you occupy. Do you wish the world were happy? Then iemember day by day Just to scatter seeds of kindness as you pass along the way ; For the pleasure of the many may be ofttimes traced

to one, As the hand that plants the acorn shelters armies horn the sau.

-ELLA WHEELER. WILCOX.

HOUSE OF COMMONS DIVISIONS.

LOCAL MEMBERS RECORDS.

The fourth break in the present Parliamentary session occurred last Friday week, when trie house adjourned for the Christmas vacation. Since the session opened in February last the house has divided on 470 occasions, and of this total no fewe, than 258 have taken place in the autumn session, and 58 of them during the last nine Parliamentary days. The following table shows the number of votes given during the session by the members in this district : Harold Smith, Warrington (C) 313 H Barnston, Edisbury (C) J E L Brunner, Northwich (L) 269 It J Neville, Wigan (C) Rigby Switt,Sc. Helens (C) Colonel W H Walker, Widnes (C) S Walsh, Ince (Lab) 284 Viscount Wolmer, Newton (C) 251

ONTARIO'S NORTHERN LAM—Ontario seems des- tined to furnish surprises. For many years it was supposed that the northern portion of the province was not suitable for agricultural settlement. The world now knows that in that area is included some of the most productive soil in the entire Dominion. Recent researches seem to indicate that certain districts in Northern Ontario are also suitable for the production of fruit. Experiments which have been made at the experimental farm at Monteith have shown that all kinds of apples and other fruits, save peaches, can be groan in kerning. If in practice this is found to be correct, it means a great extension of the apple growing area in Ontario, which is almost solely confined to Southern and Central Ontario. Perhaps the chief claim of Northern Ontario will be as a grain and stock raising country, but until more is known of that area of the province, no decided statement can he advanced. Mr F Whitson, a Commissioner for the Ontario Govern- ment, Northern Ontario, believes that at no great distant date a flourishing settlement will extend front the Quebec boundary westward for a distance of 350 miles.

DEATH OF THE REV. J, S. HUGHES.

Many of cur readers will regret to hear of the death cf the Rev. J. S. Hughes, formerly pastor of Hill Cliffe Baptist Church, who passed away at Shrewsbury on Monday. While discharging his ministerial work in Warrington, Mr. Hughes was one of the sturdiest champions of Nonconformity in the district, and took an active part in the proceedings of the Free Church Council. He was a native of Coin, near Ruabon, and before coming to Hill Cliffe he was for about a dozen years at Irwell-terrace Baptist Church, Bacup. For many years he held the position of secretary to the Brighton Grove Baptist College, Manchester. Even those who differed most from Mr. Hughes on matters of:principle, always recognised the absolute sincerity of his motives, and his good work at Hill Cliffe, to which he was most devotedly attached, will long be I remembered. The funeral will take place at Hill Cliffe tc-day (Saturday).

There was a distin- . SOME guished French author CHRISTMAS who wrote, not many REFLECTIONS. years since, "Give up

your optimism, and make it clear to yourself that we have to fight everything, and against everyone." That would be poor advice to give at a season like the present; and, indeed, it would be poor advice at any time, if for no. other reason, because the human heart cannot sustain itself on antagonisms and suspicion and disbelief in the good impulses and honest intentions of mankind. No man is so fre- quently proved to be in the wrong as he who has given up optimism. In a time of great national prosperity, Sir STAFFORD NORTH- COTE, the first Lord IDDESLEIGH, who was a kindly-hearted man, said there seemed to be everywhere an impatience of control and a conceit and vanity which boded ill for England if they were allowed to get too much ahead. We must assume either that they 'did not get too much ahead, or that they did not exist in such quantity as Sir STAFFORD NORTHCOTE supposed, for it is impossible to look back on the years that have intervened without some satisfaction at what has happened to England in the mean- time. There have been some hard times, and there have been wars and unsettling and harmful rumours of wars; and there have been strikes and other dislocations of labour, and the future has often looked black enough; but there is nothing in the retrospect by which pessimism can justify itself. The march may have been interrupted; but when it has been resumed it has always been a march forward. "Grow old along with me ; the best is yet to be," says Browning's Rabbi Ben Ezra; and this is not only a cheerful faith but a sound one, as all human experi- ence has proved.

Christmas has from time immemorial been associated with the idea of plenty. There has, as far back as history goes, been an excellent habit of distributing good things among those who could not obtain them for

.40 0 lit---- ,

Str-eet,

themselves. It is more than a couple of centuries since GEORGE WITHER sang : Now all our neighbours' chimneys smoke, And Christmas blocks are burning; Their ovens they with baked-meat choke, And all their spits are turning. Manners have changed much since the seventeenth century; but Christmas customs least of all, and if the spit does not now turn before the fire ,there i§r,,in genera I more than the former spaciousness in the ovens; and this year, greatly as the cost of living has increased, there is in almost all cases much more-to put into them. For there can be no disputing the fact that we are experiencing an almost unprecedented degree of nations, prosperity. Will there ever be a time when there will be no "out-of-works 2" That is something too good to hope for; but seldom in the history of our country has there been so little unemployment as now, for which reason the old rhyme which says that " Christmas comes but once a year, but when it comes it brings good cheer," can on few other occasions have been so true a descrip- tion of the actual event.

As a people, it has been said, we show creditably at the great holiday of December; and we do not mind adding that we show more creditably than some of our ancestors were wont to do. Our methods of enjoying • ourselves have, in the main, grown much less riotous and vastly more reasonable. There is more sanity about the observation of the festival, without any diminution of natural feeling and warmth of life. A Bishop to whom Mr. PEPYS listened on a Christmas Day just two hundred and fifty years ago preached against excess in plays and gaming, "saying that he whose office it is to keep the gamesters in order and within bounds serves but for a second in a duel, meaning the grooms' porter." That is one of the things that we arrange better in these days. We do not fight duels on Christmas Day. We are become, we may congratulate our- selves, altogether less quarrelsome. Do we still preserve the custom of eating too much? Perhaps so; but as a wise man has observed, "by eating and drinking together, sharing together one of the simplest and most primi" tive of human pleasures, and one which is closely connected with the sustenance of life, we quicken our sense of what we have in common." Who can eat or drink with those whom he despises or hates? The celebra- tion of Christmas has often been dealt with as if it needed to be defended. We shall not ourselves defend it. There is more widely diffused and unalloyed happiness at Christ" mas time than at any other season of the year. Merely to think of the thousands, even the millions, of family gatherings that take place, makes the heart glad.

The other day a leading Protectionist journal asked to what extent the people generally were participating in the times." Here is one answer to that question. 78. Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P., said the other day that the difference between 2 per cent. and 8 per cent. of unemployed "meant two million more workmen being employed." The Board of• Trade unemployment figures are now based on the returns of 885,000 trade unionists, who are only a very small proportion of the Wage- earners in the country. But, taking that small proportion alone, it is seen that 7•8 per cent. would mean that some 70,000 were out of work in 1908; while on the 2 per cent. recorded to-day less than 18,000 are out of work. So that it is provable that the" good times " have brought employment to over 50,000 wage-earners mho are members of those Trade Unions included in the Board of Trade returns, and the actual number not recorded in the Board of Trade statistics is many times as large, as Mr. Snowden shows. Further, in the November Board of Trade "Labour Gazette" it is shown that the weekly wages of some million and a half British wage-earners have increased since last January at the rate of n 05,000 per week. It is only Tariff Reformers who cannot realise what all this means to the men, their wives and their children.

We are pleased te learn THE DOCTORS AND that a large section of AND THE ACT. the medical men of Warrington are dis- posed to show a spirit of sweet reason- ableness with regard to the adminis- tration of the medical benefits of the National Insurance Act and that there is every probability of a panel being formed in this town. A deputation from the doctors waited upon the local Insurance Committee on Monday evening and the spirit in which various points of difficulty were discussed seemed to point to an amicable settlement being arrived at. This is typical of what is happening all over the country. The boycott engine ered by the British Medical Association has broken down, and the panels of doctors are everywhere filling with a rapidity that even well-wishers of the Act can hardly have expected. The attempt of the Opposition press to use the doctors as catspaws in their campaign against the Government is doomed to failure and by January 15th those workers who require them will be in enjoyment of the benefits , for which they have been contributing their weekly share during the past few months.

- Fortunately, Christmas THE OLD LESSON. 1912 has passed with- out any big disaster to mar its story, but the columns which chronicle the tale of the festive 'season are once more pitifully full of minor calamities and accidents, which have brought sorrow to many homes in the day of happiness. Some of them, doubtless, were mere tricks of fate, unavoidable, and the fault of no human agency. But that, as usual, cannot be said of the majority of the crop. Here we have a child burnt as ,a result of the old carelessness with Christmas-tree candles; there another badly injured through being left to play alone beside the fireplace without a guard. Again, here is the tale of a fire disaster wrought by the dropping of a match by a careless smoker, of a boat I swamped and a boy drowned in a gale in which he should never have been allowed to venture on to the water; and, again, of an airman who risked too much and met his death as the reward. There are many more stories of the same kind. Paragraph after paragraph chronicles them in dreary succes- sion. They are the ordinary accidents of a festive season, the reward of little omissions, little acts of carelessness. Probably it is use- less to point the moral of every tale. It has been done so often and paid such little heed to that it has become a tiresome task. Yet one cannot help thinking how very little would have kept the newspapers clear of the majority of these tragic paragraphs! Is it too much to expect a mother to see that her child does not play with fire, and that a man with his senses about him will put his live matches and cigarette ends in a safe place? Once again these are the most frequent cause of the fire disasters which top the list in the tale of Christmas calamities by air and land and sea. If one got to the beginning of the others, doubtless their cause would be as small and as unnecessary in nine cases out of ten. Some day, perhaps, the world will realise the importance of these little things.

Next Saturday we shall commence an " Examiner " legal column, through which queries from our readers will be answered by an expert lawyer. We hope the feature will prove both valuable and interesting. Full particulars are given in another column. * i.• *

Those local army veterans who are in needy circumstances were not forgotten this Christmastide, the Mayor generously arranging for parcels of seasonable fare to be sent to them. * * Truth lies at the bottom of the well—and philosophy in the Workhouse. One of the mottoes in the Warrington Workhouse dining hall on Christmas day read as follows: " Since we cannot get what we like, let us ltke what we can get. Surely this is the philosophy of content, whether applied to the Christmas fare or life's long pilgrimage ? * * * Other mottoes displayed in juxta—position might be considered as exemplifying the .doctrine of expediency, so beloved of poli- ticians, and evidently of paupers too. Side by side we see "Success to the Matron." "Thy will be Done." No doubt the Matron's will is law in such an institution as the Workhoi se. But grave offence may have been unwittingly given in another quarter. What of Mr. George Mather? Surely the "boss of the show" was entitled to an obeisance? * * Two fatal disasters with motor fire-engines last week-end, one at Liverpool and the other in the South, should give the Warring- ton Corporation pause. They somewhat hastily decided a few months ago that a motor fire-engine was essential to their local equipment. The decision was based on • quite insufficient data, and was never fully discussed by the Council. We hope the matter will be further considered before £l,OOO is spent on an engine which may prove to be a" white elephant " with a tendency to running amok! • * * Mr. Tom Reece has made the contest for the professional billiard championship next year exceedingly interesting by his defeat of Mr. Melbourne Inman for the second time. There are very few players more attractive in their style. He makes all the difficulties •of the table seem simple, and his control of the balls is exquisitely delicate. So well do e s he play, indeed, that his repeated failures in big matches have been a surprise and a disappointment to his many friends, He has not shown himself to possess the ,match-winning temperament, and in playing he :_cooler and more resolute Inman in

* * * Blessed is the season which engages the whole world in a conspiracy of love.— Hamilton Wright Mabie.

* By common consent the most popular Christmas hymn is " Hark ; the herald angels," which has a dreadful variant" Hark, how all the welkin rings," and was first used in worship by a strange accident. The printer at the University Press made a bad shot in the " make-up" of a new edition of the Prayer Book, and inserted this hymn to fill a blank space after the Psalms. It was never noticed by John Wesley, and not adopted by the Wesleyans until 1830. Most of the popu- larity is due to the music. The tune is taken from a movement in Mendelssohn's " Festge- sang," which was adapted to the hymn by Cummings, the Principal of the Guildhall School of Music, during a walk from London to Waltham Abbey..

* - Less than a year ago Norman Angell made his first appearance on a public platform. Those who were present on that Sunday afternoon will not readily forget the experi- ence. The place was crowded ; for there was much curiosity to see this wonderful , new world-force in the flesh. At last he stepped forward and there was a perceptible flutter of astonishment: the Great Man was •a small man! the World-Force was a quiet, modest, reticent, unassuming individual! There were some who seemed to feel they had been imposed upon! But as he went on, unfolding his arguments in a quiet voice, it became evident that here was a giant mind at work, and hardly one present but, before the address was over, had come under its irresistible sway. He is no orator, if by oratory you mean flowing periods, impassioned appeals, soul-stirring protests. But if you would have close-packed thought unfolded to your listening mind, if you would have light flashed on to the dark spots of your understanding, and have what was before obscure made plain, if you would know fsheer intellectual joy, hear him.— From an article on "Norman Angel!: War- breaker," in the January Pall Mall Magazine."

* - The housing question which is so much to the fore at present, and which has recently engaged the attention of the legislators in our Local Parliament, is a very old one, as may be seen from the following proclamation by Queen Elizabeth in 1580 against the build- ing of new houses and the further increase in London :

"To the preservation of her people in health, which may seem impossible to continue, though presently by God's goodness the same is per- ceived to be in better estate universally than has been in man's memorie. Yet when there are such great multitudes of people brought to inhabit° in small roomes, whereof a great part are seene very poore, yea, such as live a life of begging, or by worse means, and they heaped up together, and in a sort smothered with many families of children and servants in one house, it must needs follow, if any plague or popular sick- ness should by God's permission enter amongst those multitudes, that the same would not only spread itself and invade the whole citie and confines, but that a great mortalitie would ensue the same where Her Majestie's personal presence is many times required. For remeclie whereof . . Her Majestic . . . doth charge and straightly command all manner of persons to desist and forbeare from any new buildings of house or tenement within three miles from any of the gates of the sayde citie of London, to serve for habitation or lodging for any person; and also to forbear from letting or setting any more bmilies than one onely to be placed in any one house."

"Examiner" New Year Feature.

Next Saturday, January 4th, we shall introduce a new feature to " Examiner " readers in the form of a

LEGAL COLUMN.

_ Any reader may write to us in full con- fidence for advice on all matters connected with the law, and the queries will be answered from week to week n our LEGAL COLUMN, by an expert lawyer. Your neighbour's hens may be making horticulture a vexation instead of a joy: Your dog has perhaps had a serious difference with a motor cycle. Your great uncle may have died at the Back of Beyond and left you three acres and a cow.

The Insurance Act perhaps presents you with a knotty personal problem. You may have slipped ever so slightly from the path of rectitude (though we hope not). But in any of these events, a thousand and and one others, you will want to know your legal position.____

WRITE AND ASK US.

Address all queries to the " Warrington Examiner," mark your envelope " Legal " and enclose your name and address solely as a guarantee of good-faith. Initals or a nom-de-plume should accompany the name for use in the LEGAL COLUMN.

Dramatic Society. The Warrington Dramatic Society's choice of Mr Thomas Rareward's great drama, " Sunday" for stage production should prove highly popular. An exceptionally strong cast has been chosen, and the performances will take place at the Royal Court Theatre on January 16th, 17th and ISth. The proceeds will, as usual, be given to local charities. Warrington P.S.A. The meeting of P.S.A. members was held as usual on Sunday in the Park Picture Palace, a very enjoy- able programme being provided. Thanks must be tendered to Mr Blease who took the chair, Miss Bate for her solo "Sun of my soul," and Mr Parkinson for his 'cello solo. Towards the close of the proceedings the president (Mr Nelson) delivered an able address. The orchestra in "Nazareth" acquitted themselves with their usual ability.

Mr and Mrs Humphrey Owen, 6, Aikin-street, will be the recipients of hearty congratulations to-day on the celebration of theirlgolden wedding. They were married at the Warrington Parish Chureb, on December 28th, 1862: Both are members of old Warringt-rn families, Mr Owen being the eldest son of the late Mr James Owen, who many years ago carried on business as a sin ernaker in Bank-street. For close unon farty years Mr Owen has been employed at the works of the Whitecross Co., Ltd.. and has gained the esteem alike of the firm and workpeople. He is an , enthusiastic disciple of Izaak Walton and has long been a member of the Warrington Angling Associa- tion. Mr and Mrs Owen have brought up ten children, all of whem are married.
