Girls' -Club. The annual Christmas entertainment to the members of the Girls' Club,West-street, took place on Thursday, last week when a very large number sat down to a substantial tea. Afterwards Miss Bessie Dutton pre- sided and a lantern entertainment was kipdly given by Mr Wilcockson, who showed a very large series of fine slides- photos taken by himself from the beauty spots of England, Ireland and Wales. These were so good and so ably described by Mr Wilcockson that they were received with rntteh enthusiasm. The lantern was cleverly manipulated by Messrs F Wilkin- son and W C'artright. The proceedings were con tinned by a dialogue well given by three members of the Club, and some charming recitations by Miss Taylor. Hymns were sung by the gills. A gift of orangeg from Miss Roberts was much enjoyed. Warm votes of thanks were expressed to the kind helpers, notably to Miss Beswick secretary, and to Mrs March- rnent, matron. Ask for Whitbread's Famous Ales and Stonts in screw-bottles.—Sole. agent, PETER LEIGH, Ho rEe- market st.—Advt. Death of Mr. Joseph Bunch. A well-known and respected townsman passed away on Saturday in the pei son of Mr Joseph Bunch, 191, Winwick-roadJather of Mr C E Bunch,works manager for the Pearson and Knowles. Coal and Iron Co. The deceased, who was 78 years of age, was a member of a family who' for generations were engaged in iron- making, and was a nephew of the late Mr. Hall, an ironmaster of Bloomfield, the inventor of the present method of 'puddling. He came to Warrington about 42 years ago and was engaged in the Bewsey depart- ment of Messrs Pearson and Knowles' works and only retired a few months ago. The cause of his death was cerebral hemorrhage. He was a Liberal in politics and a warm supporter of the Government's legislation for the benefit of the workers. A staunch Churchman, he had been a regular worshipper at St Ann's Chuich since the days when the Rev W J Melville was the vicar. He had held office at different periods as warden and sidesman. His many good qualities had earned for him the confidence of the firm and the good- will and esteem of the xvorkpeople. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Tuesday, in the presence (.1 many relatives and friends Ask for Whitbread's Famous Ales and Stouts in screw-bottles.—Sole agent, PETER LEIGH, Horse- market st.—Avt. Sailor In Trouble. -

_ At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday George Foster, stoker on H.M.S. Lion was charged with being drunk and disorderly and with assaulting Police-Constable Harrison and Winifred Foster, his stepmother. It was stated that at 1.40 on Christmas morning a man came to the rfficer and said he heard screams of murder coming from the house, 25, Greenall-street. On proceeding there be found the prisoner and another man thrashing Mrs Foster. The clothes were torn from off her back, and she was in a terrible plight. The officer restored quietness for a time, but prisoner followed him along the street, and, owing to his disorderly conduct, be was compelled to take him into custody. A long and violent struggle ensued, and the prisoner bit him on the finger, the teeth penetrating to the bone. The officer had to use his staff. The Chief Constable said the struggle lasted practically an hour, and but for the assistance of a civilian and other officers, be.did not know what would have happened. Prisoner, who said he did not remember anything about it, was sent to goal for three months. LAAIPS, Oil Stoves, Coke Stoves.—J.S. GARLAND Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. 111-kept Dogs. • At the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday, before Councillor H Roberts (in the chair), and other magistrates, Hugh Tyrer,George-qtreet, pleaded guilty to a charge of having been cruel to nine dogs by not giving them proper food and attention between the 12th and Mtn December. Inspector Dee of the , R.S.P.C.A. stated that in consequence of information received on the 15th December be visited the premises of the defendant in George-street. He told him that he had called to see about some dogs which he under- stood were in a bad way. Defendant said they were a bit neglected. He (the officer) saw some of the dogs in a bad way and where they were kept was damp and ill-kept. There was no food of any description about and the animals were iery ravenous. Asked if he had any more dogs,defendant answered in the affirma- tive. A porneranian was found in a poor condition in the house and in the middle bedroom in a small wooden clothesbox under the bed he found a fox terrier bitch in a very poor condition. Defendant said it must have got there itself but subsequently he ; admitted baying put it there—Defendant was ordered to pay the costs of the case—£l Os. 6d, and he prom- ised to get rip of some of the dogs.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
POLITICS OR FLAPDOODLE.

A REVIEW.

When Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., was " dumped " in our midst, and Mr. Robert Pierpoint was told to leave the fight for the Conservative and Unionist cause in the hands of a younger man, in a not altogether pleasant way, we were led to believe that a paragon of political perspicacity was being presented to the electors, and henceforth and for ever Warrington would be famous on account of its representative. Only a few years before that, in 1905 to be correct, when the candidate at the forthcoming election was adopted, Sir Gilbert Greenall is reported to have said that : "Mr Pierpoint had repre- sented the borough in Parliament in a most praiseworthy manner for over a period of twelve years. As for himself he always liked to back a horse he tnevii. He knew Mr Pierpoint, and he thought that most people were of the same way of thinking as himself. Rather than try a fresh horse, he thought they could very well and safely trust Mr. Pierpoint to lead their colours to victory. (Loud applause.)" The late Mr. John White, than whom there was probably no harder worker for the party, said that : "In Mr. Pierpoint they had a man who would support the ' principles of the Conservative and Unionist party; and it was very gratifying to all of them that throughout his Parliamentary career, Mr. Pierpoint had never swerved from the principles they held, but he had always steadfastly supported those measures which were for the good of the country generally, (Applause)." We simply mention those facts to show that there must have been something out of the ordinary in Mr. Harold Smith, or at any rate they must have thought that there was, or else Sir Gilbert Greenall and others would not have tried "a fresh horse." * * *

Now we want to know whether the party is satisfied with its choice, and whether the members are not somewhat ashamed of themselves for allowing a gentleman who had served them so faithfully,and well, according to their own statements to be thrown over. Whatever may have been the reason for the change of candidate,the representative to-day is not treating the electors as they were led to expect. That must be apparent to anyone who has read, or listened to the speeches of the Member. Each time he has come to Warrington we have flattered ourselves, and him, by thinking that the sequence would be broken and this time, at any rate, we would have an instructive and statesman- like speech,and again and again we have been disappointed. We have heard, until we are really tired of listening to him, these puerile repetitions that as long as he had the honour of representing us in the House of Commons he would fight with all the energy he could command, &c., &c., &c., and of the pride it had given him. &c., &c., &c., and then when his political career was ended he would be able to look back upon the fact that War- rington took him, young, unknown, and untried, &c., &c., &c. Some of us may like to be told by him that he has been honoured, and there is no doubt about it, but we do not need to be reminded of the fact every time he speaks. He is such a busy man that we will forgive him if he leaves it out and fills up the time by imparting to us some of the political knowledge which we are told he possesses. He is "young, unknown, and untried"and we assure him that he will have every encouragement if we really know that be is doing his best, and not playing to the gallery.

* * * There is such a marked contrast between the thoughtful speeches delivered by Mr A H Crosfield, and those parrot-like repetitions of Mr Harold Smith M.P. that one cannot help remarking upon it. In one case we have a politician who recognizes that politics is a serious and a responsible work; in the other we have an artiste who believes that if a man wants applause he must play to the gallery. Here you have, side by side, the way in which the two gentlemen approach politics. Contrast the two statements:—

MR. A. IL CROSFIELD. MR. DAROLD SMITH M.P. "I do not talk like this 'There is a great merely bccause I am temptation, whether at addressing you whose Westminster or any interests are chiefly with other Music Hall, to play the workers. I talk in to the gallery. My exactly the same way on idea is that every questions of this kind when artiste, in whatever walk 1 meet employers." of life, who wants to succeed must play to the gallery; but the man who succeeds is the man who prevents

the Frallery from finding out. You most endeavour to delude the public into the belief that you are r eally quite natural and net playing to the gallery." After that, when are we to know when Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. is not playing to the gallery?

* * *

The rapid increase of our manufactures, commerce, navigation; the additional pro- tection and security afforded the distant possessions of our Empire ; the loyalty,public spirit, industry and enterprise of the King's subjects; the care.of the aged in the form of old age pensions; the beneits to the sick under one of the most important acts placed upon the Statute Book have been referred to by Mr. Crosfield from time to time, in a most thoughtful manner, but, what of Mr. Harold Smith, M.P ? How has he dealt with them ? Has he not jeered at the work that has been accomplished? And is he not now using his voice to decry Home Rule in that very elegant phrase of his : "one of the most infamous, tyrannical and dangerous proposals which has ever been submitted to any Parliament in the world " ? And how often he speaks in his own sweet, polite way of "the hypocrisy, cant, humbug and dis- honesty of the Liberal Government " ! And his chivalry towards ladies is very marked When he refers to some of them as "a few respectable middle-aged women " which, of course, causes his devoted adherents to guffaw like a litter of grunt- lings. And then that insinuation of his that after Mr. Crosfield had been to dinner with the Caledonian Association he was off to the station to find the next train to the golf links in the South of France. And so he goes on playing to the gallery, making his listeners laugh, and, incidentally, making every responsible citizen sick to death of such pettifogging, churlish behaviour. The symptoms of the disease which has attacked the Tory Party since Mr. Bonar Law became stage manager and Sir Edward Carson "topped the Bill" have become most pro- nounced in the Member. Whether Sir Edward Carson, who is so fluent in his tears, and eloquent in his execrations, is the artist's model we do not know, but we sincerely hope that the days of our humiliation as a thinking people may soon end, and that in the New Year farce and comedy and contumely may be taken from the boards, and politics may

be considered worth while his attention. Let him stop his fooling and commence to make !Warrington famous. A Happy New Year to you all—and may you never play to the gallery.

CHRISTMAS FOOTBALL NOTES.

WARRINGTON'S ILL-LUCK AT OLDHAM.

Since November 21st Warrington football followers have not had the p!easure of exuberating over a League victory. On that date St. Helens were beaten in a mid-week engagement. This was hut a solitary interlude, and the team has since been buffetted about and suffered some humiliating reverses. On Christmas Day the long sequence was broken and Leigh, who I provided the fare at Wilderspool, were vanquished by two goals to nil, after eighty minutes of muilarking. It was a fortunate circumstance for the finances of the club that the heavy deluge of rain which visited the district delayed its coming until the game was in progress, or otherwise the loss would have been greater. As it was one did not see the usual big Christmas crowd.

Of the game itself, there is little which calls for special comment. The heavy rain had reduced the playing patch to a pea-feet quagmire and correct play was altogether out of the question. There is this to be said in mitigation of Warrington's poor show. Whenever the locals got within striking distance of the Leigh line either McCeiver or Cain (the Leigh halves) adopted the unsportsmanlike tactics of running round the pack and playing offside. These offences were too numerous to recall, but if our goal kicking had only been on the maik the debit against Leigh would have been increased. Renwick did manage to find the right direction on two occasions and this completed Warrington's scoring account. There were other occasions when Warrington came near recording something tangible. Once Williams was floored a yard from the line, whilst on the other wing both Tranter and Davies made a sad mess of a high pass from Renwick, when the opppsition had been over- come. Leigh commenced in a style which boded ill for Warrington, but they failed to maintain their early promise and before the interval they were sub- jected to severe pressure.

The feature from a Warrington point of view was the cleverness of the forwards, who beat their opponents for possession with ease and romped round them in the open. Prescott was indeed fortunate in checking one rush by G Thomas, R Thomas and Skelhorn. On the occasions when Leigh gained possession sufficient was seen of 11cGeiver and Cain to denote that they were dangerous when breaking away from the scrummage. McGeiver several times gave the home halves the "dummy," but he lacked support from his colleagues and his efforts came to nought. It was a very narrow squeak for Warrington when he darted through in the second half but his pass was forward Just at that period Warrington were experiencing an uncomfortable five minutes. Renwick clean missed the ball over the line and Williams kicked it against the railings when two Leigh tnen were in the act of grounding. These were the only occasions on which Leigh were really dangerous. Daintith has the enviable reputation of being the best mud-larker in the Warrington team and he again upheld his reputation. He caught. cleanly and never hesitated to go down for the ball. Young Brown also rendered yeoman service and the pair looked like niggers at the close of the game.

The presence of Renwick had a steadying influence on his colleagues and there was not that raggedness about the play of the threequarter line as was evident in the Wakefield match. Little was seen of Tranter until the closing stages of the game when he obliged with one of his characteristic dashes for the line. Of the wings it would be inadvisable to judge the abilities of Williams on such a day and under such conditions as prevailed. Once or twice he caught the eye, and it is likely he will render useful service to the club for a season or two. Davies is the most promis- ing of the younger players. He has plenty of speed and dash, but is yet a little raw in his methods. The club will do well to persevere with this youth. Of Boxing Day's engagement with Oldham, there can be no doubt that we were decidedly unfortunate in sustaining a reverse. Even the most enthusiastic Oldham supporter was forced to admit that on the day's play the spoils had not gone to the better side. The ill luck seemed to dog our footsteps right from the commencement. In the first place Jolley missed his connection at Widnes and did not put in an appear- ance at Oldham until the game had been started. For quite ten minutes Warrington were playing only twelve men and the mortifying part about the result is the fact that the only try was scored whilst we were so handicapped. Injuries to players also caused depletions in the side which accounted for Leigh. G Thomas and I?, Thomas two of the best fo wards we have at the present time were out of the side. Young Brown was also unable to play. Nicholas being drafted to the half back position. Considering these circumstances one must admit that the performance was highly meritorious.

It is unusual for us to abuse the referee when defeats come our way. We have so often incurred them that we have become hardened. But in Thurs- day's game Warrington have just cause for a grumble over same of the referee's decisions. One case in point was when Nicholas, boring his way through a scrummage, grounded the ball in a favourable position over the line. The referee was not close up enough to see the legitimacy of the point, and as a result he gave a negative decision. The Warrington players as well as Oldham frankly admitted that in their opinion the try was a perfectly fair one. On other occasions be erred in his judgment.

It was a determined and dour struggle, chiefly con- fined to the forwards and the Oldham vanguard had to play second fiddle. The Warrington sextette wheeled the packs in typical Yorkshire style and when on the move gave the Oldham backs a proper worrying time. It was mainly due to their excellent

The A team have won and lost one in their Christmas engagements. On Wednesday morning they gave Leigh a hard game, but retired defeated by six points to nil. They atoned for this on Boxing Day when they disposed of a strong team representing Oldham A. It shows the wealth of reserves at the command of the Oldham club when such players as White,Young, Dixon, Webster. Wise,and McCabe are playing in the second team. The locals owed their victory to the fine goal-kicking of Critchley, who piloted three beauties over the bar. Welsby, a local recruit, made his debut and created a favourable impression. It was a near thing for Warrington, as White only missed by inches a goal kick in the last minute. A successful effort would have given his side a point victory.

To-day Warrington have a third visit from a York- shire team. Leeds sprung a surprise on Thursday by smiting the powerful Hunslet team on their own ground. In view of the improved display of the Warrington men at Oldham it is not unreasonable to expect them to gain their first victory over a York- shire team. A repetition of Thursday's form will bring about the desired effect.

The team will be Jolley, full back; Williams, Renwick, Tranter and DAvies, threequarters ; Daintith and Nicholas, half backs; G Thomas, Naylor, Cheaters Fernley, Skelhorn, Cox, forwards.

DOCTORS AND THE INSURANCE ACT.

HOPEFUL OUTLOOK IN WARRINGTON.

Will the doctors of Warrington work the Act? is the question which many people have been asking themselves when consider- ing the Government promise that the medical benefits of the National Insurance Act shall come into operation on January 15th. We are glad to be able to state that the outlook locally is hopeful; and the prob- ability is that a Panel will ultimately be formed. Many doctors have not hesitated to declare their willingness to serve and the expectation is that Warrington will be in a position to take full advantage of the pro- visions. of the Act on the appointed date. A deputation from the medical men waited upon the local Insurance Committee at the Town Hall on Monday evening. Several points affecting the working of the measure were discussed and the spirit of the meeting indicted the likelihood of an amicable settle- ment being arrived at.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
EQUALLY DIVIDED.

The latest- report is :that the members of the profession in Warrington are equally divided on the question of working the Act. At a meeting last'evening it was decided not to take any steps until after the conference with Mr. Lloyd George on January 6th. There is, however, still every prospect that a panel will ultimately be formed.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING. The Warrington A.C. held a successful handicap on Xmas Day, about 1,000 spectators witnessing a thrilling contest. C. \rose ran grandly throughout and, after a great finish, deprived W. Bolton of first place by the narrowest of margins. A. Gregeon was third, W. Walters fourth and T. Turton fifth. On the motion of C. Vose seconded by the last of of the prize winners, H. Payton a vote of thanka was accorded to the subscribers to the prize fund.

A CHAPTER OF LOCAL HISTORY.

EVENTS OF 19

It cannot be said that much local history has been made during the year which is fast ebbing to its close. This conclusion, however, does not imply a period of stagnation. On the contrary, although there have been few events of outstanding importance. 1912 will be remembered as a year of great activity in all depart- ments of local life and much useful work, which will Lear fruit in the years to come, is to be placed on record.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COAL STRIKE.

Unfortunately the early part of the year was clouded by the national coal strike which began on February -30:h and continued for five weeks. Like all com- mercial communities, Warrington depends for its very existence upon an immense coal supply and the result of the stoppage of the mines was that in the -course of a week most of the great works were stand- ing idle and thousands of workmen were walking the streets. So acute did the distress become that a distress fund was opened by the Mayor, to met the necessities of the very poor, and over .--CI,OOO was quickly subscribed. Remarkable ac flea were wit- nessed at the Education Offices which for several days in succession were besieged with men and women applying for tickets to entitle them to obtain food. At all the leading works soup kitchens were estab- lished and proved an invaluable boon to those whom only a week's wages separate from dire poverty. It was with a profound sense of relief that the com- munity heard of the settlement of the strike, but the effects of the suspension of work were felt in humble homes for many weeks aftercvards.The committee which administered the distress fund subsequently issued a report containing statements which to say the least, were startling, if not disquieting We were told that "The committee were impressed with the fact that a number of those who were earliest and most insistent in their applications were the higher paid men in connection with various industries. The lab urers and lower paid employees were amongst the last to come forward. This perhaps is due to the fact that many of the latter are continually living on the verge of starvation, and are thus more prepared to meet privation and distres. It is with regret that we have to record our opinion that by neglecting to exercise a little thift in prosperous times many of the better paid workers, &c., were obliged to have recourse to the public fund in the early stages of distribution. It is almost incredible that men and women, particularly women, would dare to venture through the streets to the relief office in the filthy condition iii which many of them were. Several of the women were not only dirty but also verminous. Another matter which is a cause for surprise and regret is the bare- faced attempts at deceit. In a large number of cases it was only after very careful cross-examinaticn of the applicants and enquiry from the works that it was possible to get at the truth." POLITICAL.

In the realm of politics, both parties have had a strenuous year, the alertness and energy displayed having occasionally been worthy of general election battles. The progressive organization has been maintained in first-class fighting trim, each branch vieing with the other to keep its armour bright so as to be able to play a splendid part in helping to regain Warrington for Liberalism when the next trial of strength comes. Daring the spring a very active campaign was con- ducted by the Liberal Association and the Progressive Labour League in support of the much-abused and misrepresented National Insurance Act. The detailed explanations given at the numerous ward meetings were welcomed by the great mass of the workers and helped to dispel many of the false impressions deliberately fostered by those who, to serve party purposes, were only too anxious to wreck this noble charter of public health which will be a source of untold blessing in years to•come. In March Mr. Peter Peacock succeeded to the chairmanship of the executive committee of the Liberal Association and under his tireless and enthusiastic leadership a ,great deal of aggressive work has been accomplisbed. Mr. A. H. Crosfield, whom the Liberals of Warrington are proud to acclaim as their standard bearer, has taken a prominent part in the educational propaganda and in addition to addressing numerous meetings of the various sections of the Liberal forces, has. through the columns of the " Examiner " made a point of keeping electors closely in touch with the progress of events. A very gratifying feature of the year's happenings has beep:the reorganisation of the Junior Liberal Association which is now attracting a mest enthusias ic band of young workers, with Mr Fl Lloyd Jones as their energetic chairman. The association came prominently before the public on March 15th by arranging a mass meeting at which Mr W G C Gladstone, M.P., grandson of the revered " Grand Old Man " was the principal speaker. His lucid exposition of Home Rule, as an urgently needed democratic measure for the salvation of Ireland's industries, will not readily be forgotten. On June 27th the association made an important forward move by opening spacious and well-appointed .club premises in florsemarket-street. The establish- ment of these headquarters was rendered possible by the kindly forethought and generous help of members of the Crosfield family and it was eminently fitting that Mrs A H Crosfield should perform the opening ceremony. The association is now making encourag- ing headway, for the club is not devoted solely to recreative aims, but is the scene of numerous meetings, a happy innovation being the formation of a mock "Town Council" in which municipal problems are debated. The Progressive Labour League held another remarkably successful open-air campaign dur- ing the summer months when the speakers included Mr J McVeagh, M.P., one of the leading stalwarts of the Nationalist party, and Mr Caradoc Rees, , late Liberal candidate for Denbigh boroughs. I On September 23rd a big meeting in the I Co-operative Hall was addressed by Mr. W. 0 Fallon,' Nationalist candidate for Mid-Cork. Without die- I paraging any other branch of the party, the palm for •consistently strenuous work must be awarded to the Women's Liberal Association which, under the leader- ship of Mrs. A. H. Crosfield I (president) and Mrs. Edmund Osborne (secretary), has left no stone unturned to maintain interest and enthusiasm at the highest pitch. In February the association promoted a fancy dress carnival, which provided one of the most picturesque spectacles ever witnessed in the Parr Hall and proved an unqualified success. In the spring and during the recent autumn meetings have been held in the wards and have proved a most 'valuable means of keeping the members and .officials in touch with each other. Ihe annual picnic ,of the association to Eastham, on July 30th, was a novel and highly enjoyable affair. It might easily have been marred by a tragedy for, when the trippers returned to Latchford, a man who was awaiting their arrival, fell into the lock. At great personal risk a seaman promptly plunged in to the rescue, although it was a dark night and the man had slipped into the narrow space between the vessel and the side of the locks. Happily the rescue was successfully accom- plished and the bravery of the sailor was afterwards acknowledged by the association in the form of a handsome prPsentation. The Liberal Association has held two highly successful large meetings, the speakers being Mr Tom Wing, ex-M.P. for Grimsby, and Mr Clement Edwards, M.P. Important structural alterations at the Liberal Club have been placed in band and they will include a ball to be erected by Mrs. Crosfield, of Colwyn Bay, as a memorial to the late Mr John Crosfield. The Conservatives added not a little to the gaiety of local political life in January by •arranging for a visit of "the dumperies " to Warring- ton. It was a weird exhibition and Free Traders derived much more satisfaction from it than the Tariff reformers, the trashy character of the foreign goods displayed and the nervouaness of the stallholders when •confronted by determined questioners quite robbing the affair of any value as a practical demonstration of the blessings of Protection. In connection with the exhibition Sir George Doughty, M.P., addressed a meeting in the Parr Hall. "FE. " has, of course, visited Warrington during the year and spoken in characteristically flippant vein. The great event was, however, the meeting on November '3oth, when Sir Edward Carson, M.P., the fire-eating leader of the Ulaterites, dealt with Home Rule from the Belfast standpoint but failed to make any great impression. The majority of the townspeople were too amused with the theatrical demonstrations c'ennected with Union Jack Day to attach ranch importance to the political oratory which was its ostensible purpose. MUNICIPAL. From the municipal point of view, the year has not witnessed the launching of any striking new project but solid work has been done in a quiet way. The erection of the new Warrington Bridge, which is to he 80ft, wide, has steadily proceeded, though the apparently slow rats of progress has occasionally been the subject of critical comment in the Council Chamber. Of course, while the foundations were

===== 0002601_19121228_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
112 REVIEWED.

Ibeing prepared it was obvious that there would be comparatively little to impress the passer-by, but it is now evident to everybody that something substantial is being accomplished, the concreting of the " ribs " being the task immediately in hand. A generally-welcomed improvement is the provision of a footbridge across the Mersey at Howley. The structure, which provides a convenient means of communication between the Howley and Latchfoid Wards, was onened by the Mayor on August Ist. Our local legislators, under the leadership of the Street Improvement and Lighting Committee,which owes much to the initiative of its chairman (Alderman Bennett), are preparing to take full advantage of the Housing and Town Planting Act to ensure that the development of the borough shall ptoceed on well-considered and symmetrical lines. All the land in the town which remains un-built upon is to be included in the scheme which the Corporation have in view, and at a preliminary cell- ferenae the principal landowners expressed themselves as willing to co-operate with the authorities. A Local Government Board inquiry was held on October 16th and the sanction of the Board to the preparation of a definite scheme has now been received. The burdens of the ratepayer show an ever-upward tendency and the rates levied by the Council in April reached Ss in the an advance of 2d. on last year. This, however, was entirely due to the increased expenditure on education and not to lavish over- spending on the part of any departments of the Corporation. During the summer the long-needed extension of the Corporation baths in Legh-street, was commenced and when it is completed Warriugton will boast one of the largest and most efficiently equipped public baths in the kingdom. The scheme is to cost over £B,OOO, There are welcome signs of a quickened public conscience on the question of the health of the town. During the week beginning April 30th the Health Committee co-operated with the Guild of Help in promoting the observance of " Health Week" one of the principal features, being a visit by the Mayor and Corporation to some of the worst slum areas of tl..e town. The feeling has long been gaining ground that to meet the ever-growing demands imposed on municipalities in the adminis- tration of national concerns, the ratepayers are entitled to substantial relief from imperial sources. A resolution voicing this opinion was passed by the Town Council on the proposition of Councillor Pemberton. That eternal topic —the smoke nuisance —has been considerably to the fore during the year, thanks largely to a movement initiated in the columns of the " Examiner." It cannot, however, ,be claimed that there has been real improve- ment in the methods of dealing with the nuisance. The Council has had under consideration the necessity for an enlargement of the Town Hall so as to provide adequate Council Chamber accommoda- tion. The Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committue went so far as to approve instructions to architects and recommend the Council to advertise for tenders but so I strong was the opposition to any tinkering measure which might iuterfere with the harmony of the architecture that the resolution was referred back and the scheme at present stands in abeyance' The municipal elections in ',November resulted in striking Liberal gains in Whitecross and St John'a Wards, while the Tories captured a seat in Fairfield Ward. For the third year it succession, Councillor Dr Joseph, whose indefatigable labours hive gained the praise of all parties, was elected to the Mayoral chair and, in accepting the office, he took occasion strongly to con- demn the increasing extent to which party politics intervene in municipal contests. Be also put in a strong plea for determined efforts to deal with the slum problem, and expressed regret that Warrington had not been the pioneer in the provision of cheap municipal dwellings for the working classes.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATIONAL.

The Education Committee is still engaged in an effort to provide all parts of the town with adequate and up-to-date school accommodation. Naturally the work of such an authority cannot be carried out on progressive lines without a continual straining of financial resources, and the education rate levied in llarch amounted to is 91(.1 in the X.—an increase of 3d. As, at that time, four new schools were in immedi ite contemplation, the prospect for the rate- payer is not a cheering one. The new Evelyn-street Council school, which provides accommodation for 1,260 children, was opened on August 15th by Councillor Henry Roberts (chairman of the Education Committee In some quarters it s contended that the provision made by this school is extravagant and has had the effect of depleting the attendances at other. schools in the town, but the Education Committee maintain that they have only given due attention to the requilements of a very populous neighbourhood. By voluntary effort, St. Benedict's school, Orford-lane, has been rm derni zed and extended at a cost of .f.. 2,500. On several occasions the Educa- tion Committee has been invited by the local members of the teaching profession to restrict competition for the headships of Council schools to teachers already in the employ of the authority. This principle was again warmly discussed in connection with the appointment of the headmaster of the Evelyn-street school, but the committee declined to give their own teachers a preference and the position, on being thrown open to free competition, was secured by an outsider. The Committee has had a dispute with the architectural profession on the terms of com- petition for the des,gning of Council schools, and the decision not to appoint a qualified architect as an assessor of the plans resulted in the withdrawal from competition of local members of the R.1.8.A. New schools in Oakwood-avenue, Parr-street and Oliver-street will shortly be commenced and discus- sion has been aroused by the decision of the committee to ask the firm of architects who were successful in the competition for the first-named school to prepare plans Lir the other two, without throwing the work open by advertisement. The committee defend their procedure on the grounds of economy and efficiency. Councillor Henry Roberts, whose keen interest in all edgcational affairs is well known, has been re-elected to the chairmanship of the authority and is most assiduous in his attention to the duties. BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The past year has been notable for the number of lively discussions arising at the Board meetings, and, apart from debates on policy, thef e have been several events of unusual interest. An important building scheme has been carried through in the shape of a new dining ball to replace the unsatisfactory arrangements previously existing. The kitchen accommodation has been entirely over-hauled and up-to-date cooking appliances installed. The Board has at present under consideration schemes for the erection of a new block of buildings for the accommodation of imbecile and epileptic patients. The urgent need for this improvement is recognized by everyone who is acquainted with the present lamentably deficient means of dealing with such cases, but the Board is at present hesitating between two alternative schemes, the less cxpensive of which will cost about £.12,000. The Board will also sooner or later have to face the question of providing 11'3W Union offices. A Proposal o rent premises in King-street was submitted by the Building Committee but eventually abandoned as unsuitable. A proposition by the Rev A M Mitchell that in all the Board's records the word "poor " should be substituted for "pauper " gave rise to an interesting discussion but the resolution was lost, although the Clerk explained that the word " pauper " was only used in statutory forms. Acting on medical advice, Mr J F Awbery resigned his position as master of the workhouse in May and Mrs Awbery relinquished the post of matron. They have been succeeded by Mr and Mrs W J Brown, who came from the Blything Union. Owing to the death of his wife, Mr Arthur Wright vacated the °Moo of superintendent of the Padgate Cottage Homes. The vacant positions of superintendent and matron have been filled by the appointment of Mr and Mrs C E Lawrance. The Board took a forward more in June by granting permission to the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union to give a scientific temperance lecture to the children of the Cottage Homes—a little concession which had previ- ously been resisted with unaccountable obstinacy. On the question of granting extra allowances to recipients of outdoor relief during the Christmas season, the Board still maintains an unsympathetic and parsi- monious attitude, the circumstances under which the proposal was shelved tnis year being a matter of recent history.

MISCELLANEOUS. A social event which aroused a remarkable degree of interest was the marriage of the Mayoress of Warrington (Miss Joseph) to Mr Arnold Nield, which took place at Holy Trinity Church on January 30th. There was a crowded congregation to w tams the

ceremony, and the• Corporation and officials recognized the happy event by presenting the Mayoress with a silver tea and coffee service and a massive solid silver table lamp. Mr William Silcock, chief cashier at Parr's Bank,

retired at the end of February after 48 years service and was the recipient of a solid silver rose bowl from his colleagues at the bank. The Citizens' Guild of Help continues to fulfil a useful purpose in local social life. During the year endeavours have been made to focus public attention on special questions, such as "Poverty and citizen- ship" and the sanatorium clauses of the Insurance Act by holding conferences of those particularly interested. The Bishop of Liverpool presided at the annual meeting of the League and cordially commended its work.

A tragedy marred the Warrington Sailing Club's Easter boat race on April 611, one of the competing yachts capsizing and two of the occupants being drowned.

H .nour was done to the memory of a !peat Warrington authoress on April 18th, when a tablet erected by the Warrington Society to mark-the site of the house in Dial-street where Mrs Barbauld lived from 1758 to 1774 was unveiled by Mrs Ridgway. One of the most disastrous fires which has occurred in Warrington for many years took place on April 25th when the large commercial warehouse of Messrs Gartons, Ltd., the world-famous firm of seed specialists, was gutted. The damage WSB estimated at between £60,000 and £70,000.

Con,iderable'interest was'aroused by the passage up th«+ Manchester Ship Canal on May 24th of the fine cruiser " Bristol " and four destroyers, en route for Manchester.

Wart ington received its first visit from an airman on August 20th when Mr. B. C. Hacks '' flew " from Southport and alighted in Victoria Park where be gave a thrilling exhibition flight. The whole district was shocked by the news of the terrible railway disaster whici took place on the London and North-Western Railway at Ditton on September 17th, fifteen persons being killed and about fitly injured. Two local passengers—Mr and Mrs Frank E Brown, of Sephton Park, Liverpool. members of well known Warrington families—had a miraculous escape.

A fire which did damage to the extent of £3,000 broke out at the works of Messrs H D Pochin and Co., chemical maimfacturers,Bank Quay,on November 28th. Another outbreak cccurred on December 511), in the wire netting stores at the Whitecross Company's works, the damage amounting to -5,2,500.

The past year has been one of great importance for Lymm. The Urban District Council decided to avail itself of Sir William Lever's offer to sell the water works, the far-famed lake and 14 acres of land for £20,250 and a Bill is being promoted in Parliament to enable that to be done. In the parishes of Grappenhall, Latchford Without, and Thelwall,.the chief topic of interest has been the big sewerage scheme, sanctioned by the Runcorn Rural District Council, which is to cost £18,500. strong opposition from the three parishes concerne,l found expression at the Local Government Board inquiry which took place on Docember 4th and 10th

SPORT.

The record of the Warrington Football Club for the season 1911-12 was the worst since the formation of the Northern Union in 1895-6. For the first time the percentage of League points fell below the 50 per cent, mark and the team finished up in a very lowly position in the League table. In the Cup-ties Wallington did something to justify their reputation as cup-fighters by twice drawing on their opponents' territory and winning at home. So far as the present season has progressed, Warrington have cut a poor figure and their supporters have been bitterly disappointed on many occassions. The War- rington Cricket Club had a very unsatisfactory season, for against five matches won,seven were lost:and seven drawn. The Warrington and District League compe- tition resulted in a triumph for the Sankey eleven. Bowling was the more popular summer sport, and the Warrington Works Bowling Association had a record season. In the final Fletchers' Moulders won the Finlayson cup, defeating Howley tannery by a majority of 18. In the Warrington and District League Rylands' Recreation were the champions, the Liberal Club winning the consolation competition. Among Liberal trundlers a tourney for the various ward committees, for a challenge cup presented by Mr Peter Peacock, proved a huge success. The winners were St Austin's B who, in the final, accounted for Town Hall Aby a majority of 36. Cross-country running has many devotees in Warrington and the local con- tingent did uncommonly well in the 1911-12 season. The Warrington teams secured the second place in the National and Northern championship races. H Holbrook and C Vose were third and fourth respec- tively in the individual placings in the National and Holbrook was fourth in the /Northern. TRADE.

Notwithstanding the serious dislocation of indust7 caused by the coal strike, the past year has, on the whule, been one of great proaperity for Warrington's many industries. The town has participated to the full in the great trade boom and Mr F A Frost, the president of the Chamber of Commerce, recently declared that the trade of the town was never better than to-day.

RELIGIOUS.

In the religious world the year has not been very uneventful, the various denominations having pursued their usual course undisturbed by much change or conttoversy. Progress is to be recorded in some directions and several important building schemes have been carried out. On January 14th a new porch elected at Christ Church, Latchford, as part of the jubilee celebration, was dedicated. A tablet commemorating the memorable ceremony of the previous October when the old colours of the 2iid Battalion South Lancashire Regiment were deposited in the keeping of the Rector and churchwardens, was plac.d in the Parish Church in Fe4ruary. The Rev. Dr. Mellor entered upon his pastorate at Cairo-street Chapel in March, and has avowed much interest and thought by his sermons on "Religion and Democracy." In April the Rev. Michael Howley left for Waterford after two-and-ahalf years' service as assistant priest at the Church of the Sacred Heart and as Roman Catholic chaplain at the Workhouse. He has been succeeded by the Rev. Father Murphy. The late General Booth, the revered bead of the Salvation Army, visited Warrington on April 28th and addressed two meetings in the Hippodrome. The Rev. John Yonge closed his thirty- six years' pastorate at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Juno 16th. His retirement was made the occasion of two handsome presentations £270 from the congregation of Wycliffe and £55 from other townspeople. The members of the church have just selected the Rev T T James, M.A.,of Sheffield, as their new pastor and he will take up his duties in Febuary. The Rev John Gunnell com- pleted his fiftieth year in the Wesleyan Methodist ministry on September Ist. At the Workingmen's Mission a new vestry erected at a cost of .£6OO, was opened on September 14th by Mrs John Crosfield of Colwyn Bay, to whose generosity the provision of this much-needed extension is entirely due. The Rev R Parry Jones became pastor of the Welsh Calvinistic Church, Crosfield-street, in September. On October 12th a beautiful memorial window was unveiled at Moore Wesleyan Church. The Patten Hall, Hanover-street, which provides a handsome recreative institu- tion for the parish of Holy Trinity, was opened on Nov. oth, by the Hon. Mrs Lumley. The foundation stone of a new vestry at the Warrington Parish Church was laid by the Rev W J Stanton, of Thelwall, on Dec. 12th. The flue new Sunday School erected in connection with Golborne-street Baroist Church, at a cost of S-2,350, was opened on the same date.

THE LATE MR, SEPTIMUS KENYON.

The passing away at Blackpool last week of Mr Septimus Kenyon, removrs one who was until a few years ago an interesting and active figure in the business and public life of Warrington. Although be never aspired to membership of a municipal body, Mr Kenyon took a keen interest in local affairs and was a consistent supporter of the non-trading agita- tion. He was present at all the meetings and occasionally wrote and spoke in the interests of the movea,ent. His great hobby was the writing of verse and many contributions from his pen have appeared in the columns of the " Exam:ner." Only a few months ago he sent us some lines which, we observed at the time, seemed to have the pathetic valedictory note of the swan song. blr Kenyon was very fond of the game of bowls. He was at one time an enthu- siastic member of the Arpley Cricket and Bowling Club and occupied the position of president fora short period. He was generally respected in Warrington for his conscientious,out-spokeuness and the disinterested- ness of his motives, and the news of his death was received with deep regret.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MR. S. KENYON.

Mr Kenyon, who was 73 years of age, was born at Mount Plessant about ten miles from Preston and half a mile from Brock Bottoms, in the beautiful valley of the river Brock. His father and mother lived in the house where he was horn for more than fifty years. Mr Kenyon received his education at the Whitechapel school, about two miles from his home, and at the age of fifteen he was apprentieed to a chemist and druggist in Preston, a few yards from where the Town Hall and Library now stand. Going to Lancaster to complete his apprenticeship he made the acquaintance of the Rev. Henry Solly who afterwards became well known for his interest in social science, in which Lord Shaftesbury took an ever-memorable part. Mr Kenyon waited upon Mr Solly with reference to the formation of a Mutual Improvement and Debating Class which proved a great success and was the pioneer of many similar organizations in other parts of the country. Leaving Lancaster at the age of twenty, Mr Kenyon took up a situation in a Midland town. He was wont to describe the place as "the hardest he had ever bad." Along with another assistant, he opened the shop at seven o'clock in the morning and did not close until 10 o'clock at night, and at midnight on Saturdays. They had also to stay in all dayon alteri.ate Sundays Living on the premises,they were never away from there except on their Sunday out. The continuous standing had a bad effect on Mr Kenyon's phrique and since the age of twenty-one he had borne what he described as "the indelible marks of that slavery." Oa leaving the black country, Mr Kenyon went to Messrs Bowers Bros, of Chester, and bund the change a happy one. He was afterwards manager of a business in Whitehayen and finally a dispenser at the Bolton Infirmary. The Rev Canon Powell, in a striking testimonial, said : "Mr Kenyon was considered an excellent dispenser and performed his duties with amazing punctuality and diligence." Whilst at Bolton Mr Kenyon became friendly with the late Mr James Dodds and their happy relations continued throughout the life-time of Mr Dodds for whose sterling work in the cause of municipal purity Mr Kenyon had the highest admiration. Forty-three years ago Mr Kenyon was induced to leave Bolton and to take a chemist's and druggist's business in Warrington. Tabs did not realize his expectations and therefore he was obliged to devise something which would induce manufacturers tn establish a con- nection with him. He invented dipping-wire varnish but it was a long time before he could get people to use it. Mr Edwin Woods. of the Longford Wire Co., Ltd., was one of the first to give it a thorough trial and he found the results so satisfactory that other firms quickly followed suit. A firm in Germany also interested itself in the process but Mr Kenyon ultimately received a letter stating that the duty on the varnish was so great that they could not advantageously make use of it. Tbe passing of the McKinley tariff also did great injuty to Mr Kenyon's varnish trade. About six years ago Mr Kenyon retired from business and went to live at Ashton-on-Ribble, subsequently removing to Blackpool. His interest in the old town continued to the last and his views on various topics found expression in letters to the " Examiner." He also wrote the story of his life for this journal. Mr Kenyon's standpoint on public wrk is hest summed up in his own words: "I believe that the public welfare is above all other things ever sacred and that disinterestedness in public life is a very sacred duty, for it is by far the most important factor in promoting the common weal, in making the world happier and working out the purposes of God."

THE FUNERAL. • The funeral took place very quietly as St. Thomas's Churchyard, Stockton Heath, On Monday afternoon, the service being conducted by the Vic tr, the Rev. J. Collins. The mourners were Mr. R. Eastwood, Black- burn ; Mr. R. W. Robinson, Blackpool ; Mr. and Mrs Carter, Warrington ; and a few friends of the deceased gentleman. Wreaths and other floral tributes were received from the relatives and friends.

SOMERSET HOUSE AND ITS WORK.

[COPYRIGHT.]

A frightful thing is the huge palace in the Strand which is called Somerset House. Massive and heavy piece of architecture of which the hollows are inked, the porticoes blackened with soot, where, in the cavity of the empty court. is a sham fountain without water, pools of water on the pavement, and long rows of closed windows—what can they,possibly do in these catacombs?" . . . So M. Taine •on a damp October day in the middle of- the last century. His depressibn was pardonable. .The present writer has experienced Somerset House on many damp days, and there are few places where trouser legs get wetter in a driving rain than they do when crossing its big auadrangular court. The pools of water on the pavement are a trifle to those in the courtyard, and by the time one has scuttered through its puddles and reached the Probate Registry ,a tinge of gloom is natural enough. There is one particular official in a dark room in the Inland Revenue department, who affects a red tie and a super- cilious air whom it is positively dangerous to see on such a day. . . . However, M. Taine was mercifully ignorant of the contents of the catacombs, and had lie seen Somerset House on a sunny morning in early spring, when the hawthorn on the right of the entrance from the Strand is a fresh and fascinating green, when the darkened, sooty patches throw -up fly.? pleasant whitentss of the facades against a clear blue sky, and the pigeons walk about with a self-satisfied and portly air, he would nu doubt have been more cheerful. ITS VICISSITUDES.

The building was erected by Sir William Chambers in 1776, and has been described as the greatest architectural work of the reign of George the 7.'hird, which may or may not be high praise. Its river front, at all events, is an imposing line of buildings at all times and in all weathers, reminiscent, in sunshine, of Italy; wrapped in soft greyness, one of the beautiful things in London. !rs roofs have sheltered a succession of widely- differing tenants. The Royal Academy, the Naval Museum, the Royal Society. the Society-of Antiquaries, • the Tax Office, the Salt Office, the Hawkers and Pedlars, the Lottery—a great procession of them, some stilliflourishing, some pale ghosts of the past: To-day Somerset House is essentially a seeious and business-like place. It is crowded with lawyers studying wills and registering com- panies in the place of ladies studying water colours and walking on the terrace as they did in former days. It is a vastly dull and a vastly interesting place. It contains the records of all our little lives—our births and marriages, and wills and deaths, and the taxes that fol- lowed after us, all "ranged there with careful art" in big folios by little clerks.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
_SOME FAMOUS. WILLS.

For one shilling you may inspect any original will made since 1483, and the copies go back a century earlier still. A period of five minutes is sufficient as a rule to enable the will to be produced, and when one comes to think of the enormous number of wills that are stored here, on their miles of shelves in those catacombs that puzzled M. Paine so much, the system of storage and indexing that is employed must really be an amazingly good one. With proper credentials a visitor may see the original wills of Shakespeare (in an air-tight frame of oak and glass), Vandyck, Lord Nelson, Dr. Johnson (who bequeathed the bulk of his property to a negro man-servant), Edmund Burke, William Pitt, Sir Isaac Newton, John Milton, the great Duke of Wellington, and many other famous and infamous •persons. Short particulars, such as the name and address of the testator and of his executors, the amount at which the estate was sworn and the date of probate (for the will itself is supple- mented with these particulars); are all that one is allowed to copy free of charge, and the will had to be read under the eye of a watchful official. A complete copy can be obtained, how- ever, on. payment of a reasonable fee, is. per, folio of 72 words, I believe.

If you have notiiing to do and a few shillings to spell& this is really quite a fascinating place —occasionally. The habit of looking up wills grows on one, just as looking peopl.e up in "Who's Who" does. And life—sorne.times sad, sometimes pathetic, sometimes a little mad. often quite funny—can be inspected here at first-hand, in addition to all these memorials and records of the dead. Strange old Miss Plites hanntqlle place and search endless wills with inexhaustible interest, and disappointed relatives insist on seeing Aunt Jane's original will in order to ascertain beyond all doubt that the lawyer isn't telling lies, and that Aunt Jane really has had the rank ingratitude to leave them out.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MODERN INQUISITION.

The new west wing of Somerset House, which faces Wellington-st., is occupied by the Board of Inland Revenue and also contains the Joint Stock Companies Registry, and the department 6 concerned with the stamping of all documents liable to duty. The Board Of Inland Revenue has many duties to perform and to collect, chief among them the Excise Duty, the Income Tax, the Inhabited House Duty, and the lAnd Tax. The early history of the Excise Duty 11; full of thrilling stories of the smuggler, but the "bond- ing" system and modern methods of adminis- tration have, made his calling too precarious to be popular, and to-day he sills adulterated ,butter and wears a hard felt hat. The Income Tax is easily first in pcipular ab- horrence. Like the rain, it falls on the just and the unjust alike, and it is only very small fishes that escape through the meshes e.ff its net. The equitable principle of differentiation between earned and unearned income was carried ti step further by the Finance Act of 1207. However unpopular and di ...gusting the tax may seem, it is based on a broad bedrock of commonsense justice and will remain for all time, like the .Tews, a necessary evil. Land Tax, in its preoent form, is an anomalous anachronism which ought to be abolished and replaced by a comprehensive scheme, it is gradually being redeemed on terms of 20 'earl' purchase, and only some £700.000 now remains, a statutory rent charge on certain lands.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE " GUINEA SLUMP."

The stamp departments located in the new west wing are thronged at alrhours of the day with lawyer& clerks, who wait in tedious files to present documents of every variety under the sun to the casual inspection of bored-lookinq officials whose task is to assess the duty payable upon the instrument. In simple cases the duty is asseswd at'once, and when paid the appropriate stamp is impressed, but in cases of difficulty the document Li left.with a copy for adjudicatioor by the official Solicitor of Inland Revenue. In 1902 the total revenue from stamp duties was well over nine millions, and it is probably consider- ably more to-day. The ordinary layman has no conception of the wide scope .of the Stampl*cts, nor of the strictness with which they are con- strued, until lie may chance to become involved in litigation necessitating the production in evi- dence of some unstamped document upon which hie case rests. He then not infrequently dis- covers to his disgust that it should have been tamped with a certain duty, and that his case I must fail unless the duty, and a heavy trnalty, je paid forthwith.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
DOLEFUL DUTIES.

The old west wing of Somerset House is the headquarters of tie Estate Duty ,Departinent, which gathers in tho national honey from the Death Duties. Ttile way in which' these duties are traced and exacted is quite extraordinary. Some obscure person dies in some obscure village, and his obscure, descendants .enter into his ()b- -emire possessions, and time passes and the de- ceased is forgotten—by everyone except some clerk at Somerset House. The whole facts are noted there, and sooner or later the demand for duty Milt's. It is never forgotten or overlooked. Every detail is examined, and every penny of tax is apparently the sUbiect of much care. The registration of births, marriages. and deaths is carried on in the Strand front of the buildings, and. that no important crisis in our dyes may pass here unrecorded, the interlocutory proceedings in, divorce take place in the east wing. 'Unlike the sundial, Somerset House numbers all hours sne, both sunny and dark.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW I BEGAN.

[BY GEORGE EDGAR.] Mr George Edgar, author of "The Blue Bird's Eye," "Swift Nick" &c, who is a Warringtonian and began his journalistic career on the staff of the "Examiner, eontributes the following autobiographical notes to" T P's Weekly" : Actually, I began in a Lancashire town by being born. Imu not a Manchester man, and I do not think grace only abounds about the Irwell. Still I, am sufficiently proud of my c,.unty to cling to a belief that if one must begin at all, one might as well get the business over by being born in Lancashire. The town from which I came lies midway between Man - cheater and Liverpool, on the Mersey, and is given up wholly to a variety of manufacturing interests. My home looked down a narrow street leading to a forge. The chimneys belched black smoke by day and furnaces glowered angrily at night. Processions of work-soiled mechanics, puddlere, boiler-makers and labourers, coming and going to meals, divided the days into equal parts. I could tell the time in the early morning from the noise made by the knocker-up, or by the steady ring of metal-shod clogs on the pave- ments. The last heralded the passing of the factory workers before six, huddled in their shawls, to the near -by mills.

CLUB TALK. One of my earlisst recollections is of being carried on the shoulders of a giant with blackened face, through a rolling mill. Another is of the fascination exercised over my four-year-old mind by the work of riveters. I liked most of all, when the bout was darket,ing, to see the men heat the bolts and hammer the white-hot metal home. I did not know they were working, but believed rather they were giving a fire- work display. I saw death in those early years, for the first time. A bright merry-faced man, who never failed to give me greeting as he went backward and forward to his work, was carried past our house one fotenoon, on a rude stretcher. I never saw him again. I knew a lot of simple-minded, kindly intentioned, good-hearted folk in those days. Sometimes, sitting in my club, I hear kindly-intentioned and eves good-hearted folk who ate much better fed and not nearly so simple- minded, talking of the working people I knew as of so many mad dogs. One sleek old Colonel, retired, denounced them as lazy, shiftless, discontented rascals and spoke of a whiff of grape shot as a remedy. He spoke a year ago, when Labour looked like asking for its own. I smiled—the gallant Colonel did not understand.

SEEING AND LEARNING THINGS. That is where I began—within sight of a forge and within sound cl a steam hammer. Before I was aged ten, I knew many things first hand that few men ever learn—of good repute and ill. I had much freedom as a boy, and used it. They did not give object lessons in the schools of my day, but what I missed in that direction I gained by seeing many very real things—how iron was melted, cast or rolled; how glass was blown and cut; how wire was drawn; how bricks were baked; how cotton turned to fabric in the loom; how velvet was made by hand cutters, who sang • to relieve the monotony cf their work. lam bound to add that by following my nose, I acquired familiarity with many things I ought not to have known as a tender child—pigeon flying, dog racing, prize fighting, shady forms of professional athletics, some methods of poaching, a variety of bad language, and the kind of musical evening at which the vocalist gets an excellent effect from a concertina accompaniment. I like to remember my public school days. The knowledge I gleaned was not academic but it was extensive and peculiar.

TIMEKEEPING AND ROMANCE. I had one or two preliminary adventures as a worker before I found my place in lire. I was in a lawyer's cffice. There I kept the stamps, copied the letters, and read" Sketches by Boz," surreptitiously,l shall never forget the joy Dickens gave me as I read him in stolen time. I was next in an office where I checked the workers as they came in and out, and rendered an accurate account of their movements. I did not give such an accurate sccount of my own time- keeping, hut-- well, that is the priviiegs of the ruling classes. I started to write in those days. The time lingered heavily on my hands, for I do not think I had two hours' work to eo each day, and I was on duty about nine hours. I used to begin long tragic romances, very twiving in their intensity, and strangely fascinating. Not one of them ever reached completion. A modern system sokeelsetribieses-sesssela--Iteve atom 16' wicked leakage in the foolscap account of that office. My commercial experiences ended with a brief, in- glorious career as a junior railway clerk. JOURNALIST AT SIXTEEN. Journalism claimed me by sheer accident SR a boy of sixteen years. I did the ordinary work. I do not suppose one gets the nicer graces of a literary style by performing the rough and tumble tasks in journalism. But as a reporter, I did see real life, and a lot of it, and having seen it, I was expected to say what I had seen, with one eye on the clock and an ear open for a growling news editor. I cffer no continent on the relative merits of learning to write. Journalism was my way. I had to say what I saw quickly, and then go out and see something else. Those many years I spent in journalism seem to rue now, looking back, to have been crowded with much interest. I lied no social or domestic life, and scarcely lived apart from newspapers and newspaper making. I was wedded to the newspaper and had hot tinae to be self conscious, or even seif-otitiesi. sometimes in , train, getting home late at night, or waiting about a railway station for an awkward connection,• I used to question myself as to where I was going on my human pilgrimage. I had- a hankering after the writer's place as distinct from that of the stormy petrel of social life-the journalist. The newspaper habit was in my blood, and willy nilly I had to keep on. I just grew with the flight of time, out of one phase of journalism into another, and almost unconsciously. A constitutionally lazy man, I responded to opportunity at the rasp of the spur of necessity. REGULAR HOURS.

t

And then came a complete change. I was in London, and out of the routine of staff journalism, calling me here, there and everywhere es a spectator. For the first time in my life I kept regular hours. I found the smoothness of my existence irksome after years of nomadic irregularities. I missed the colour,'________ the movement, the interest of going about, the gossip - of the shop, the scent and sound of newspaper produc- tion. Without knowing how or why, and through DO - - act of initiative on my part, I was living a relativel9 .s quiet life in Chelsea. I was editing a monthly publi- cation, writing much for another, and contributing 's articles, abort stories and character sketches for many more periodicals. I became the journalistic free man. Euphoniously the world calls him a "free lance." As a matter of fact, the unattached man only changes the form of his thraldom. He becomes the slave of his own ideas instead of being the slave' of the passing event. The change had some COMpeosations, but the hours are not quite so crowded. CLAMOURING FOR THE MOON. That is how I began. I wrote a novel because a journalistic freebooter has to write a novel. It is se much a part of the game as the music of the horn is part of coaching. The critics were kind enough W The Blue Bird's-Eye" was a readable novel, Naturally, I wrote another. So far as I can see, the world will have 0 bear with mess a novelist for a while to come as some punishment for encouraging me. If the people do not mind it—why should I 7 ~ I have long become used to the quieter life, and I love to sit and tell stories. I shall never convince , myself that making puppet figures move across B. . mimic stage is so fascinating a business in life ss mixing amongst crowds of men on the tail of the big event. One never is really • contented, so 4 the earlier habit of moving recurs. I fic° a growing ambition sweetens my task. It 'is to make my accidental mission in life, the telling of tales, to take on an, increasing purpose., Shall I confess it I would like to make the stories I write as real a 5 some of the people I have met—so real that I shell not be able to tell the difference between them an life itself. If I ever succeed I may partially lull the old craving to be out and about, seeing and bearing and trying to understand: I know I set myself a,l; impossible task.. On the other hand, when a cl?.1;c!: clamours for the moon it is demanding the impostaWL" Aspiration brings its own pleasures, and, on We whole, I find it better to cry for the moon than t? ~,De content with the easily achieved and ever familiar soap bubble of childhood.

Williem Ronayne, of /12, Green-street, was charged at St. Helens on Mondiy with acting as a marine store dealer without a licence. Evidence was given that the accused bought iron at Haydock and Burtonwood, and that since Septen.ber he had sold large quantities of marine stores to Samuel Hindley, of Waterloo-street. On November 8 accused bought a la-ge stock of scrap wire from Messrs W Glover and Co, Liverpool-road. Evidence was also given that he sold large quantities of 'iron to Mersin, Monks Hall, & o. Aroused said he was all his lifetime in the koking business, and cirried on business for forty sous at Newton-le-Willows without complaint. He hid never kept a marine-storo. The Examiner, Saturday, December 28, 1912.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

POSSIBLE PREMATURE BURIAL.

Of course Mr. James R Williamson took advantage of a melancholy local occurrence to get a word in for the cause he s keenly represents; he Lever allows such an opportunity logo by unimproved. And how he does make one's flesh creep! still, one can bear him out to some extent. Not long ago the eerie expel i- once of two vrorklicuse inmates was described to the mem' ers of the Tullatnore Board of Guardians. A tramp had been detailed for undertaker's duty at the mortuary in which lay a dead body. The man and another inmate went to the deadhouse to place the body in a shell when they were arres'ecl by sounds as of one singing "Oh, oh, Antonio . . . left me on my olio," and they leaned against the doorpost and enjoyed the music. Then deciding. that some of the inmates of the workhouse were indulging in a celebra- tion somewhere in the building they entered the mortuary to complete their task. To their arra zement they Lund the EUi po ed dead man comfortably seated on a shelf in a corner chanting the story ef Antonio's desettion with infinite enjoyment. With a wild yell the amateur undertakers dropped their shell and ran for the woikh use, followed at top speed by what they conceived t be the shade of the dead man. The mus'eal inmate Las been discharged f torn the work- house and is now in reedit of an old-ago pension. It is all vely funny, no doubt; but suppose the metry old chap had not so coin tecovered from his cataleptic condition? PRECAUTIONS.

S .me well-le-lo per pie take care to previde against this sort of thini in their wille.There have been several such instances of late. Let me reeal two or three.
