Mrs Louis Kizabeth fl aris, of R.-, lbarn Pri my, Kilburn, N W., who died in Fe btu try last, stated : "I most. sincerely desire that there shall I e no peseihility of may being buried alive or rut underground alive or in a trate', and that there shall be the 'nest undoubted pro f of death by ore or more fully-quallified medical men (ter personally seeing my body " Mr Ad .Iphus lirn, solicitor, of 21, Mincing-lane, E.O , who left estate of the gross value of .C. 7,540 directed th t te ben definite signs of decomposition Lad set in, r r other means bad been taken to in .ke certain that Me was extinct, his body should be cremated.

TERRIBLE. It must at least be admitted that our burial laws are in an unsatisfactory state, and that the demands as to this petiodically made by the Ass,tciation of Prevention of Premature Burial are reasonab'e enough. At their Lot annual meeting, the Chairman, Mr Gt orge Greenwood, MP., speaking of the urgency Ef ' legislation, said that if they had a separate Parl amrnt for England some measure dealing with the subject might be carrit d. The House of Commons was to busily engaged on other matters. The law at present, he said, offered evety facility for the conceal- ment of clime. In 1911 tl ere had be en approximately 6 666 carer in which no certificate of tie cause of death had lyon given by either medical man or coroner ant, taking this as the lowest figure. he asked if Dia state of Of Airs was rot scandal, us One of the speakers related the story of a woman who at the age of 18, had lain in a public mortuary for three days apparently dead, but who tubseque .fly lived to be 70 years cf age. Mr B R Balfour, vi ho said the Bill was more urgently needed in Ireland than in Eng'and, told of a man buried in a trance Muffled sounds were heard from the grave, the coffin was opened, and the supposed corpse was able to walk home In Drogheda a lady was buried in a vault, and a maidservant, knowing that she wore a va'tt tble ring, went to the vault at night and uttempted to cut off the finger The lady was aroused by the pain, and the maid fled panic-stricken. In this case also the supposed corpse recovered. Meanwhile, let me ask why More of those who fear burial %%bile in a state of suspended animation do not arrange for crem tion of !heir remains? The law as applied to cremati in is much more strict, careful preliminaty Examination being insisted upon. A CHRISTMAS GHOST. "

This being the season when churchyards are supposed to yawn aid graves to give up their dead, a little adventure which an acquaintance cf mine has just had may be mentioned here as a seasonable i.ern. He lives cut in the cow try, and on his way out and home has the choice of two wsys —the ordinary high- road and a foo'path through the fiel Is. Returni...g late on the occasion in gm stbn, he chose the latter as a sliort cut. When within abint half-a-mile of his destination, he was brr right to a stand by the eight f a light object fixed motionless right by the path alorg which he would have to pass lie looked hard at the strange figure, and then, cedf. s edly scared, hastened back to the pcint from which he had diverged and completed his j ,urney by the highway. Next morning be went down to see what had frightened him, and found to his nisgust that it was only a noticeboard which had been erected while be was ill on the previous day, and which had been painted white ! THE CHRISTMAS TREE. Considering some of the local doings in which it figures, I wondered if many of the younger partici- pants knew what gave rise to the Christmas tree— until " Uncle Caleb" told them about it. It seems to have been popularised in this country by the late Prince Consort, and long prior to that the custom was familiar in Germany Asa matter of fact, it is another of these which Christianity has derived from heathen sources. The Bacchanal an celtbrations of the Romans included a tree in all respects similar to he Christmas •rees that have figured in our local festivities, being specially decorated. CHRISTMAS BOXES. It is not unlikely that by this time the worried householder will hardly be in the mood'to peruse a paragraph under this heading ; but if he has patience to read on he will no cloubt be ready to agree that this very sec ular cnstom is of heathen crigin. It comes from an ancient Roman goddess, and, like others referried to in this colurrn last week was adopted by the early Christian Church. Ships carried certain boxes which were.. under the protection of a saint, and all were expected to " put something in the priest's box until the ship returns." The latter was known as the Christ's Mass Box, and flit fact that employers allowed money to their servants for this purpose seems to have led to the habit whereby nowadays whole classes of men as well as boys expect us to distribute largesse among them at this season. It is indeed a time-honoured institution, for in this country a regulation was issued to forbi.! it as long ago as 1419, declaring it "not becoming to propriety to beg like paupers "on the feast of Our Lord's nativity." So far as our own diArict is ooncerned,the contribu'ions most usefully and ungrudgingly made this week are those which brcught a little I:pightness into the lives of the poor. THE FIRiT PANTO. The first pantomine properly so called was produced in 1724 by John Rich at the;Lincoln's Inn Fields I heatre. It was a comic version of the German Legend of Dr. Faustus, and was entitled "The Necromancer." It was understood that Rich's attention had been drawn to the subject years before that by the success of some performing dolls broaght from Germany which appeared in " Harlequin executed," Rich himself playing Harlequin to the great admiration of patrons. What would these have thought of the elaborate productions which many Warnngtonians will witness during the pantomime season upon which we are just entering? CURIOUS CHRISTMAS CUSTOMS. I read of many curious customs and tenures con- nected with Christmas-tide. I may add the following, as it relates to Cheshire : --In the Harleian MSS. it is mentioned that the manor of Hawarden was formerly , held of the King in capite by Robert de Montalt, Earl of Arundel, by being Steward of the County of Chester, viz., by the service of setting down the first dish before the Earl of Chester at Chester on Christmas Day. Welcome as the return of Christmas might he under such circumstances to the steward of Cheshire it was! not without its drawbacks as concerned some other Cheshire men, for as related by a contributor,to " Notes and Queries," according to an old custom in the county the farm servants engaged themselves to their employers from New Year's Eve to Christmas Day, when for six or seven days during the Christmas week they left their masters and mistresses to shift for themselves, while they (the servants) resortad to the towns to spend their holidays, hundreds of farm servants, male and female, dressed in their gayest

attire, parading the streets of Chester and other towns in the county to the advantage of the tavern-keepers and small traders— a survival of the ancient Roman Safrunalia previous'y referred to in which the relations of master and servant were reversed, and which still prevails in some parts of the shire.

A FEW LOCAL CONUNDRUMS. What kinclipf face should every auctioneer advertiz- ing in this Mar cultivate ?—A for-bidding one. When is a certain well-known Warrington gardener like a man whose affairs are on the verge of tack and ruin? - When he is going to pot. Why might Warrington's Mayor be expected to be chilly rather than genial ? Because he is just-ice. What is it that we cannot a ell do without and yet we always turn it out ? Gas. W hat is that we could spare unite well, and yet that cur public representatives deem indispensable ? Same thing, with a difference—gas. What is the best way to keep the water out cf cne's house?--Don't pay the water rate. What bus has found room for a great many more people than all others put together, tram cars, &c., included? —Colombus.

Why is." An Old Journglist" like a chicken? Because he has to scrbtch for a livi, g. Why is a certain popu!ar Wariington ministrr like a shutter on the railway? - Because he does a lot of coupling. Which of the clergy in this district sh uld be best able to teach the ycung idea how to shoot ? —The Cancn.

In what does an rid local schoolmaster who shall be nameless, resemble a disused gtiodstone ?—Be has spEnt his years in sharpening cther people's blades, and may be said to have worn himself out in the process. Why shculd Warrington's District Nurses be less liable than the rest of us to upset on the ocean ? They are more used to sea sickness. .1\ hat contradiction have they cccasionally to erconnter ?—An impatient ptiei t, When is a member of our Brrough police Ftirca like a watch that io not keepi g time? —W hen he is off his beat.

When is cur leading local auditor like a venomous reptile? —When casting up accounts. lie is an adder. When is each of our W arriogton butchers a thorough thief ?—When he steals a knife and cuts away with it.

1 CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

"PEACE."

A Tale of Christmas for the Children. " HOWE."

It was Christmas Eve and Mrs Overton aid her two children, were sitting before a roaring fire in the dining res en w'en the straMs of a band outside floated through the air. " Oh mother ! listen to the band, does it not sound nice? It makes me feel quite happy with everyone" " Yes Jack, its cheerful music makes one glad and loving, and after all that should be the spirit of Christmas time." Yes mother," said little Fanny," The sweet music lets us k: ow that Christmas has crme at last, but while we are feeling happy towards those who love us, we cannot be loving towaid our enemies." "No dear, that is a mistaken n aion," said mother, " we must be at peace with all people, even those who hate us." "But moth( r, how can I love Edith when we have guar: elled you know that it was all her fan - . "Hush dear, I expect that it was six of one and half a dozen of the other, and even if she was partly ii the wrong how can you say that she bates you when she has sent 3ou an invitation to her Christmas paity." "This spirit of enmity, dearest, not only hurts father and I, but it also grieves Jesus; see no v, ju t b fore y u g upstairs to bed slip your hat on and run across the road to beg Edith's pardon and thank her for the kind invitation. " No, Mother, I cannot do that, she should not have quarelled with me." " Ah, well, dear, lam sorry you will Lot do as ask; good-night, darling, and when you say ycur prayers ask Cad t make the way clear for you to ask Edith's pardon. Good-night, Jack dear, and after kissing mother the t vo childrea went out of the room. A little later on the children were got ready for Led by " Nurse," who was surprised to bear the following additi in to Fanny's usual prayer: "Oh Jesus, if you will let Santa Claus b:ing me that big d ill's house I have asked him for I will go to Edith's to-morrow and beg her pardon." So reader, you will notice that the true spirit of Christmas was not fully present in this little girl's heart. After the gas had been turned low and with eager expect ttions for the c lining morning the litt'e brother and sister soon fell asleep. Some time after Fanny found herself awake and hearing a small noise coming from the direction of the fire-place, she began to feel afraid,but suddenly it dawned upon her that it must be Santa Cause coming down the chimney and being anxious to s-e this mysterious person, she peeped that way. In a few moments a tiny pair of legs appeared, then a back, and flaally a small held, and out of the file-place jumped a hinny little man whose head did riot come even up to the seat of the ch .ir. Vs as this Santa Claus? Where were his toys? Where was her doll's house? She was just going to jump up an I ask the little matt these enestiois when —something was thrown round her face, her aims and legs were fastened and wl en she tried to scream she was unable to make any wise at all; she next felt herself being lifted up and then —knew no more. When she came to her senses again she saw such a sight. There facing her at the foot of a very large toe at the edge of a wood s t such a lovely fairy dressed in a beautiful white and silky garment—far more pretty than any fairy she had ever seen in her picture books. At ova eide of the fairy were her br.ither Jack, and Edith, the little friend with whom she payed until their quarrel a few weeks back. All round the four of them were rings of ottier fairies and little men like the one she had seen come out of the fire-grate in her bedro, m ; the moon was shining brightly and the snow lying all about them made the place quite light. The next thing that Fanny was 'aware of was that a sweet voice, just like her mother's, was calling her by name. " Fanny," it was the fairy that was speaking, and she wondered what was g ing to be said. Christmas Chatter.

FIREWORKS AT YULETIDE.

It you visited Naples during the days of Christmastide, and were not quite sure of the exact date, you would assuredly say that it was the sth of November. Squibs, crackers, rockets, golden rain, Catherine- wheels, and Roman candles are in evidence everywhere at night, before, on, and after Christmas Day. Every balcony—and Naples is a city of balconies—is crowded with people intent on letting off fireworks, to the discomforture of the passer-by unless he be extremely wary. A stream of fiery matter is not a desirable asset at Christmas.

However, as everybody lets off fireworks at some time or other during the holidays, no one grumbles, and peals of laughter and shouts of joy help to relieve the monotony of the dull roar of the gunpowder. The custom is very old, and shows no signs of being on the wane.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
lIELPING TIIE lIOSPITALS.

The great success of the Christmas hospital seal-stamp in Denmark, Norway, and Sweden is to be attributed partly to the favour of the Post Office Department, which facilitates the sale of hospital seal-stamps, the Post Office officials receiving 10 per cent. from the sale as a return for the extra labour involved. During December every year since 1904, when Postmaster Holboll introduced this system into Denmark, a Yuletide hospital- stamp has been printed for Denmark and for the Danish West Indies. Norway and Sweden now also issue a Yuletide stamp, and accord to the Post Office officials 10 per rent, on the annual sales. In Sweden, one Christmas, £50,000 was the amount realised.

A CIIRISTMAS WISH. This wish for you: that past rough roads unheeded You march ahead - - - •

Undaunted, with the hope of trust begotten To win life's bread; To wear a smile e'en when tears be your portion, With sighs unsaid.

To find fair blooms from last year's brown leaves springing Upon your way; To reap the worth of deeds gone by that left you A. bit more grey, A. bit more strong to live and love with others From day to day.

In fruitful fields may Time think wise to give you A gentle part; With love of home and friends to twine about you, May this year start— Blue skies to. cheer, and peace of God to guide you, 0, faithful heart !

" PLUM PUDDING" WITHOUT PLUMS. What is generally called a plum pudding —the Christmas pudding—never contains , -

plums, if we accept 'the denition of a plum. As everyone knows, the fruits used in Christmas pudding are generally raisins and currants, both of which are dried grapes. There are several puddings in the cookery books containing raisins which are called by various fancy names, but never " plum pudding"; there is also a pudding (reminis- cent of schooldays) containing currants, which is called a currant pudding or by offensive terms, according to the temper of the speaker. We should not expect these pud- dings to be called plum puddings, and they never are; but when the currants and raisins appear in the Christmas pudding the product is called a plum pudding. With no logic it is agreed .to accept the word plum as applicable to raisins or currants when they happen to be in Christmas pudding. If plum jam were asked for and a grape conserve were wilfully substituted, we should expect such tt bargain to be regarded as a misdemeanor within the meaning of the Food and Drugs Act, but whether that should be the case when plum pudding is asked for and a pudding without plums (strictly speaking) is tendered, is not so clear.

CHRISTMAS IN OTHER LANDS.

In English-speaking countries Yuletide festivities are very much alike, but other nations have their distinctive and diverse ways of celebrating Christmas. The prettiest custom in Norway and Scandinavia is the Christmas dinner to the birds. Every gable, or house, or outbuilding is decorated with a sheaf of corn, for birds to have their Yule- tide feast. The poor and the well-to-do alike give to the feathered folk. With the Russian Christmas begins the festive season known as Holy Evenings. These "even- ings " are chiefly devoted to singing frag- ments of old songs that refer to the ancient pag.(tism of the country. In other respects Yuletide in the Czar's dominions is much the same as elsewhere in the North. The house- wives in one part of Germany have a custom of throwing the crumbs from the table round the apple tree roots; in another part, holes are picked in the ice, and food is pushed through as an offering to the Spirits of the Water. In some districts the Yule log is put on the fire with much ceremony. In Sunny Spain great preparations are made on all sides for the great " fiesta," and the luxurious and almost tropical vegetation, and the gay colours of the peasants' cloth- ing, form a delightful setting to every scene. The markets are particularly attrac- tive, but the great feature of the Christman market is the number of " pavos " (turkeys). These are sold alive, and it is curious to notice the women bearing off, as one would a baby, live turkeys in their arms, where- with to make merry at the Christmas " fiesta." Even the poorest peasant will have his " nay° " at Christmas. __Very eay

RING GEORGE AS SANTA. CLAUS.

King George always remembers his tenan- try and servants at Christmas. The former are entertained at a play royally com- manded, and the humbler workers on his• estates given substantial presents of beef, etc. To his personal servants the King gives two dances in the New Year, and as evening dress must be warn these aristocrats of domestic servants by no means resemble the usual kitchen dancers. During the dancing their Majesties are expected to " look in," ' and it is just this personal touch that lifts the function out of the ordinary. The late King was most parti- cular about the comforts of the large staffs employed at his residences, and King George i follows in his father's footsteps n this

respect. But the real Christmas to Ring George is when he is busy depositing the surprise pre- sents by his children's beds. Only when they are asleep does the King enter their rooms, and, as it takes the youngsters a long time to close their eyes owing to the pre- vailing excitement, His Majesty does not have much sleep the night before Christmas. But he is first and foremost a family man, and, as everybody knows, Christmas was made for children, and the greatest enjoy. ment can only be extracted from December 25 when there are youngsters to amuse.

CHRISTIANITY AND CONSCRIPTION, REV. H. DUNNICO'S VIGOROUS SERMON.

At Ks nsington Chapel on Sunday evening, the Ree Berke t Duunico (formerly of Warrington), delivered the anneal peace sermon, the sehject being "Christianity and Conscription, or Ito Curse of Militarism." In the course of a vigorous utterance, Mr Minnie() said that dui ing the nineteeuth century it was calculated that 14,0t0,000 able-bodied men had been sacrificed in war, yet, notwithstanding that enormous sacrifice of men and money, the net result was not more security but loss. Two great obstscles stood in the way of international peace, nanely, self-interest end superstition ; both forces were anti-social, but they were strongly entrenched, and hitherto had withstood all their attacks. Moreove,, during, the past few years an organisation known as the National Service League—worked by ex-Army office:s and subsidised by dukes, lon's, titled aristocrats, and a few industrial magnates—had succ,eeded in making even conscription a live question ; sad a well-known politician had openly avowed not only his conversion to the principle, but Lis di termination to subordinate all other questions to it. The preacher then proceeded to point out that conscription was unnecessary, anti-democratic immoral and un-Cnristian. To assert that patriotism arid loyalty implied that a man must be willing to bear arms and train himself to spill the blood of men against whom be had no quarrel was to prostitute noble words for base and sordid purposes. The risks taken by the Sc Idler {sere not nearly so great as those taken by worters in many brunches of industry. To run a locomotive seventy miles an hour day after day demanded infloitely greater courage than to be one of 100,00 men on a battle-field. But why labour the point ? For thousands of workmen and professional mc n life was one long continuous battle, not in armed ranks where uniform glittered and bugles rang, but battles fought ten of in dart ness, loneliness, and sulitude,and involving anguish of heart and sweat of soul. Moreover, conscription vas immoral, and even the physical advantages claimed by its advoca.es were illusory anti they had no exist- ence in fact. But the preacher's greatest objection to it was based upon the fact that wherever a conscript army existed, it was a menace to honour, honesty, virtue, and purity. The garrison tomes in this country were hot- beds of vire, and under conscription these would be multiplied. But let them look at France and Germany with their brothels at every street-corner,their vice and debauchery, and they would soon discover what the Dreyfus case in France and the harden case In Berlin revealed, that foul lying and moral cowardice were inseparably bound up with conscription. Young men left their homes clan in body and mind to undergo their military training, and they returned sti ei ed in debauchery rni suffering from diseases and d. grading vices. And the whole system seas on-Christian. It was the outcome of mental and moral depravity. It was a wicked device, cunningly conceived by the enemies of the human race. The party or people who sought to initiate it were guilty of a base crime against mrnhood and womanhood. Ninety-five per cent of the peoples of Germany and England did not desire war. lbe thought of it outraged every sense of decency. They had nothing to fight for, and nothing to fight against; but if war was inevitable, let it be a war against those who found it easy to set on fire racial and religious hatreds than to engage in beneficial work for the preservation rather then the destruction of human life.

The Examiner, Saturday, December 28, 1912.

heard of the Bill in the louse of Commons until the autumn Session.

THE ULSTER CAMPAIGN.

In the meantime, however, the anti-Home Rule campaign in Ulster, under the leader- ship of Sir Edward Carson, had been car- ried on with the greatest energy. Eith the battle-cry of "No Surrender" Sir —Edward gathered the Ulster Unionists to his ban- ner, and many prominent leaders of the Unionist Party took part in a remarkable series of demonstrations which culminated on "Ulster Day," Saturday, September 28, when the Ulster covenant was extensively signed. It pledged the signatories to use every possible means to prevent the estab- lishment of an Irish Parliament, and, if it should be established, to refuse to recognise its authority. Nine days later, on October 7, Parliament re-assembled after a recess of two months. A time-table was adopted for the remaining stages of the Bill, and a drastic application of the closure was agreed to, though strenuously resisted by the Oppo- sition. One of the most important of the amendments to the original Bill was one passed on October 31. It was a Govern- ment amendment, providing that the Irish Senate shall be for the first five years a nominated body, after which it will be re- cruited on a system of proportional regis- tration. AN EXCITING SCENE.

The Opposition stubbornly opposed the Bill clause by clause, but with the help of the guillotine progress was made, the Gov- ernment majorities in the divisions being generally in the neighbourhood of a hun- dred. But on November 11 there occurred a very remarkable incident. An amendment was moved by Sir Frederick Banbury pro- viding that the total payment to Ireland from the Imperial Exchequer in any one year should not exceed £2,500,000. There was a brief debate, and on a division being taken the Government were defeated by twenty-one votes. The House was immedi- ately adjourned amid a scene of intense ex- citement, and cries from the Unionists of "Resign! Resign !" On the following day Mr. Asquith gave notice that he would move a resolution rescinding the vote on the Banbury amendment. On the 13th the resolution was moved, and when a motion by Mr. Bonar Law for the adjournment of the debate had been defeated, there followed one of the most extraordinary scenes in the history of Parliament. The Opposition re- fused to hear any further speeches. Minis- ters were pelted with order papers and as- sailed once more with cries of "Resign !" In the excitement a Unionist member seized a copy of an order book and threw it at Mr. Churchill, striking him in the face. (For this incident an apology was afterwards ' made and accepted in the house.) An ad- journment of an hour having failed to cure the trouble, the Speaker adjourned the House on the ground of "grave disorder." THE SPEAKER'S TACT. It seemed highly probable that the dis- order would be renewed on the following day, but all fear of that vanished when the Speaker, in one of the tactful speeches for which he is famous, suggested that mem- bers would be the better for an interval of reflection. The suggestion was acted upon, and the House adjourned, re-assembling on Monday, November 18, a week after the fateful division. The Government did not insist upon a rescission of the vote, but car- ried a resolution negativing the whole of the financial resolutions as amended, and adopting a new resolution. But more than a week of time had been lost. The year has not witnessed the passage of the Home Rule Bill even through the Commons. The Session is to continue into 1913, and tha measure is expected to reach the Lords on January 10.

i WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT. I Next in importance to the Home Rule Bill is the Bill "to terminate the Establishment of the Church of England in Wales and Monmouthshire and to make provision in respect of the temporalities thereof." It was introduced by Mr. McKenna, the Home Secretary, on April 23, and there was a majority of 78 for the first reading. On May 13 the second reading was moved, and carried on the 16th by 348 to 267, a Govern- ment majority of 81. A time-table and closure resolution have been adopted for this Bill also, and the Government have found it impossible to get it through the Commons during Iql2. Its final stages will be preceded with after Home Rule Bill has gone to the Lords, and it can scarcely reach the Upper House before the end of January. FRANCHISE REFORM.

The third measure of importance men- tioned in tho King's Speech, that for the Reform of the Franchise, was introduced on June 17, the second reading being carried on July 12. The Bill provides that an elec- tor shall not be registered for more than one constituency, that only residence and occupation, not property, shall qualify for a vote, and that the qualifying period of resi- dence or occupation shall be limited to six months. Instead of the present system of annual registration, there is to be a con- tinuous registration for both Parliamentary and local government purposes, and Revis- ing Barristers and Revision Courts are to be abolished, the duty of hearing objections being placed upon the County-court. Uni- versity constituencies are to disappear. Whether this Bill will be proceeded with in the New Year remains to be seen. TRADE UNION BILL.

A Bill providing that the funds of a trade union may be applied to political objects, if approved by a majority of members voting in secret ballot, has passed the second read- ing and been considered by a Committee of the whole House. The Bill provides that any member who gives notice of his unwilling- ness to contribute to the political fund shall not be compelled to do so, and it is laid down that contribution to the fund shall not be made a condition of membership of the union.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] STORY OF THE YEAR

A RECORD OF REMARKABLE EVENTS.

Both at home and abroad the course of the year 1912 has been marked by great and far-reaching events. It will be memorable for many things, but most of all for the War in the Balkans, a war which has cost enormously in blood and treasure, wrought remarkable change in the map of Europe, and seen the birth of a new Power—the Balkan League. One of the year's tragedies was the sinking of the White Star liner Titanic. Many mourners on both sides of the Atlantic will remember 1912 as a year af grief. Politically, it has been a Home Rule year, though other important measures have occupied the • attention of Parliament. Pwo disputes stand prominently out among the labour troubles of the year—the great coal strike, which resulted in Parliament enforcing the principle of the minimum wage in the coal-mining industry, and the strike of transport workers on the Thames. There has been much talk this year of generals and soldiers, and it witnessed the closing of the career of the greatest soldier of them all, General Booth, who, in the words of the official announcement made by the Army which he founded and had so long led, "lay down his sword" on August 20.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KING'S BUSY YEAR.

When the year opened the King and IQueen were still in India, the central figures in the pageantry and festivity which followed the historic Duibar at Delhi in IDecember. On January 9 their Majesties ' sailed from Bombay in the Medina for home. They had a prosperous voyage, and on February 4 their ship arrived at Spit. head. On the following day they travelled to London, and received a loyal and enthusi- astic welcome home. A solemn service of thanksgiving at St. Paul's Cathedral on February 6 was attended by their Majesties, whose progress through the streets of the capital called forth a remarkable demon- stration of loyalty and affection. Among those present in the Cathedral were Queen Alexandra, their Majesties' children, Prin- cess Victoria, and Prince Arthur of Con- naught. On the 14th of the same month the King, who was accompanied by the Queen, opened Parliament in state, and on the 28th their Majesties attended with the Princess Royal and her daughters as chief mourners at the interment at St. George's Chapel, Windsor, of the Duke of Fife, who died at Assouan on January 29, from illness consequent upon his sufferings at the wreck of the P. and 0. liner Delhi on Decem- ber 13. THE GIFT OF THE GEORGES.

An interesting event took place on March 130, when the gift of the Georges of the Empire, a badge of the Order of the Gaiter, was presented to his Majesty as a Corona- tion gift. Easter was spent at Sandring- ham, and early in May the King was at Weymouth with his Fleet. Unfortunately a fog caused the postponement of the exer- cises which had been arranged, but his Majesty witnessed successful flights by four naval airmen. On May 8, the King, accom- panied by Prince Albert, had a two mile trip in the Submarine D 4, during which the vessel was submerged. On the 10th his Majesty witnessed the big gun practice of the Fleet, and a week later was with the Army at Aldershot, where he saw field operations and flights by Army airmen, and presented colours to three regiments. On June 25 their Majesties travelled to Cardiff, where on the following day the King laid the foundation-stone of the Natio- nal Museum of Wales in Cathays Park. A visit to Merthyr Collieries and the Dowlais steel works was paid on the 27th, and next day the King and Queen left for Bristol, where the King Edward VII. Infirmary was opened by his Majesty. On July 8 their Majesties went on a visit to Earl Fitzwilliam at Wentworth Wode- house. They showed their warm inte- rest in their working-class subjects by taking the opportunity to make a tour of the West Riding industrial district, inspect- ing mines, mills, and factories. At Elsecar the King descended a coal pit, and on July 9 paid a visit to the scene of the disaster at the Cadeby Pit, near Sheffield. A number of State functions at Windsor and in Lon- don, and then on July 22 his Majesty was at Grimsby opening the great Immingham Dock of the Great Central Railway Com- pany. In September very important mili- tary manceuvres took place in the Eastern Counties. These lasted three days, and his Majesty spent two long days in the saddle with his generals and their staffs. THE PRINCE OF WALES.

It has been an interesting year in the career of the Prince of Wales, whose eigh- teenth birthday was celebrated at Windsor Castle on June 23. His Royal Highness has now therefore attained his majority, accord- ing to precedent where Royal personages are concerned. The Prince has not yet, how- ever, been called upon to play any prominent public part. lie is still being educated and trained for the great position he is destined to fulfil. It is believed to have been in accordance with the wish of his grand- father, King Edward, that his Royal High- ness spent some months of this year in France studying the language and institu- tions of the country. He went to Paris on April 1, as the guest of the Marquis de Breteuil, and during his stay in France wit- nessed the manoeuvres of the French Medi- terranean Fleet at Toulon, and had the ex- perience of going down in a submarine. At the Michaelmas term his Royal Highness went into residence at Magdalen College, Oxford, as an undergraduate. •

===== 0002601_19121228_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
IN PARLIAMENT.

It has been a Home Rule year. Othet measures have been debated, and some of them passed, while others are still to pass; but from the beginning of the year until its el6se Home Rule has been the dominating political question both in and out of Parlia- ment. All through January discussion raved around the determination of Mr. Winston Churchill to address a Home Rule demonstration in Belfast, the stronghold of Irish Unionism. The meeting was to be held in the historic Ulster Hall, and the Ulster Unionist Council declared its inten- tion to prevent its being held. Feeling ran high on both sides, and it became certain that if the promoters of the .meeting carried out their intention there would be grave dis- order. A fortnight before the date of the demonstration Mr. Churchill announced that he was prepared to recommend that the meeting should be held in another part of the city. In this way the matter was settled, and Mr. Churchill and Mr. John Redmond spoke at a demonstration in the Celtic Park football ground, in the Nationa- list district. TrIB BILL INTRODUCED.

Six days after the meeting, on February 14, the Session was opened by the King, whose Speech indicated the three chief measures-- Home Rule, the reform of the Franchise, and the Disestablishment of the Church in Wales. It was not until April 11 that the Home Rule Bill was introduced by Mr. Asquith. It provides that the Irish Parliament shall consist of the King, a Senate of forty members, and a House of Commons of. 164 members. It is to be sum- mimed to meet on the first Tuesday of Sep- tember, 1913, a date which will allow of the measure being passed in three consecutive Sessions by the House a Commons, in order that if the House of Lords reject it, the Bill may become law under the provisions of the %.3arliamcnt Act. The second reading was lased by Mr. Churchill on April 30, and arried on May 9, the Government having a iajority of 101. The committee stage began m June 11, after which little more was

===== 0002601_19121228_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
SUFFRAGETTE OUTRAGES.

Women have not yet obtained the Parlia- mentary vote. The Parliamentary Fran- chise (Women) Bill, known generally as the Conciliation Bill, was defeated on second reading in the House of Commons on March 28 by a majority of 14. The militant Suffra- gists have been very busy during the year. On March 1 an organised outrage was com- mitted in the West-End of London by some hundreds of women, who broke with ham- mers the plate-glass windows of many shops. Further outrages were committed on March 4. A largo number of arrests were made, and many of the, women were sentenced to hard labour. As the outcome of the out- rages, Mr. and Mrs. Pethick Lawrence, Mrs, Tuke, and Mrs. Pankhurst, leaders of the Women's Social and Political Union, were tried on a charge of conspiracy. Mrs. Tuke was discharged, but the other defendants ! were sentenced to nine months' imprison- ment, but were released after one month. Mr. Asquith was the object of the militants' ! attentions when he visited Dublin to address a Home Rule meeting in the Theatre Royal on July 19. A hatchet was thrown at his carriage and an attempt was made to burn down the theatre. For this two women were sentenced to five years' penal servitude, but have since been released. In November and December a number of pillar-box outrages were committed in London, paint end other liquids being poured into the boxes.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL INSURANCE ACT.

On July 15 the National Insurance Act came into operation, but it will not be in full working order until January 15, when the payment of benefits to insured persons will begin. At the time of writing the dis- pute between the Government and the doctors as to the terms under which the medical benefits shall be administered, is still unsettled in spite of much negotiation. The British Medical Association demanded a fee of Bs. 6d. a head, apart from drugs a.nd extras, or a total of 13s. for each insured person. The Government's original offer under the Bill was 6s. In May it was

announced that unless their terms were con- ceded practitioners holding club and other contract appointments would resign them and refuse to work the Act. So matters stood when on October 23 Mr. Lloyd George made another offer increasing the medical fees to Bs. 6d. a head, which would involve an additional expenditure of £1,650,000. The Chancellor of the Exchequer stated at the time that if the scheme failed, an orga- nised national medical service would have to be established. Negotiations are still going on. The number of persons insured under the Act is over 13,000,000.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO GREAT STRIKES.

Among many industrial disputes more or less serious which have occurred during the year, the national strike of miners demands first attention. The agitation for "a mini- mum wage had been carried over from the previous year, and in January the question was submitted to a ballot of the miners—to strike or not to strike. On January 18 the result of the voting was announced. For the strike 445,801 miners voted, and 115,721 against. The men were instructed to give notice to cease work at the end of February, while their representatives announced their willingness to continue negotiations with the employers. A conference between coal- owners and the miners' leaders proved abor- tive, and on February 20 the Government intervened. The parties were invited to meet Ministers in separate conferences. The invitation was accepted, and the conferences held, but on February 26 the strike began with the miners of Derbyshire ceasing work. 800,000 MINERS OUT.

On the last day of the month the stop- page of work became general, and 800,000 miners were out. Mr. Asquith announced that the Government had adopted the prin- ciple of the minimum wage, and would give effect to it "by every means in their power." rhough the strike was in being, the negotia- tions were continued, and at length it was found possible to get the owners and the representatives of the men together at a round-table conference. But this came to nothing, and agreement seemed as far off as ever. By this time there was acute distress in the colliery districts, while railways and other industries dependent upon the coal supply had to restrict their activities or to cease work altogether. The price of coal mounted to an alarming figure. THE MINIMUM WAGE BILL.

On March 19 Mr. Asquith introduced into the House of Commons a Bill designed to settle the strike. The measure instituted a minimum wage in the coalfields, the amount to be fixed by joint district boards, with an independent chairman. The miners de- manded that the Bill should fix the minima at ss. for men and 2s. for boys per day, but Mr. Asquith refused to agree, and although the Labour Party voted against the third reading, the Bill was passed and became law. It ended the strike, though in some districts the miners showed their dissatisfac- tion. On Monday, April 8, the majority of miners returned to work, and in a few days the miners were in full activity once more. STRIKE OF TRANSPORT WORKERS.

A. strike of Thames transport workers began in May, and soon assumed alarming proportions. The immediate cause appeared trivial enough, being nothing more than the employment by one company of a man who was not a member of the National Transport Workers' Federation. On May 21, 6,000 lightermen came out, and two days later a general strike of transport workers was declared. Recognising the seriousness of the situation, the Government promptly ordered an inquiry into the dispute, and Sir Edward Clarke, K.C., who conducted it, re- ported on five points in favour of the men, and on two in favour of the employers. His award was against the men so far as the immediate cause of the trouble was con- cerned. The employers declined to accept the award, and the Government endeavoured to arrange a conference between a com- mittee of the Cabinet, the employers, and the workers. The employers, however, re- fused to attend, although the House of Com- mons passed a resolution declaring that it was expedient that they Ehou 1 d meet the men's representatives. UNCONDITIONAL SURRENDER.

Lord Devonport, chairman of the Port of London Authority, communicating with the Government on behalf of the employers, de- clared that they would agree to no condi- tions until the men had returned to work, after which they were prepared to consider grievances. So the strike dragged its weary length along, causing considerable disloca- tion of trade, and involving the strikers and their families in deep distress. There were disorderly scenes, and some incidents of vio- lence at the docks, and on July 24 there was an unpleasant affair on Tower Hill, when Mr. Ben Tillett, one of the dockers' leaders, called on a meeting of strikers to join with him in a prayer for Lord Devonport's death. Three days later the strike came to an end, the men having failed entirely, and return- ing to work unconditionally.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OCEAN TRAGEDY.

The greatest tragedy in our maritime his- tory took place on the night of Sunday, April 14, when the White Star liner Titanic, the largest ship afloat, struck an iceberg off Cape Race while on her maiden voyage to New York, and sank. The Titanic had on board 2,206 persons, 1,308 passengers and a crew of 898. Of these only 705 were saved. Of the 815 passengers lost, 661 were men, 101 women (including 81 third-class passen- gers), and 53 children (all third-class). The best-known name among those who were drowned was that of Mr. W. T. Stead, the famous journalist and publicist. Mr. Thomas Andrews, junr., of Messrs. Harland and Wolff, the designer of the Titanic, went down with the vessel, as did also Captain Smith, the commander. Colonel John Jacob Astor, the well-known American millionaire, who was on his honeymoon, was drowned. WOMEN FIRST.

The Titanic struck the berg at about half- past ten o'clock. At first it was not believed that the ship was in any danger, as she was thought to be unsinkable, but on investiga- tion she was found to be filling rapidly, and the passengers were ordered to put on life. belts and to go on deck. Boats were got out and the women placed in them. From evi- dence given at the subsequent inquiry it appeared that many third-class women pas- sengers had been unable to reach the boat- deck. NOT ENOUGH BOATS. There were only sufficient boats to accom- modate. a third of those on board, and even these were not all filled. But they got safely away from the vessel, which sank two hours after the collision. Immediately the acci- dent °marred the Titanic wireless opera- tor had sent out into the night calls for assistance. These calls were picked up by several ships, but they were all too far away to render help, and when the Cunarder Carpathia arrived at four o'clock in the morning she found only the survivors in the boats. She took them on beard and headed for New York. FALSE NEWS AND TRUE. It was some time before the whole truth of the disaster was known in this country. The belief that the ship was unsinkable was so strong that a report that she was making for Halifax, and that all the .passengers had been saved, was unquestioningly accepted. When at last definite news was forthcoming the magnitude of the tragedy caused univer- sal consternation. An impressive memo- rial service Was held at St. Paul's Cathedral on April 19, the same day on which the Carpathia arrived at New York with the survivors. The American Senate at once ordered an inquiry, and as the Titanic was a ffritish ship with British officers and crew this caused some comment, while the manner in which the inquiry was conducted by Senator Smith was the subject of a good deal of criticism. It was not until the end'

of the month that the American authorities permitted Mr. Bruce Ismay, the chairman of the White Star Line, who was one of the survivors, and other witnesses, to leave for England. CAUSE OF THE DISASTER.

The British inquiry into the loss of the vessel was opened on May 3, and lasted thirty-six days. The report of Lord Mersey, who conducted the inquiry, was made on July 30. The Court found that the catas- trophe was due to the excessive speed of the ship, and, among other recommendativs, advised that vessels should be compelled to carry sufficient boats for all on board. Lord Mersey also ' expressed the opinion that tit: 3teamship Californian from which rockets were seen on the night of the tragedy, was within ten miles of the Titanic, and that if she had pushed her way through the ice, as she could have done, she might have saved many, if not all, of those on board. SUBMARINES SUNK.

Two submarine tragedies have occurree this year. On February 2, while engaged in flotilla exercise off the Isle of Wight, sub- marine A 3, with fourteen officers and men on board, while submerged collided !with the Hazard, and sank immediately with all on board. On March 4 the A 3 was raised and towed to Portsmouth The second disastei occurred off Dove: on October 4, when th( submarine B 2 collided with the linei Amerika. Two lieutenants , and fourteen Petty orficers and men were on board, and all wer.c drowned with the exception of on( lieutenant, who had a miraculous escape.

WAR IN THE BALKANS.

One of the most remarkable wars in his- tory broke out in the south-eastern corner of Europe in October, and was prosecuted with such vigour and skill that in a month, to quote Mr. Asquith's speech of November 0, changes more sudden, more startlingly and overwhelmingly complete were effected than even the campaign of Austerlitz pro- duced. Probably only the lack of agreement among the Balkan States prevented them declaring war upon their mutual enemy the Turk long ago, and when Bulgaria, Servia, Montenegro, and Greece came to an under- standing and an alliance it was recognised that they would not be long before uniting in the effort to redress their common griev- ances. Obtaining no satisfaction by diplo- matic means the countries of the Balkan League mobilised their armies with dra- matic suddenness on October 1, their object, it was understood, being to obtain the independence of Old Servia, Albania, and Macedonia. WAR DECLARED.

Montenegro anticipated the other States by declaring war on Turkey on October 8, the declaration arising out of a frontier "incident." Turkey declared war on Bul- garia and Servia on the 17th, but not upon Greece, who, however, at once threw in her lot with the Allies. Thus Turkey was beset on all sides, and was everywhere de- feated. Her army was ill-fed, badly led, and generally inefficient. The force which opposed the Servian advance was crushed and put to rout, and King Peter's army entered Uskub on October 26. The Serbs came into touch with the Greeks, and Monastir fell into their hands after two days of stubborn fighting. Many thousands of prisoners, with guns and stores, were captured. The Greeks were also victorious. They drove the Turks back upon Salonika, and took that important town on November 9. A BRILLIANT- CAMPAIGN.

Meantime the success of the Bulgarians had been of the most dazzling description. They had a genius for organiser and com- mander-in-chief. The campaign General Savoff had planned was carried through with uninterrupted success. On the day after the declaration of war the Bulgarians, advancing with great rapidity, captured Mustafa Pasha, and on October 24, General Ivanoff defeated the Turks with great slaughter at Kirk Kilisse. The greatest battle of the campaign was that of Lule Burgas, which lasted from October 29 to November 2. The Bulgarians, flushed with their previous victories, fought with extra- ordinary enthusiasm and courage, carrying position after position. A TRAGEDY OF WARFARE.

The Turks had at length to give way before the fierce onslaught of the Bul- garians, an the story of the retreat of the beaten, wounded, hungry men is one of the most terrible tragedies in the history of war- fare. The Turks are said to have lost 40,000 killed and wounded in this sanguinary battle, and the Bulgarians 15,000. The victors, having left a force investing Adrianople, pushed on to the Tehaldja lines, the last defences of Constantinople. To add to the miseries of the beaten Turks, cholera broke out in Constantinople and ,in the remnant of their army which defended the Tchaldja lines. On November 19, just over a month after war had broken out, hostili- ties were suspended for the discussion of terms of 'peace. The conditions of the Allies, however, were not acceptable to Turkey, and fighting was resumed, to be again suspended during the last week in November. On De- cember 3 the belligerents signed an armis- tice, and on the 16th delegates from the various States began the peace pourparlers .in London, at St. James's Palace.

MISCELLANEOUS MATTERS.

Some important changes in the policy of the Admiralty have been made during Vio twelve months, beginning with the creation of a War Staff for the Navy, announced by the First Lord of the Admiralty on January 8. The War Staff absorbs the Intelligence and Mobilisation Departments, superseding the War Council instituted in 1909. Early in December it was announced that increases of pay were to be given to the officers and seamen of the Navy. In the same week came the news of the proposed Canadian gift of three super-Dreadnoughts, and the appointment of Prince Louis of Battenberg as First Sea Lord of the/ Admiralty, in suc- cession to Sir Francis Bridgeman. AEROPLANZ FATALIiI2B.

The year's death roll among aviators has been heavy, and the number of fatalities in this country has been heavier than in any previous year. The following is the list— February 17, Mr. Douglas Gilmour, at Richmond; April 8, Mr. Hamer L. Allen, lost over the Irish Sea; May 13, Mr. R. V. B. Fisher and a passenger, Mr. Victor Mason, an American, at Brooklands ; July 5, Captain Loraine, of the Grenadier Guards, and Staff-Sergeant Wilson, at Salisbury Plain; Auguh 3, Mr. Lindsay Campbell, an Australian, at Brooklands ; August 13, Mr. R. C. Fcnwick, at Salisbury Plain • September 6, Captain Patrick Hamilton and Second-Lieutenant A. Wyness- Stuart, of the Royal Flying Corps, at Hitchin • September 10, Lieutenant C. A. Bettinglon and Second-Lieutenant E. H. Hotchkiss, of the Royal Flying Corps, near Oxford • September 21, Mr. H, J, D, Astley, at Belfast. THE "Fror-Psela " MURDEFC.

The most sensati-mal criminal trial of the year was that ot Frederick Henry Seddon, an insurance agent, and his wife, of Tolling. ton Park, Holloway, for the murder of Eliza Mary Barrow, a lady of sixty-nine, who died on September 14 of the previous year while lodging at their house. It was alleged that she had been poisoned by means of water in which arsenical fly-papers had been soaked. Seddon was arrested, and later his wife appeared in the dock with him charged with being an accessory before the fact. Their trial at the Old Bailey lasted ten days. Seddon was found guilty and Mrs. Seddon was acquitted. Seddon was executed on April 18. SOUTH POLE DISCOVERED.

The discovery of the South Pole really belongs to the events of 1911, but it was not until March of the present year that the news of Captain Amundsen's success was made known. The Norwegian explorer

readied the goal on December 16, stayed there for twenty-four hours making observa- tions, and reached his ship again on January 25. On April 1 news was received that on January 3 Captain Scott was still 156 miles from his objective, and that he would remain in the Antarctic for another winter. DISASTROUS FLOODS.

The summtr of 1912 was as remarkable u that of 1911, but of a very different cha- racter. Instead of the tropical heat of last year we had low temperatures and rain almost every day. Crops were in many cases almost ruined, and there was much distress in agricultural districts. In August heavy rains caused floods in many parts of the country. East Anglia suffered badly. On the 25th and 26th of the month there was an abnormal downpour in Norwich and the neighbourhood. In twenty-nine hcprs nearly 7i inches of rain fell. The lower parts of the city were flooded, houses were rendered uninhabitable, and thousands of people had to be accommodated in schools and public buildings. There was acute distress among the poorer sufferers. Enormous damage was done to the roads and bridges in the coun- try, and for some days Norwich was com- pletely cut off from railway and telegraphic communication with other parts of the king- dom.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Fire Dangers at Christmas Parties.—The United States he its National Association for Fire Protection, whose special cbject is to instruct every wife and mother how to protect home and family from fire, We have not got as far es household instruction in such matters, but the British Fire Prevention Committee has called attention to the special dangers of fire, attaching to Christmas parties, and festivities, in which a good deal of flimsy, and inlimmable material is used fur decorations or fancy dress purposes. In a leaflet it is pointed out that special care should be taken as regards the pcsition and lighting up of Christmas trees, and the of Chinese or paper lanterns. No celluloid articles should be allowed on Cbritmas trees, and unless it is properly treated, danger always attaches to the use of cotton we'd, either for dress or decorttive schemes. Attention is also cliled to the risk of playing "snap- dragon" and the advisability of first removing any personal articles made of celluoid, such as beads, bangles, or ornaments before indulging in it. It is more esptoially at children's parties, that rigid pre- cautions are advisable, and much niay be done by a little care and forethought to minimise the risk of accidents.

The Vegetarians.—Make believe (niers rather largely into the Christu.as dinner of the vegetarian, but it has one raving virtue, and does not invite that over indulgeuce so generally associated with the celebration of the festive season. Plum pudding and mince pies made without suet may be excellent, but "turkey of which the principal constituent is rcasted nuts, scarcely justifies its Lame. The thief indictment against vegetarb nisrn is that, in order to obtain a sufficiency of one necessary element, you have to take others in excess of what you require, and this, it is said,ls almost as bad for the 83 stem as excessive indulgence in mixed dietary. Nevertheless the vegetarians still maintain several vigorous societies, and one of them has been holding a Christi:lms exhibition in London, showirg a hat may be done in the way of producing food and clothing which does not involve death or pain of any creature. Mary of the humane furs and feathers," are wonderful Imitations, and excellent.vs eating, as are also i the hoots and shoes, n which thtre is not a scrap of leather.

The Brains f the Family.—There have been many protests against the tradition that it is the fool of the family who goes into the church, and it, is not surpris- ing that objection should be taken to the presentation, in rile of the characters in a London play, of a particulaily stupid and feeble type of the Anglican clergy. Ihe stags as a rule does not make heroes of the servants of the church, but many of the clergy have been among the great men of the past, and to no small extent, h story has been made by those who, like Nelson and Cecil lihtdes, began life in the country vicarage. A study of the " Dictionary of National Biography " tends to the conclusion that more often than not, it is the eldest son vlto goes into the Church. Whether the eldest son is likely to possees the lions share of the family brain., is a point on which the eugenists are not Agreed. It is a fact that most of the great men ot the world are younger Inns, but It would be strange if it were other. aisle, seeing that 'MBA there can be only one eldest there may he six or more younger ones in a family, and collectively their chances of superiority are the greater. The eldest son is in n any ways in a position of advantage. II i 3 training brothers have—the oppoitunity of sharpening their juvenile wits from the experience and greater wisdom of their elder playmate. Working Married Women.—lt is much more general for mrrried women on the Continent to maintain their own economic independence, than it is in this country. In Denmark it is the rule, rather than the exception for the married woman to contribute to the upkeep of the home through her own labours, and whatever may be said against such a system it undoubtedly helps forward the general prosperity. On the continent too, women as n rule earn a higher percentage of men's rates of pay than is the case in this country. Sir Alexander King, secretary of the Post Office, recently declared that it women Lad to be paid the ' same wages as men, none woeld be employed by the Post Office. ['hey married and left, and others had to be trained to MI their places. Moreover they could not do night , duty or stand the strain of overtime so well as men. One can understand that in many occupations women are Lot so valuable as men, but there are in this conntry, hundreds of thousands of women, who, for ' doing precisely the same kind of work, receive only from 50 to 70 per cent of what men receive. That is not the case in Denmark, where in many occupAtions women earn the highest rate of pay. They have also a way of making certain industries exclusively their own. Ile baking and confectionery trade in Denmark is entirely a woman's industry. It, used to be much the same in this country, tut in this ,particular trade, instead of women ousting men, the modern tendency has been reversed, and the baking and confectionery business is now almost entirely in the hands of mom Rings for Gloves.—Parisians weAr gloves only occasionally for evening functions this winter. Instead the fingers of Loth hands are laden with beautiful and oostly rings, and to set these off to the best advantage, the old-fashioned black velvet wristlet fastened with a carneo,cr other old-world ornament,has been revived. Not only dces the black velvet form an excellent back- ground for the jewels, but it makes the hand look whiter and smeller. Tue attempt to introduce hand- painted gloves failed, as did also the effort to revive white stitohery on black gloves. In white and brown,these find considerable favour among women who are much in the open-air. The Black Velvet Gown.—When the middle aged woman of to-day was in all the freshness of her first youth, a rich black silk dress was accorded pride of place in the wardrotes of her elders and of the young ' matron. The black velvet or velveteen gown not only occupies this position at present, but it has obtruded itself into the trousseaux of the greater number of this years society brides. As many as four black dresses figured in the cutfit of a youthfuf bride this winter. they were not all of velvet, but of tulle, charmeuse, ninon, or similar materials. White velvet Is also favoured, end in ivory-tints is a most desirable fabrio Drapery not Paniers. —The term panieis has met with scant welcome, and dressmakers prefer to call the Mimes on evening and afternoon dresses, drapery, which means the same thing except that there is no looping up to add to the size of the hips. Skirts of soft silk or satin have some kind of drapery, but in most instancis this is very slight for intricate draperies that look so simple require the manipulation of a practised master-hand, and are quite beyond the capabilities of the average dressmaker. Besides full draperies suit only the very slight woman, therefore the desired effect is generally obtained by allowing extra length to one or more gores end then looping them up by means of pleats or gathers. The Cuban Heel.—Fashion mongers borrow ideas from many periods and countries. The Cnban heel came to us from Andalusia, throngs' the island of Cubs,whose inhabitants had adapted it from Spain, and Spanish women are supposed to have tho prettiest feet in Europe. The fashionable walking heel this winter is a modified Cuban, and a decided improvement in the original importation. Military and Louis heels are no longer worn out of doors, the Cuban is more solid than these latter, yet it raises its wearer sufficiently to add slightly to their height. An arched foot is still desired and a comfortable support that strengthens the insteps has been designed for use, where Nature has proved unkind in this respect. Walking shoes 1. ro rade chiefly, of box calf, glace and patent leather. A new glace kid in soft shades of grey, is preferred to the suede BO universally worn during the last slimmer.

LYiiM.

- CHRISTMAS FOOTBALL.—The members of the Lymm A.F.C. organized a crstrity match on Christmas morning between teams representing "Sweeps v Bakers." After even, if exciting play the "Sweeps" won by the odd goal in five, 3-2. The receipts which amounted to £1 2s were given to Mr William Cooper who has been a cripple from acute rheumatism for many years. CHRISTMAS CANTATA.—At the Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening alter a brief service had been conducted by Mr H Blane of Warrington, the choir gave a good rendering of Maunder's Christmas Cantata "Bethlehem." The piece which is divided into three portions," The Shepherds' °lite," "The King's Gifts." "Our Gifts," is the story of the Nativity in music, and consists of solos, duet, hymns, carols, and choruses. The principal parts were sung by Madame Wilson (soprano,) Mr Tynan (tenor), Mr Edgar Water'. orth (baritone), and MA B Pryce (bass). Mr H S Brown provided the organ music. The, e was a good attendance. DEATH oF MRS. OTISTON,—The death occurred, at her residence, Higher lane, on Monday morning, of Mrs Ouston, widow of the late Rev John Holt Ouston, a former pastor of the Lymm Congregational Church. Mr e Ouster, whose health had been failing for some time, passed peacefully Keay in her sleep. Toe deceased lady, though of a very retiring disposition, was highly respected, and was connected with the local Congrega- tional body up to the time of her death. She leaves one daughter and one son ; the other son it will be remembered died two Christmasses ago while returning from India. The funeral took place on Thursday. DEATH OE MRS. DRUCE.—It was with painful surprise that the people of Lymm learued of the death of Mrs. Druce, wile of Mr. F. W. Druce, the well•known saddler, of Bridgewater-street, who passed away early on Sunday morning. About two years ago Mrs. Druce underwent an operation, welch was unfortunately not over successine with the result that she has not been very strong. Recently it was discovered that she was suffering from another inward growth, and tbat a , further operation was inevitable. She was taken to the ' Manchester Royal letirmary on Thursday morning of last week, but the disease was found to be in a too advanced stage and she passed away on Sunday. The body having been brought home, the funeral took place on Thursday. LYMM TONTINE SOCIETY.—The annual distribution of the tuods of the Lymm Tontine or Self-Help Society took place in the Primitive Methodist Schoolroom on Monday night. The phenomenal growth and success of the Society is shown by the fact that in the year 1889 there were 31 memb.rs only, but owing to the indefatigable efforts of the Secretary, Mr J Rogerson, who has been supported by an energetic committee, the Society now has 331 members. The total acuoust paid in during the past year rearthed the" high-water marls " with a total of .073 12s 7d.Out of this the sick and burial payments at Os. 2d. per member and other expenses have been paid, leaving a total for distribution on Monday nigtit of LSD 7s 7d. Hering the meeting Mr Rogerson in giving his report stated that they had not lost a single member through death during the past year but that there had been heavy sick calls on the fund of the Society. The advantages that the working man gained through being a member were also clearly shown. Mr Diamond, who was in the chair, made sympathetic allusion to the sudden bereavement that had befallen their treasurer, Mr F W Druce. A vote of thanks to the officers was passed, and it was decided to re-form the Society for the next twelve months,the past year's officers being re-elected. FREE CHURCH COUNCIL—CHRISTMAS SERVICE.— This year tne Lymm Free Church Council held their annual Christmas morning service in the Congregational Church when the use R W B 'Whiteway wee the preacher. Mr Whiteway cheee for his text the words to be fonnd in Luke ii He spoke of the beauty and charm that the story of the birth of Christ had for both old and young, but said that even this beauty was surpassed by the importance of the birth. This Advent was mote full of meaning than any other in the worlds history, its influence spreading all over the earth. He gave evidences of the influence that this birth in the manger had created, for example,philauthropic ages-des had been founded as a result of Christian influence ; the dating of the world's history was altered to the time of Christ's birth ; the recent history of such countries as Japan, China, and even England had been affected by it, and he mentioned the peace conference that is now sitting in London. The preacher said that the radiance of this birth must be eegraven in the lives of all, and that we must turn to Christ amidst the conflicts of opinion as to what is meant by Christianity. " Let us learn to seek this Christ,and our Christmas time will be truly prosperous, happy, and blest." Mr A Leather presided at the organ, and appropriate hymns and Bible readings formed part Of the service. ST. HELENS.

CHRISTMAS CELEBRATIONS AT ST. HELENS St. Helens was very quiet and orderly this Christmas. There were all the usual signs of good cheer and good trade, and the huge crowd of " Saints " that journeyed to Widnes came back in most, hilarious mood after tbe team had given an improved display and beaten the Widnes Rugby men. On Christmas Day morning col- lections for Christmas gifts for tbe poor were the rule at nearly all the places of worship. The largo body of inmates of Whistou Workhouse and Infirmaries were regaled with a splendid Christmas dinner, served under the direction of several of the Guardians. At the St Helens Hospital the wards had been prettily decorated by the matron (Miss Oates) and staff. The Christmas dinner of turkey, &c., was carved by Sir David Gamble (chairman of the hospital), Dr Jackson, and Dr Knowles and every one of the ninety patents was made as happy and comfortable as possible. In the evening the nurses gave au interesting entertainment. At the Providence Hospital, which was full of patients, there was tbe usual Christmas tree in the children's ward,and presents for all the patients, and a concert was held in the evening, arranged by several members of the committee.

WIDNES OFFICER'S RETIREMENT.

A cheque to the value of £lll was presented to ex-Superintendent R Strickland at a gathering of business men in the Town Hall, Widnes, to mark the completion of forty-one years' service in the Widnes police division. Superintendent Strickland had practically served the whole of the forty-one ysars inthe Widnes division, and had been promoted from the grade of constable to that of Superintendent. 'I he Mayor, who presicl,-,d, said there could be no gainsaying the fact that the recipient had shown the utmost ability and efficiency as a police officer, and the public generally regretted that he had to sever his connection with the force.

THEFT OF CHRISTMAS ORANGES.

Annie Regan, of Windmill-street, was charged at Runcorn Sessions on Monday with thefts of oranges and onions from a cart belonging to Kershaw Brothers, greengrocers. Lawrence B Cobb, a hawker in the employ of the firm said that on Saturday night be saw the prisoner put her band in the hampers on the cart twenty times. He told her she had taken enough. She became very abusive and threw the oranges at him. Mr Posnett, one of the magistrates, said that traders should avoid putting temptation before people, so far as they possibly could, at this season. Prisoner was fined ss. but went to gaol for seven days. YOUTHFUL TIMBER, STEALERS AT WIDNES. Two ten-year-old Widnes boys, named Peter Whittle and Joseph Basnett, charged with the theft of timber, admitted to the justices at the Widnes Police Court on Monday that they were guilty. Superintendent Foster said when the police arrested WLittle, Detective-Sergeant Davidson visited his borne in Gossage-street, and found timber stacked in the 3.ard similar to the timber missing. The parents admitted that their boys had been bringing timber home. The farmer, added the superintendent, bad suffered con- siderably from theft and damage to his fencing, so much so that a plain-clothes officer was ordered to watch at the spot. The damage amounted to £5. The parents of the boys were severely admonished, and a fiats of ss. in each nose was inflicted. The bench also warned the lads as to their future conduct.

NATIONAL RESERVISTS AT WIDNES. ENLISTING EFFORTS.

At the new Drill Hall, Peelhouse-lane, Widnes, of Monday evening, tte Mayor (Alderman F Neil) presented the badges to the members of the National tteserve, and also the Christmas shooting prizes to the local Territorials.

Captain J Caldwell said it was their earnest desire that a battalion of the National. Reserve should be formed in Widnes and coupled with the St Helens and Prescot Divisions, and that Colonel R NV H Thomas should have control. Widnes should provide easily 300 members. .

The Mayor said that whenever he could he would give the National Reserve movement his whole-hearted support. With regard to the Territorials, the tour years' service of many men had almost expired, and he hoped they would rejoin. There was a shortage of something like 50,000 he believed, and with a population of 40,C00,000 he thought there ought to be no difficulty in getting the number to bring the Territorial Army up to its required strength.

Colonel R W H Tii.omas saki Widnes went into camp 920 strong, about 200 more than any other bittalion this ye ir. As to the National Reserves, he %Nits very anxious that the three battalions formed in Widnes, St Helens and Presoot, should be at full strength on the ocolsion of the King's viat in July. THE DITTON JUNCTION DISASTER. OFFICIAL REPORT.

_ The rsport by Lieutenant Colonel Yorke, RR, on the accident wtich occurred on the 17th September to a passenger train at Ditton Junction, on tho London and North-Western Railway, has been issued. In this disaster fifteen persons, including the driver and fire- man, were killed and 39 were injured. The accident, which is described as the worst since the Shrewsbury disaster in 1907, was due to thewiesme cause—the trains becoming derailed by high speed at junctions or curves where low speed was necessary for safety. The differ- ence is that in the present case the signals were off. The derailment of the train, the Inspector says, was due to the misreading of the signals by the driver con- sequent upon his lack of familiarity with the road, and he considers that the engine was cot efficiently manned for work upon such a busy section of the railway a that between Chester and Liverpool. The driver oughs to have asked for a pilot, but the real cause of tht disaster is to be found in the lack of an adequate system of ascertaining and testing a driver's knowledge of the road. Colonel Yorke suggests that a responsible officer from headquarters should be appointed ae inspector to examthe and, if necessary, test drivers in this matter. Speaking at Wrexham on Sunday Mr J H Thoutaa, M.P., alluded to Colonel Yorke's report on the Ditton disaster, and suggested that the Government st,ould appoint a Commission or a small Departmental Com- mittee to consider the whole question carefully, and not hastily to adopt any principle thit might in the eat be an injustice to the locomotive men themselves as well as no safeguard or value to the travelling public. Now publicity had been giveo to the qnestion it was hound to be very seriously tackled. There must be uniformity on all systems, COMPANY'S APPRECIATION OF HELPERS' WORK. •

During the last few weeks a representative of the London and North-Western Railway Company from I in:le-street has been making inquiries in Ditton and Widnes for the names of those who rendered assistance to the injured in the Ditto', railway disaster on 6eptember 17th. Up to the present almost ICO residents of Ditton and those who hurried to the Feello from Widnes and the surrounding village of Ditton have received rewards of one guinea. The whole of the company's servants who worked assiduously saving life and assisting generally, have not been forgotten, and quite a large number have received lOs as a Christmas present. This gracious act on the part of the comrany is much appreciated not so much for the value of the reward as on account of the tact that their services, which were risky and even perilous at times, have been officially recognised. £2O CHEQUE AS CHRISTMAS CARD.

_ DIT lON RAILWAY VICTIM'S GRATITUDE.

Mr S Lloyd, of Foundry-lane, Hallbank, foreman of the North British Manure Company, Ditton, has received a cheque for £2O as a" little Coristroaa card" from Miss Wells, of 142, Liverpool-road, Birkdale. She also wrote him an appreciative letter, thanking him for his services to herself and a friend, Miss Brown, of

Liverpool, on the night of the ill-fated train smash at Ditton, on September 17th. Mr Lloyd, with Mr Davies (the recipient of £lOO from a thankful lady residing also in Southport), and other helpers, rendered yeoman assistance to many passengers in their perilous position when the train fired. In her letter of" a working womsn's gratitude" to Mr Lloyd, Miss Wells wrote that her friend and she would never forget the comfort he was to them. She had been very poorly for a long time, and it would take many months for her to be as well and strong as she was before the accident. She felt she could never repay him for all he had done. She inquired bow he and "all the rest of the brave helpers " were. They must have suffered after all their exertions, and ended the letter with every good wish for a happy Christmas and as glad New Year."
