WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAUCHURCH

Visit of the Rev. Professor Armitage

- - - The unfavourable weather on Sunday interfered with the attendance at Wycliffe Congregational Church,when the Rev Professor Armitage, M.A., of Bradford College, preaehed morning and evening. Tee annual Christmas choral service was held at night when selections from the " Messiah " were given by an augmented choir under the conductorship of Mr W B Pay ton, Mus. Bac. Addressing the children at the morning service, the preacher alluded to the first Christmas morning scene in the inn at Bethlehem, and in alluding to the celebra- tions of that important event in the orld's history, warned the children to take great care they did not play with religion, but to be sincere. " The day that changed the world " was the subject of the morning discourse and the text was taken from Zechahria ix. 9-16. He pointed out that there were people even in this Coristian laud who would not join in the Christmas festival. They did not take Jesus Christ seriously, rind they believed the world could leave Christ out and not really feel it. As a matter of fact the world was a better place to live in because of Christ's coming on earth. The preacher proceeded to demonstrate this by showing that Christ's influence lied changed the home life, dignified work, and changed all our thoughts of God. Dealing with the first, point, he said nothing had been more critical in the story of any nice of men than the question of what they had thought of woman's place in man's life. No question was so vital to Asia and Europe in Christ's time than that some force should come into the world to redeem the race by vitally, changing the thoughts that men had about women. Nothing could do it unless some holy passion could drive out unholy passion. This mighty redemption was brought about by Christ. When Christmas can3e round it was a reminder of what Christ had done for woman. To the mother, the husband and child would not only render the tribute of love, but honour and the deepest and tenderest respect. The mother would reign in the home, and round her would be gathered all deepest and holiest thoughts. Was not that a mighty redemption ? If Christ had stood for nothing else than that, the critic must acknowledge that he could not afford to leave Jesus Christ out of the world's history. Christ had changed the world by changing our homes. In alluding to his second point that Christ had made work honourable and dignified, he said it was hardly possible to realise the condition of work in Christ's time. Practically all the work was done by slaves, and work was regarded as abhorrent and disgraceful by the well-to-do class. By engaging in work at the carpenters bench, Christ dignified labour and lifted from human scc!ety one of the most blighting curses and led men to think of work as an honour, a blessing and a privilege.--In his con- cluding sentences the preacher contended that Christ had changed the world by changing the people's thoughts ot God and revealing the leather. During a brief interval in the choral service at night Professor Armitage referred to the great names which God had given to Christ—many of which were given expression to in Handel's " Messiah." Speaking of the name Emmanuel (God with us) he raid that name was given because Christ took our flesh and was tabernacled in the form of man. There were those who stumbled at this doctrine of incarnation, and said the dce.trine savoured of superstition, ignorance end darkness. He thought it was because we did not reverently appreci- ate our own manhood. We did not reverently under- stand what this tabernacle was in which our spirit was incarnated. After referring to the wonderful senses with which we were endowed, he said there was nothing sordid and unclean in the tebernacle in which our Lord had dwelt. The choir and all concerned are to be congratulated upon the highly effective manner in which the choral service was given. There was a consensus of opinion that never have the selections from " The Messiah " been heard to greater advantase at Wycd ire Church than on the present occasiornfor soloists,chorus and orchestra entered very whole-heartedly into their respective parts and the result was a finished and expressive perform- ance. Madame Sara Silcock sang the soprano solve in a manner which created a great impression, demonstrat- ing not only the well known beauty and flexibility of her vocal powers but a complete realisation of the devotional spirit of the work. Her rendering of "Rejoice greatly" WDS most artistic and she infused a depth of feeling , into' Come unto Him." Mrs E A Sanders sang in her usual expressive manner in "Behold a Virgin" and "0 Thou that telltst." Mr W Booth acquitted himself with credit in "Comfort ye" and "Every family" and Mr F Brown did justice to "The people that walked" and "Why do the nations." The choir brought out in admirable style the impressive grandeur of the various , choruses, the "Hallelujah " chorus being particularly fine. The orchestra which did its work in a painstaking I and effective mariner, consisted of the following : First violins, Mr A Bennett, Mr W Brown, Mr T Frain ; second violins, Mrs Havionr, Mr A Knowles, Mr H Bond; bass, Mr R Whitfield. Mr W H Payton, Mus. 139 c , as conductor, directed all the forces engaged with his usual skill and judgment, Red Mr T F Wallington efficiently presided at the organ.

THE POSTMAN'S TIP.

It was the day after Christmas, and the hard-working postman ploughed his way through snow and cold winds, a sack of un- usual Size on his back.. He ascended the spacious steps of a West End residence, and in answer to his ring a manservant in rich livery appeared. "Wait a moment, please," said the ser- vant, as he took the letters. "The mistress wishes to speak to you." The postman's eye brightened. It was the holiday season. Ile had 'done his duty with fidelity. Now, no doubt, in recognition of his regular and faithful— "I shall he glad," be said politely, await your mistress's pleasure." "Are you," she asked, on her appearance, "our regular ,postman?" "Yes, madam," -he answered, bowing. "Do you come in the morning?" "Yes; ,madam." • "And in the afternoon and evening?" Again he assented, smiling eagerly. . Then the lady said: "Well, was it you who broke our bell?"

TORIES' PLIGHT.

"WORST SITUATION SINCE 1906."

The desperate tangle in which the Conservative party become itivolveo is discussed with disconcerting candour by Mr J L Garvin in an article in the London " Observer," headed "The crisis for Unionism : Rally or Rupture." We make the following extracts The Unionist party will require the very coolest and most careful management dui ing the next couple of weeks unless the ill-otnened beginning of 1913 is to see the end of it for a generation or for always. The danger is not less than that. Pcmerful forces are in conflict. There are on both sides very resolute and resourceful men. The attempt of any single influence to impose its will—especially if that influence is of an essentially iriesponsible and non-political kind —will only challenge the gathering strength of crushing reply. Taken at a disadvantage for the momeut—i•tabbed, as they feel, suddenly in the back— Tariff Reformers, if the worst happened, would soon hold at least the balance of power in British politics. MR. BONAR LAW'S POSITION.

He continues: "The situation is not dissimilar from what it Was after the debacle of 1906." Of the leadership question be says We urge all Uni,mists to consider Mr, Bonar Law's position, to ask themselves whether any effective Unionist leadership, either in his hands or other hands, is henceforth to be possible ; to consider also the position of Mr. Borden, to realise that the supreme cause of the naval unity and the Empire is hanging in the balance. There is infinitely more at stake than any personal issue, any sectional issue, any party issue. Alluding to the reception of Mr. Bonar Law's Ashton- under-Lyne speech in which he made an admirable case for the industrial rtspEct of "Tariff Reform " but on its agricultural side "reduced it considerably," Mr. Garvin says:— The "Times," under some unhappy inspiration and with a lack of aptitude for practical politics such as it had never before shown in its whole history, came out in conformity to the aims and methods of the " Daily Mail," with which it is now allied. It reiudiated the food duties altogether ; practically requested the total surrender of their advocates. . . In a few days the mischief was done ; the conciliatory intention of Mr Bonar Law's speech at Ashton under-Lyne was ruined, and there was created a worse situation than has existed since the debacle of 1906. But, this being the situation, how is it to be mended? Mr Garvin then makes a call for "some decision which shall be plain, honest, and final—not ignoring any reasonable proposition with regard to the real changes in political conditions since Mr Chamberlain began, but not throwing over the Empire and not tainted by the unpardonable sins of mere oppor- tunism, faithlessness, cowardice, and surrender."

SIGNIFICANT ADMISSION.

Mr Garvin cf ntends that the counties have been bullt up since 1906 into a dominating position Once again, " on a basis of Tariff Reform, including duties on foreign foodstuffs as well as on foreign manufac- ture. In the last two elections the counties have furnished the unshaken foundation of Unionist strength. "The Labour party has changed the old position in the boroughs. Radical-Socialism has changed it. No possible gains in he boroughs, where we nevertheless have had every reason until now to expect considerable gains, could compensate us for heavy losses for the shires. That is the bedrock ttuth of the electoral situation. " L'oyd George's Land Campaign is approaching If we simply fling over the old policy—by which in the rural districts his Budget was triumphantly met—just when Mr Lloyd George is starting the new policy,then the Chancellor of the Exchequer might just happen to sweep the counties, and what was left of the Unionist party would look—well, it would lock rather FOOD TAX QUANDARY DISCUSSED IN PRIVATE. •

LANCASHIRE M P.'S TO FACE THE MUSIC. Lancashire is expected to give the lead to the Tory leaders in their food-taxing dilemma. The greatest indignation was pi ovoked in northern centres of industry by the jettisoning of the Referendum, and it was expected that this indignation, voiced by the " Liverpool Courier," would find definite expression at the annual meeting of the Lancashire Divison of the National Unionist Association held in the Con- stitntional Ciub, Manchester, on Saturday. Fearing a quarrel, however, the conference decided on the suggestion of the chairman, Lord Derby, to exclude the Press and discuss differences in private. In proposing this course, Lord Derby said: "We are going to discuss—do not let us mince it differences in our party. I want to emphasise that they are differences of method and not differences of principle, ard they are differences that can be well adjusted. There is no split whatever in our party, nor have any cf us any intention of creating one. We have ore object in view, and one object only, and that is to turn out the present Government. A gentleman in the body of the meeting rose at this point, and said : I want to ask whether you, as the head of this association, were consulted -- Lord Derby : I certainly shall not answer that in public. The Press representatives then left, at the request of the chairman. The official report 'issued at the close of the meeting was as follows : A discussion took place with reference to Lord Lansdowne's Albert Hall pronouncement. It was unanimously agreed that any apparent difference of opinion was merely one as to method rather than as to principle. Various suggestions were made as to how these differences might be overcome. The meeting adjourned until January 11. when it is hoped the Unionist members and candidates for Lancashire con- stituencies will be present. It is evident, therefore, that the differences were not composed, and that Tory M.P.'s are to be asked to "face the music," and help to find a way out of the wilderness.

FURTHER COLONIAL CRITICISM OF TORY :PROPOSAL.

NEW ZEALAND CONDEMNATION.

Further severe criticism of Mr Bonar Law's proposal to refer the question of food-taxing this country to the Colonies comes from the Colonial Press. Here are some significant New Zealand extracts quoted by the " Times" Wellington correspondent : The "Auckland Star," while commending Mr Bonar Law's bold.advomcy of Protection, conaiders that the opening given ,to Free Traders to accuse the Unionists of submitting England's fiscal late to the opinion of the Colonies shows a manifest error of judgment. The" Lyttelton Times" condemns the proposal as a bad move. The " Press " rays that to tax Great Britain according to the opinions of an advisory board representing other States would be a flagrant injustice and a gross breach of constitutional propriety. The " Otago Times " regards the proposal as highly mischievous.

The "Daily Mail," which is busily working up an agitation against food-taxes, quotes two well-known ' provincial Tory leaders: One is Colonel Henny Howard. of St. Asaph, a Unionist candidate, who writes : " I have fought three elections in Flintshire and one in Flint Boroughs for the Unionist Party, and am sure that if we propose any tax on the people's food we are certain to be defeated." The other is Mr Jonathan Formby. a former chairman of the Conservative Council for the Scotland Division of Liverpool, who says : " Without the Referendum the Unionists stand to lose the Liverpool Exchange and the Southport Divisions, the two seats recently won in Man— chester will be recaptured by the Radical Free Traders ; Wigan ithd the constituencies around Manchester, such as the Eccles Division, which are aching to come back to the Unionists, will return Labour or Radical cadidates, while the . Eastern Counties—Suffolk, Norfolk, and Lincoln- shire—will be as in 1906. TARIFF "REFORM'S" NEED OF FUNDS FOR

A COMMON-SENSE RULE

"EDUCATION."

Commenting on the fact that the oversee dominions are unanimously opposed to Mr Boyar Law's scheme of food taxes, the " Daily Chronicle" adds : Decidedly Mr Bonar Law's next speech will be inter- esting. Meanwhile the Duke of Westminster in the " Fortnightly review" appeals for vast funds neces- sary, he thinh a, for a campaign of education in favour of that great policy of Imperial Preference which is associated with the name of Mr Chamberlain. When not only the country remains unconverted but when, as the " Times" says, GO to 70 per cent of the Tory rarly in the House of Commons is against food taxes, the basis of preference, and when the Tory press too is deserting the cause, obviously vast funds are neces- sary ; but we shall be sorry for the education, seeing that the curriculum cannot be fixed. It would be interesting to know how vast the funds are which, ate subscribed, especially from Tory farmers.

CHRISTMAS TRAGEDIES.

There is an unusually and sadly long list of tragedies this Christmastide; violent death has overtaken people on land and from the air, in water and by fire, and also in the mine. One of the most terrible catastrophies occurred in Islington' London, where a father, mother, and two of their children were burned to death. In another case a big wall that collapsed after a fire at Dundee fell on a cottage, and tour children perished in the ruins. In addition to these there were deaths by shooting, by drowning, through an aviation mishap, land through a fall of roof in a coal mine, and also by ' accidental shooting on the part of a brother of the• victim.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
GIRL SHOT BY HER BROTHER.

Miss Louisa lichrer, aged 23, the only daughter of Mr Leopold Rohrer, jeweller and watch-m,ker, 14, Wellington-road, Todmordec, was accidentally shot dead by her youngest brother, George William Rohrer, aged 11, an apprentice to his father, on Christmas Eve. The boy caught her under the mistletoe and kissed her, and she ran to the other side of the room. The fun continued, and the boy picked up a toy pistol and pointed it at her, thinking it was unloaded. To his astonishment it went off, and when the other members of the family hurried into the room they found the girl on the floor, shot in the head. The bullet had entered behind the left ear and penetrated the brain and though two doctors were immediately summoned she died within half-an-hour. The police were informed, and the boy was taken before a magistrate He was formally charged with causing his sister's death, and remanded until Monday on bail of his own recognisance. for £lO and two sureties for £lO each.

MEDICAL TREATMENT OF SCHOOL

GHILDREN.

Slr,—Allow me to, thank Mr. Kingclon for his courteous reply to my letter in your issue of December 14th. I think it would be well if he would kindly give his information to other inquiring minds besides my own, in this matter of the legislative basis for the medical and dental treatment of school children by education authorities. The High Court of Scotland has decided that the Scottish education authorities have been acting ultra tires in providing medical and dental treatment for school children, and the questions—does Scottish and English law differ on this point ? and how ? naturally arise. I do not suppose that the Glasgow School Board thought they were exceeding their powers until an enterprising ratepayer tested these powers in a Court of Law.—Yours etc.,

SCRUTATOR.

Sir,—Now that a number of the leading men of the town, and clergy, irrespective of religion and politics, have oncouraged us by becoming actively associated with our movement by joining our club as honorary members, we wish this fact to obtain publicity through the medium of your paper. There are numbers of persons who have actually begun learning the universal language at home and many others whose interest has been thoroughly aroused, and it is these whom I wish to reach and appeal to. For due° years now the Warrington Esperanto Club has been established and possesses a " local habitation and a name." We meet every Wednesday evening at 8 p.m. at 27, Bewsey-street,and receive instruction from a certiticattol teacher. Esperanto is not merely a language but has for its aim the destruction of race animosities and the human- ising of mankind. There is a special fitness in my appealing for recruits at this season of peace and goodwill. I take this opportunity of urging all who are interested in Esperanto to join the club at the beginning of the New Year and I shall be very pleased to supply them with any information they require.— Yours &c.,

THO G. CATER°, Eon- Sec.

COMPULSORY SERVICE AND THE NONCONFORMIST CONSCIENCE. Sir,— I am not sure whether it is worth while reply- ing to " Nonconformist " as he goss a long way to , confirm what I stated in my letter of December 7th. When Colonel Seely reminded the recent Noncon- formist deputation that their churches were lacking in patriotism and were not supplying their share of the country's defenders he knew what he was talking about Is it not a disgrace to the Free Churches that the state of affairs. is such as to warrant the Minister of War in making such a statement ? If we were called upon to defend our country, " Nonconformist " and the whole ' of the section he represents would with their wives and families have to be defended by Churchmen. A much larger section of those who believe with more or less indifference, in the present voluntary system, but • will do practically nothing to make it a success—would be in much the same position. The Free Churchmen .% who have given teal encouragement and help to our defensive forces represent so small a section that we - may call it infinitesimal. Whereunto shall we liken the Nonconformists of this generation? They are like uwo children sitting io the markets and calling unto their fellows, and saying unto them : We have pipEd unto you and ye have not danced, we have mourned unto you and ye have not lamentEd," for they refuse to support the voluntary system, and as for National Service, that, they say, is of the devil. " Those who desire to see , a nation in arms are " says Nonconformist " nothing short of what the old divines called. children of the devil.' If these persons are really " children of the devil" and there be any truth in the doctrine of "heredity, then even the devil must have some good traits, because these, his children, desire to see their ',country strong and vigorous not for aggression, but that it may be ever ready to defend its glorious heritage and help it to continue as a centre towards which the nations may look for an example of strength , combined with justice and righteousness.—Yours truly,

G. DALE

ROMANISM V PROTESTANTISM

Sir,—" Phil Squod " is astonished at what he considers my attack on Protestants. He need not be. Canon had accused the Catholic Church cf adding to her doctrines, and in reply I pointed out his inconsistencey in repudiating Rom e's authority to pronounce on Anglican orders after an appeal thereto for that purpose. I alluded also to the violence and injustice pursued by Henry VIII and his co-operators at the Reformation. I also stated that the Church which was commenced at that time was fast failing, and that it could not be the True Church eat iblished by Christ as His Church mustiest until the end of time. He then belauds the liberty extended to all in this country, and questions that bestowed in others. With the controversial methods of other countriee we are not conversant, but must rest thankful with that little liberty given here. Surely Mr Editor your aim is not to promote any polit cal movement for what it is called but because of its being in conformity with your sem.° of justice; itnd to allow controversy on religious matters in your columns is to permit, if possible, of arriving at the truth. I write as a Catholic as I am confident I possess the truth, and would like everyone else also to possess it. I should hope this is Phil Squods' view. He is not very happy nor logical in his quotation of Macaulay. The general purport of the extract is an allegation that because Catholic nations are not com- mercially successful (and I don't agree with him) it is a sign that the Catholic Church is not the true one. When Christ came down on earth to redeem man and establish that authoritative institution which was to be the infallible guide to heaven, He did not promise His apostles and disciples that the sign cf being true followers of His was that they were to bask in the sunshine of material prosperity. No. Quite the reverse. lie said "If any man will come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross daily and follow me." Does a good servant desire to pursue any other course than that indicated by a loving master? Mortification and penance then mark the true followers of Christ if we ale. to follow His example and heed His word. The Jews fondly hoped that when the Messiah came He would restore to them their earthly grandeur, and when they realized that He had come to erect a spiritual and not an earthly kingdom, they crucified Him. To refer again to the extract from Macaulay : " Whoever passes in Ireland from a Catholic to a Protestant county, fancies he has passed from a lower to a higher civilization." Now just fancy that, Mr Editor, after the recent doings in Ulster? As I said at the beginning of the letter, his quotation of Macaulay is purposeless. May I quote him brii-fly. Replying to Gladstone on unity, Macaulay says: " Gladstone dwells much on the importance of unity in doctrine. 'Unity, he says, is essential to truth.' And this is most unquestionable. But when he goes on to tell us that this unity is characteristic of the Church of England, we are compelled to differ from him widely. The Apostolical succes-ion she may have or may not have; but unity she certainly has not and never has had." Thus Macaulay on unity. Yes, Mr Editor, it is unity which is so important and necessary a mark of the one true Church of Christ, and it is in the Catholic Church alone that that unity is found. She proclaims her doctrines with an infallible certainty, an undivided mind, and with a clear and clarion voice, and does so because she has no power to do otherwise. Canon Willis alleged that she has added to her doctrines. No Sir! Impossible. Neither the right nor the power for such was granted, for Christ admonished His Apostles to " teach all things as He commanded them,' and He added " For behold, lam with you all days, even to the consum- mation of the world." Yes, Christ is ever with His Church, guiding her and speaking through her, and so the Catholic Church isthe "voice of God upon earth."—Yours JOHN RICHARDSON. 186, Buttermarket-street, Warrington

MRS CROSFIELD AND PEACE,

THE BALKANS CONFLICT.

' Mrs. Crosfield responded to the toast of "The Ladies" at a banquet given in London on Saturday in honour of the Greek Minister. In the course of her speech she said :—lt is with feelings of special pleasure that I rise to thank the proposer of the toast for the charming eulogy of our sex which he has just pronounced Happily for us, and for him, he does not share the opinion of that well known Greek writer who said "There's no evil so terrible as woman." (Cheers and laughter.) But it is not only for having so eloquently sung our praises that I thank Mr Stavridis but more especially because we are all proud of the opportunity he has given us, that of expressing, or trying inadequately to express to our great leader Mr Venezilos--(loud cheers)—the warmth of feeling, the affection and the deep gratitude which stirs the hearts of us women to-day. (cheers). Although it may seem strange to some that should rise to speak in response to this toast, let me say that while we have heard many a poaan of praise sung this , evening, the feminine enthusiasm has not yet found expression and we feel it is only right on so memorable an occasion that we too should laY our modest tribute of admira- tion and gratitude at the feet of our leader. (Loud cheers.) For if the nation's interest in maintaining peace grows ever stronger it is we women after all who more and more vividly realise the

APPALLING AND UNSPEAKABLE AGONY OF WAR •

and who suffer most from its devastation. (Hear, hear.) It is we who above all others suffer when law and order cease, and we must always feel that the sacred cause of peace has paramount claims upon us. (Hear, hear.) But still there are limits; and never have women been found to urge or counten- ance peace when it is only to be had at the price of enduring dishonour, degradation and cruelty. (cheers.) Mr. Stavridis has spoken of the way in which lunder the , indefatigable leadership of Madame , Gennadius the Ladies' Community has ' worked to send food and clothing to those who , are fighting for our country, and he has alluded to the admirable and splendid example which our Queen and the Princesses Nicholas and Alice have indeed set us. (Loud cheers.) But after all what a joy to do all this and more for our beloved Greece. Is it not right and natural that we who come of a race of women who knew no fear, and feared ,no death, should stand side by side with the men ; to dare with them in war, and suffer with them in peace? For we cannot forget the suffering there has been. The brightness of these hours reminds us by the very force of the contrast of those dark hours and days and years when men might have given way to despair. But they did not despair. (Cheers) The spirit of our ancestry was there. It kept their faith and courage, in their dreams they still heard the clash of shields and the cry of freedom. And some may have repeated to themselves the never-to-be-forgotten words of one who felt, suffered and fought for Greece.

" Yet, frePdorn, yet, thy banner torn but flying Streams like a thunderstorm against the wind." (Cheers).

THE INSURANCE ACT.

Adequate Service Prepared.

The " Times " says :—We understand that Sir Robert Moran, the chairman of the Insurance Com- missioners, has just been in conference with the chair- men of a number of important insurance committees, and careful steps are being taken by the commis- sioners with a view to the provision of an adequate medical service should there be any difficulty in any district in the formation of the panels. Mr Lloyd George is to address a conference of chairmen and clerks of Insurance Committees to be held in London neat Thursday. The representatives of the committees will doubtless report RS to the position in the several districts, and it is assumed that the plans of the commissioners will then be made known to them.

On Christmas Eve the titreees, were thronged with people en shopping bent nr.d the business done by the tradesmen roust have appr, ached a record. One aßsays feels sorry for the much-liarra-sed postsl officials at Christmas time, for the sea.ou when other people are making merry spells only increased work for this brancil f the public service. In smite of earnest appeals to 'post early," the public persists in postponing the dispatch of its missives and gifts until the latest possible moment, On Christmas Eve the ecnnter at the Port Office w;,s literally besieged—in fact from seven o'clock until closing time there was one continuous retie= of purchasers of stamps. On Christmas morn ing the postman was seen staggering beneath heavy loads cf parcels and greeting cards, but in spite of his worries he had a cheery word for every one. The religious side of the festival found doe observance at the churches, many of which were beautifully decorated with flowers and evergreens. The Midnight Mass was offered at the Roman Catholic Church on Christmas Eve, while at the Anglian Churches there were numerous early morning celebrations of the Holy Communion. Every Effort was made in older that the joys of the season should be shared, by thi as fated to spend the time in public institutions and below we give some details of the festivities. Boxing Day was again a day of mixed but chiefly unpleasant weather and multitudes betook themselves to the varieus places of amusements which were crowded to overflowing.

NO TIOT-POT DISTRIBUTION. The state of trade is so satisfactory in Warrington that, after making inquirics from m.ny scurees usually in touch with distress at this period of the year, the Mayor decided that It was unnecessary to make the customary distribution of hot-pot, bread and mires pies this Christmosttdo. However, his Worship assisted those deserving cases which were reported to him by sending 'hem supplies of gioceries

'HE MAYOR PRESENTING GIFTS FROM THE CINDERELLA CLUB.

AT THE _COTTAGE _HOMES.

Christmas Day at the Packate Homes is a day long looked forward to by the children, and this year no efforts had been spated to give them a bright and happy time. All the Homes had been very tastefully decorated by the mothers, and looked extremely pretty. The large schoolroom, which was transformed for the day into a dining hall, had also a very inviting appear- ance. An exeellent repast of roast beef and plum pudding was provided and the huge. joints and monstre puddings rapidly disappeared under the united attack of the healthy apeetites of the youngsters. After dinner cach child had two crackers, and upon leaving the dining hall was presented with nuts, sweets, an apple and an orange. An entertain- moot had been arranged for the evening. Mrs Partridge, Miss Green, Mr Williams and the Matron sang. Some recitations were given by the girls, and the proceedings closed with a cantata performed by the children. The singing of the National Anthem I terminated a very happy day, during which all the children enjoyed themselves to the full. The visitors present during dinner included the Chairman of the Schools Committee (Mr W C Brooks), the Vice- Chairman (Mr R Lee), Mr William Houghton, Mr and Mrs R I Partridge, Dr and Mrs Peacocks, arid Messrs French and Ifilditch. During the afternoon the Chairman of the Board (Mr E L Bent') raid a welcome visit to the institution. --The crackers were the kind gift of Mrs Frost, and Mr Seton sent sweets.

The building contract has been carried out by Messrs \V and A Ashton, Bridge Foot. The ball was beauti- fully embellished with streamers, garlands and banging bAckets of flowers, while on the walls appeared a number cf mottoes and • scaonablo greetings. Con- spicious among the.° was the philosophic rt Election : "Since we cannot get what we like, let us like what we can get "—and on this occasion the inmates did like the provision made for them, judging by the avidity with which they attacked the good things. They were also t 1d: "It is better to fai lin doing right than to succeed in doing wrong," and "It is more blessed •to give than to receive." The complimentary greetings included " Success to the Guardians " and "Success to the master and matron." Promptly at twelve o'clock the inmates trooped into the ball, seated themselves at the long tables raid a small army of helpers brought in the steaming dishes. The Guardians had decided that on ibis occasion beer should be supplied only to certain classes and not to those known as " ins.and outs." However, those Guardians who attended the festivities did not approve of this distinction and, on their iustiuction,beef was provided for all who desired it. For teetotallers there was a supply of mineral waters. Among those present in the dining ball were the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Messrs E L Bent (chairman), G Mather and W Furness, members of the Board of Guardians, the Rev W Bracecamn (chaplain), Mrs and Miss Blacecarnp, Mr F Bracecamn-, Dr Megaw (resident medical officer), Mi•ri Furness, Mr Furness, jun., Mr F LI Aukland (relieving officer), Mrs and Miss Watkins. The whole of the arrange- ments were under the supervision of the, master and matron (Mr and Ms W J Brown) who worked untiringly for the success of the festivities. They had an active lieutenant in Mr 0 Moss (assistant master). At the close of the dinner, there was a little speech-making. Mr EL BENT moved a vote of thanks (which was accorded with cheers) to the Mayor and other notable visi tore. The MAYOR, in responding, commended the excellent arrangements made for the comfort of the inmates not only on Christmas Day but throughout the year. In wishing them all a happy Christmas, be satd that they ought to be happy if they could only bring their minds into a state of contentment. He said without fear of contradiction that their actual conditions, as regarded wealth, comfort and sanitary arrangements, were on the

e *=,

best possible lines, and if they were not happy and com- fortable it must be due to being discontented in their minds. He hoped that some of the inmates would have a prosperous New Year from the ordinary standpoint. One heard sometimes of inmates of an institution like that getting big windfalls of money. Of course, those were very rare, but apart from such possibilities, he hoped some of them who might have ambitions to leave the institution would see them realized. To those who were unable to leave he would say that they might be a great deal worse off. He hoped they would be happy and contented not only that day but as long as they remained in the institution. Everything was done to make them not only happy but healthy, aud to prolong their lives to the very utmost, and their thanks ought to be given to the Guardians who devoted so much energy and thought on their behalf. (Applause). Mr Bent, in acknowledging the compliment to the Board, said all the credit was not due to the Guardians They must remember the officials who were the back- bone of an Institution like that. Without their help, the Guardians would be practically useless. He (the Speaker) had bad a unique year of office, as the business over which he had been called upon to preside had included the appointment of Mr and Mrs Brown as master sud matron of the Workhouse and Mr and Mrs Lawrance es superintendent and matron of the Cottage Homes. Mr Bent concluded by extending to the inmates his hearty good wishes for the coming year. The MAYOR added a few words in praise of the officials who administered the Institution. He was generally a good judge of character and from his slight acquaintance tcith Mr and Mrs Brown, he should say they would make an excellent master and matron—two of the best officiale, probably, that could, be attae3ed to such an Institution. (Applause.) Cheers having been given for the Master and Matron, Mr BROWN, on behalf of the staff, acknowledged the kindly sentiments expressed towards them. They could be relied upon to do all they could, especially at that season of the 3 ear, to assist the inmates in spending a happy and enjoyable time. (Applause). During the afternoon all smokers (male and female) were given the opportunity of enjoying a whiff of the fragrant weed, and oranges and apples were also dis- tributed. For tea there was a liberal supply of fruit ke.

lathe evening the inmates again met in the dining ball when a most enjoyable concert was given by members of Mr Charles E Bradley's" Royal " Stock Company. The items included selections by the orchestra, songs by Miss Agnes M Bradley, Miss Connie Isca, Miss Violet Manley, Miss Rose Smith, and Mr G II Doyle, songs and dances by Miss Hattie Smith, comic songs by Mr Percy Braithwaith arta Mr Will Priestley and humorous chorus songs by Mr Dan Fraser, Scotch comedian. Mr H Val Vousden gave a monologue in his usual dramatic style, and "Buval," the happy Dutch- man, delighted the gathering with a comedy musical interlude. There were two admirable comedy sketches "Major McPhee, M.P.," by Messrs Doyle, Wright and Priestley and Miss Leah Corentzs, and "The Secret," by Messrs Val Vonsden, Braithwrith, Priestley and Warne and Misses Gidding and Dillimore. Mr C E Warne was the stage manager and Mr T West the musical director.

At the close the Master proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr Bradley and the "Royal" Stock Company for so kindly giving up their time to provide such an enjoyable programme. This was seconded by Mr Robert Lee (Guardian) and the applause given by those in attendance fully proved how much the entertainment was appreciated. Nlr Bradley responded on behalf of ttle Stock Company. OBSORD BARRACKS.

Christmas is eagerly anticipated by Tommy Atkins, who, if he is on leave, visits those who are dear to him. If, however, his Christmas has to be spent within the confines of the Barracks, he is not down hearted, for the 25th is a time for the usual revels, even in this usually dull building. Practically 75 per cent. of the men from Orford barracks are on leave from Christmas until New Year, but for th,se who remained there were the usual festivities and a sumptuous dinner, which, needless to say, was thoroughly enjoyed. The rooms had been prettily decorated, and on the walls were mottoes expressive of the good will of the men to the officers, their wives and families.

AT THE Posr OFFICE. For the Post Office staff Christmas is by fir the busiest time of the year but this Christmastide a record has been created in the amount of traffic dealt with. Never before has there been such a number of huge "bags " as this year, and it has been the one aim to please and gratify the public. The " rush " necessitated the employing of 70 extra men on the outdoor staff and even such a vast number were

barely adequate. The casual employees were chiffly those who were at liberty owing to works having been shut down, and we understand they have rendered very useful service. A stranger to the town on Thursday evening might have been excused if be had mistaken the Post Office in Springfield-street for a theatre, such was the large crowd assembled by the se terr box. It was like one long queue. How many of those who were posting Xmas cards thought of the hard work which was being il•coluplished behind the scenes? To cope with the increase in the sorting &pal tment, the police parade r )oin was utilized, and h,re again a large number of extra hands were employed. A number of motor cars a id horse vehicles were used to facilitate the to wn C allection.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

. "THE CHOCOLATE SOLDIER."

Warrington playgoers are this week afforded their first opportunity of hearing the much-talked-of comic opera '' The Chocolate Soldier," which has achieved triumphs wherever presented. The management of the Rylands-street house could not have secured a more welcome attraction for the Christmas holidays and the verdict of crowded houses has been one of delighted approval. "The Chocolate Soldier" is fully entitled to be styled an opera, for its music is a decided advance on that associated with the generality of modern musical plays. It is ear-haunting and, for the most part, light and sparkling, but some of the numbers make a powerful emotional appeal. This is particularly true of the fascinating waltz song " Come come, I love you only," which it is not too much to say has been the making of the opera. The story is not one of those inconsequential apologies for a plot which in most musical comedies merely serve the purpose of connecting links between a series of musical items. It is cleverly conceived and abounds in awkward situations and genuinely funny incidents. Nadina Popoff, the winsome heroine, is betrothed to Alexins Spindoff (colonel in the Bulgarian army) a braggart who never tires of posing as a hero and man of action and studiously seeks to impress Nadina with a sense of the high honour he is about to confer upon her by making her his bride. Nadina persuades herself that she loves Lim passionately for his soldierly qualities, and when the opera opens she is rhapsodis- ing before Alexius's portrait. But a change comes over her feelings with the advent of "the Chocolate ' Soldier "—one Lieutenant Burnerli, to wit. He is a a Servian spy and, being hotly pursued by a squad of Bulgarian soldL•rs, he takes refuge in Nadina's b udoir. Bumerli is the exact antethisis of Nadina's idealised hero, for he makes no pretensions to gallantry, confesses, to a weakness for chocolates—hence the contemptuous designation " The Chocolate Soldier " and generally conducts himself with a delightful originality and irresponsibility. Still he has a wheedling way with him, and not only does he persuade Nadine to conceal him from the Bulgarian search party and to lie fluently and bravely on his account, but he lays siege to her heart with a success that is upt altogether surprising. Of course, there are all sorts of amusing complications 'in which the blustering old Colonel Popoff, his frivolous wife and pert cousin are concerned, but the " Chocolate soldier " carries all before him by sheer irrepressible personality. The fact that the company is one of Ur George Dance's is in itself a guarantee of , the aclequateness of the production, and the action is is bright and busy throughout. Miss Alice Coverick achieves a brillfant success as Nadine, for she unites an engaging persoualty as an actress with vocal gifts of a high order. Her impassioned and artistic singing of " Come, I love you only, " is the ' gem of the opera. Bewitching and vivacious she carries through the part in quite a natural way and is repeatedly rewarded with rounds of applause. Mr Villiers Arnold portrays " The chocolate soldier "as to the manner born, and quickly wins the favour of the audience by his cool, unconcerned way of rising to emergencies. He is also an acceptable singer and one

===== 0002601_19121228_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
USINESS HOURS 9 to 8 DAILY,

of the most popular features is the letter duet in which Nadina and he are associated. Mr Harry Cross dis- tinguishes himself with a clever portrayal of the self- opinionated Alexius, and makes artistic use of a tenor voice of exceptional purity. Mr Charles Simms adds much to the gaiety of the piece as the choleric Casimir Popoff —his acting in this part being more genuinely amusing than snything seen on the Royal Court stage for a considerable time. Miss Flo Hilton, as the fussy Mrs Popoff, is to be credited with a remarkably fine characterisation, for she abandons herself so com- pletely to the spirit of the play that the actress is entirely subordinated to the role impersocated. Miss Maggie Gormon is a captivating Muscha and Mr Walter Williams is vastly entertaining as Massakroff, a typical comic opera captain. The chorus and orchestra do their work admirably, under the direction of Mr J yells Knight.

===== 0002601_19121228_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

A capital Christmas week programme has been sub mitted before large houses. The turns are all good but pride of place is taken by A D Ronius who is so admirably described al "the cycle tamer." In a style so unmistakably western, Robins gives us the most intricate and daring cycle tricks in quite an unconcerned manner and he is greeted with round upon round of sincere applause. A prize of £5 is offered to the person who can ride the bucking bicycle five consecutive tours of the stage and much laughter is occasioned by the funny antics of the competitors as they try to master this freak machine which wobbles and bucks most disconcertingly. Robins, however, mounts and shows how simple the trick is —when you know how. A wonderful exhibition of canine sagacity is provided by Duncan's Royal Scotch collies. On miniature harmoniums the dogs give us selections from various pieces in a very clever manner, speaking volumes for the marvellous way in which they have been trained. Four of the animals take part in a marvellous acena, ".A motor mishap." There is a smash, one of the dogs dies and is carried dl on a stretcher by other canines, whilst the antics of the wonderful dog chauffeur who " mends " the car are very funny and cause much laughter. The musical McGregors, two gentlemen arid a lady, are highly successful as entertainers on various inuEical instru- ments. Their wonderful manipalation of the Xylophone. is. very praiseworthy and the turn altogether is indeed a grand treat for all lovers of music. Florence Cooper is a clever vocalist using a flexible contralto voice to decided advantage in popular numbers. Frank Case scores a huge success with his unlimited comedy and vocal powers. Unlike the general run of comedians, Cass has a good voice and sings a tenor song in a pleasing way. Lytton an& DeSilva are operatic and ballad duettists of quality, their voices blending harmoniously in two well chosen songs. Described as the worlds' premier post horn bugle and cavalry trumpet soloist. Major Wiz is decidedly clever and an accomplishment worthy of all praise is the manipulation of two horns at the same time. The pictures are of the usual high order.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. The programme for next week will iuclnde : Max Erard's company of vaudevillians who will interpret a series of beautiful song scenas ; Nixon Grey, corn- median; St. Malar and Cootubes, vocal and musical comedy act; Frank Lagay Trio, Parisian Dancers; the Three Vims, real lightning jugglers; The Piquays entertainers; and the excellent Hippodrome cinema pictures.

ROOM Voit BRAINS.—Is there room in Canada for educated meo,cor does the Dominion hold out a welcome only to DIPLI e.ho sent to work on farms and to domestic servants ? This is a question frequently asked, and no better EIT/,117Pr could be give , than in the words of Sir Thomas Sh mehnesey, 0 , President of the Canadian Pacific Railway, who says There is room for everybody in Canada, especially for educated men. The young Englishman of birth strikes me as a hard-headed capable young fellow with a keen eye to the man chance. It is true that the Englishman'& ways are not our ways, and that be is lacking in the humility which is supposed to be prop,r in a stranger in a strange land. But, just because he believes in himself, the Englishman soon makes his way." "There is always room for brains in Canada, never mind where they come from. In general, lam of opinion that the Englishman of birth is every bit as valuable to Canada as the English farm labourer."

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the neat GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, tbe 30th day of December, 1912, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when tbe Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton, Chester Castle, Daresbury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Divisions, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 30th day of December, 1912, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COUIrT will be Adjourned to the COURT HOUSE. KNUTSFORD. on WEDNESDAY, the IsT DAY OF JANUARY, 1913, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Bucklow,Dukinfield, Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton,Northwich, Prestbury and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grard and Petty Juries for the linutsford District must be in attendiince at the Court House, firm tsford at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the Is day of January, 1913, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. hat, she rushed across to Edith's, and finding her just •getting up begged her pardon,and after Edith's willing fergivenrss told her the adventure she had been through. Sbortly afterwards Fanny's mother saw the two little girls coming arm-in-arm across the road and by their beaming hoes saw, and thanked God, that the "Prince of Peace and Joy" had indeed bean born in her little girl's heart, and nowhere in dear old Britain that Christmas-tide were found three happier children than Fanny, Edith, and Jack. DECEMBER. Oh ! Holly branch and mistletoe, And Christmas chimes where'er we go, And stockings pinned up in a r.tw ! 1 hese are thy gifts, December ! And if the year has made thee old, And silvered all thy locks of gold, Thy heart has never been a-cold, Or known a fading ember. The whole world is a Christmas tree, And stars its many candles be. Oh ! sing a carol joyfu'ly '1 lie year's great hest in keeping! r once, on a December night, An Angel held a candle bright. And led three Wise Men by its light To where a Child was sleei ing. --lIABRIET F. PLODGETT.

A number of dolls and toys were teserved for members of the Cripples' Guild and will be distributed at a party early in the New , Year. Before handing out the toys the Mayor made a short sreech in which he expressed the sincere and grateful thanks of the people of Warrington to the Cinderella Club. Those who were influential had very great power to contribute to the happiness of others and he did not know of any institution which had so much power fpr helping on the progress and good of the per pie as the Press. The you gsters then came forward to receive their presents and thtir delight and gratitude were unhcunded. Before dispersing they gave hearty cheers for the Cinderella Club and the Mayor and Mayoress. In the distribution the Mayer was ast-isted by the Town Clerk (Mr J Lyon Whittle) and members of his depattrnent. The Mayor supplemented the gifts with an orange and apple for each child.
